# LiteLLM → Claude Shepherd — feature-mining report

> Source app: `/Users/adam/Programming/check out/litellm` (LiteLLM — open-source AI gateway/proxy).  
> Target app: **Claude Shepherd** (this repo) — a local single-user macOS console for Claude Code sessions.  
> Method: 12 parallel feature finders → dedup → adversarial FOR/AGAINST judge → skeptic verify. 
> Counts: **236 raw features → 143 candidates → 46 ADAPT, 97 DON'T-ADD, 0 pure-ADD** (all 46 ADAPT survived skeptic verification).

**Why zero pure-ADD:** Shepherd makes no API calls, has no network server, no multi-user, no secrets store. 
Nothing in a proxy transfers verbatim — every real win is an *adaptation* onto Shepherd's own primitives 
(spawn presets, providers, the gate, the ledger, session groups, the routing dispatcher, token-usage tracking).

This file is one of several per-project mining reports; the intent is one consolidated 'big push' of new adds across projects.

---

## ✅ RECOMMEND ADDING (adapt) — 46 items

Tags: confidence (H/M/L) · effort (S/M/L). 'How' = where it plugs into Shepherd.

### Agents/Skills/MCP/Tools

#### Skills registry (managed SKILL.md packages: CRUD, versioning, ownership, provider-native Anthropic Skills API)  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The user explicitly named this as high future interest: "same with skills... we haven't leveraged those well enough." And the underlying object IS local-first: Claude Code already consumes Skills as filesystem packages at ~/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md (verified — deno-fresh2 and rune are installed there exactly that way, plus ~/.claude/plugins/marketplaces). So a "registry of reusable, named SKILL.md instruction bundles you can hand work to" is real and squarely in Shepherd's wheelhouse: it already manages everything that surrounds a spawn. Shepherd already has the perfect attach point — cc-presets.json saves a one-click spawn profile (name/folder/editor/permMode/provider via core.presetPush, PRESET_FILE), and spawnSpec/spawnFlags build the claude launch line. Letting a preset declare which on-disk skills/plugins should be active for a session (and a small panel to browse/enable/inspect the SKILL.md packages already in ~/.claude/skills) directly serves the stated "saved agents / skills / MCP" goal and turns Shepherd into the local skills-management surface the user wants. display_title/description/instructions map cleanly to a local card view.
- **Against:** As specified, this feature is multi-tenant HTTP-proxy machinery that violates every Shepherd hard-scope limit. It is a Prisma DB table (LiteLLM_SkillsTable) with file_content Bytes zip storage, owner/visibility scoping resolved from an auth object (get_primary_resource_owner_scope), version objects (SkillVersion), and a provider switch that either CRUDs the DB or proxies Anthropic's native /v1/skills HTTP endpoints. Shepherd has no DB (it reads JSON off the filesystem), no network server, no multi-user/owner concept, and makes zero API calls — it is the GUI in front of local Claude Code sessions, not the thing that serves skills. None of the registry plumbing (CRUD-as-a-service, versioning, ownership, zip-in-bytes, the Anthropic Skills API proxy) belongs locally; Claude Code itself already loads skills from disk, so a managed registry-with-versioning is redundant on top of "drop a folder in ~/.claude/skills." Build it as-is and you ship a tiny LiteLLM clone that nothing in Shepherd's architecture uses.
- **Ruling:** The NEED is real and explicitly user-requested, but the LiteLLM shape (DB CRUD, versioning, ownership scopes, zip Bytes, Anthropic /v1/skills proxy) is the multi-tenant-server version and must be discarded — that's textbook ADD-rejection scope creep for a local console. What survives is the local-first core: Skills are already filesystem packages Claude Code reads from ~/.claude/skills, and Shepherd already owns the spawn/preset surface that decides how a session starts. So the verdict is ADAPT: a read-mostly LOCAL skills view (enumerate ~/.claude/skills/*/SKILL.md, parse display_title/description for a card) plus extending the existing spawn-preset to optionally pin which skills/plugins a spawned session uses. No DB, no API, no versioning, no ownership — those are the parts that don't fit. Not ADD (would import proxy plumbing Shepherd can't host); not DONT_ADD (the user named it, and it cleanly extends presets + spawn). High confidence because the filesystem skill layout and the preset primitive are both verified in-repo and on-disk.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend the existing spawn-preset primitive: cc-presets.json + core.presetPush/presetList/presetForProject (cc-core.lua ~3640-3720) and FX.readPresets/writePresets (claude-dashboard.lua ~738-746) gain an optional skills/plugins field on a preset, threaded through M.spawnSpec/M.spawnFlags (cc-core.lua ~3001/3061) into the claude launch line. Add a pure cc-core enumerator that lists ~/.claude/skills/*/SKILL.md and parses front-matter (display_title/description) for a read-mostly skills card view in the New-session modal (the #n-presets / spawn UI in claude-dashboard.lua ~3244+), with an FX.listSkills file-read effect. Drop entirely: the Prisma LiteLLM_SkillsTable, file_content Bytes/zip storage, SkillVersion/versioning, owner-scope/visibility, and the Anthropic /v1/skills proxy — none have a local single-user home. Authoring/CRUD of a SKILL.md stays a "drop a folder in ~/.claude/skills" affordance, not a DB.
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; the ADAPT ruling survives. Both common failure modes were checked against the actual repo and disk. (a) Proxy-dressed-as-local? The FEATURE as written IS the multi-tenant-server version — LiteLLM_SkillsTable DB CRUD, file_content Bytes/zip blobs, SkillVersion versioning, owner/visibility scoping, and the Anthropic /v1/skills proxy. None of that fits Shepherd's hard scope (NO server, NO DB, NO multi-user, never proxies API calls), and the judge already discarded all of it. But what survives is NOT an empty shell: Skills are genuinely local filesystem packages — verified on disk at ~/.claude/skills/<name>/SKILL.md with the exact front-matter the FEATURE names (name/display_title + description, e.g. /Users/adam/.claude/skills/rune/SKILL.md). And the local mechanism the adaptation relies on is real, not speculative: `claude --help` (binary present at /Users/adam/.local/bin/claude, contradicting the stale memory note) exposes --agent/--agents, --plugin-dir <path|.zip>, and skill resolution from ~/.claude/skills (--disable-slash-commands disables skills). So the adapted core is a genuinely local single-user concern. (b) Already present? No. Grep finds ZERO 'skill' references in cc-core.lua, claude-dashboard.lua, context.md, or README (the only LiteLLM mentions are the unrelated gateway-provider feature). The spawn preset exists (cc-core.lua ~3640-3722; FX.readPresets/writePresets at claude-dashboard.lua 738-744; round-tripped through the #n-presets New-session modal at 3245/4005) but pins only {folder, editor, permMode, provider} — it does NOT pin skills/plugins/agents. So this is a true extension, not a re-add (keeps it off DONT_ADD). The adapted shape — extend the preset to optionally thread a skill/plugin/agent selection into the existing flat flags list in spawnFlags/spawnInner (cc-core.lua 3001/2970) as --agent/--plugin-dir, plus a read-mostly FX.listSkills enumerator over ~/.claude/skills/*/SKILL.md for a card view (FX.readDir/readFile/listDirs already exist at 269-696, so no new infra) — is exactly the ADAPT envelope. It sits where the skepticism would push it: not ADD (importing the DB/API/versioning plumbing Shepherd can't host), not DONT_ADD (user explicitly named skills as high-interest, and it cleanly extends presets + spawn). The heavy nouns (versioning, ownership, DB, zip Bytes, /v1/skills proxy) must stay dropped — but the ruling already drops them. Verdict unchanged: ADAPT.

#### MCP server registry (multi-transport stdio/SSE/HTTP) + namespacing + display/description overrides  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The user explicitly named MCPs as a top future interest ("same with skills and MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough"), so the NEED is real and high-priority. And the registry shape maps almost 1:1 onto a primitive Shepherd already ships: the `providers` array is a stored list of named, non-secret, reference-at-spawn bundles, and spawn presets (cc-presets.json: {name, folder, editor, permMode, provider}) already let an operator save a reusable launch setup. A named-MCP-server list is the same pattern. Crucially, Claude Code's CLI already accepts MCP config at launch (--mcp-config <file> / --strict-mcp-config, plus .mcp.json), and Shepherd already builds launch flags in core.spawnFlags(mode, effort, rc) — so attaching a chosen MCP set to a spawn is a small, in-grain extension. allowed_tools also folds naturally into Shepherd's existing gate.tools allow-list rather than needing new machinery. This makes "spawn a session from a saved agent/MCP profile" concrete and local.
- **Against:** The LiteLLM feature as specified is mostly multi-tenant-proxy plumbing that Shepherd structurally cannot and should not own. The gateway opens a live MCP ClientSession per server, namespaces tools server_name/tool_name on list and strips the prefix on call to route — but Shepherd does not proxy and never holds MCP connections; Claude Code (the editor) owns its own MCP clients, so there is no central tool list to namespace, rename (tool_name_to_display_name / tool_name_to_description), or route. mcp_access_groups is multi-user RBAC (no multi-user in Shepherd). The DB/Prisma backend, and especially per-server stored auth, collide with hard scope limits: NO secrets storage — stdio MCP servers routinely carry API keys in env, exactly what Shepherd refuses to persist. Taken literally, almost every distinctive piece of this feature (sessions, namespacing, overrides, access groups, DB, auth) is out of scope.
- **Ruling:** Split the feature into "the proxy mechanism" vs "the registry need." The mechanism (live ClientSessions, namespacing/strip-on-call routing, display/description override maps, access groups, DB, stored auth) is proxy-only and violates Shepherd's no-proxy/no-secrets/single-user limits — drop all of it. But the need — operators registering named, reusable MCP servers and choosing which a session launches with — is real, user-requested, and fits Shepherd's existing providers + spawn-preset + spawnFlags seams cleanly. Therefore ADAPT, not DONT_ADD (the need is genuine and reuses existing primitives) and not ADD (it must be heavily reshaped, keeping only ~a quarter of the spec). Concretely: a cc-mcp.json list of named records (transport stdio/sse/http, command/args, url, allowed_tools), surfaced in the New Session modal and attachable to a preset; at spawn Shepherd emits an mcp-config file and appends --mcp-config / --strict-mcp-config via spawnFlags; allowed_tools feeds the existing gate.tools allow-list. Secret discipline is mandatory and already solved: store the env-var NAME and let the spawned login shell expand it (the exact providers/authTokenEnv pattern), never the value. New pure logic lands in cc-core (validation + flag/file emission), keeping the cc-core purity invariant. Off by default / opt-in like every other Shepherd registry.
- **How (Shepherd):** Model after the existing `providers` registry: add a `mcpServers` config block / separate cc-mcp.json (like cc-presets.json / cc-templates.json — operator data) of records {id, label, transport(stdio|sse|http), command, args, url, allowedTools, authTokenEnv}. Pure logic in cc-core.lua: a validator + an mcp-config-file builder + extend M.spawnFlags(...) (cc-core.lua:3001) to append --mcp-config <path> and optional --strict-mcp-config; wire allowedTools into core.resolveGateTools/gate.tools (cc-core.lua:2537). Add an MCP picker to the New Session modal in claude-dashboard.lua and an mcp field on spawn presets (M.presetPush/presetList, cc-core.lua:3658-3694; PRESET_FILE in claude-dashboard.lua:65). Secrets: follow providerEnv (cc-core.lua:2710) — store env-var NAME only, expand in the login-shell wrap (M.loginShellWrap). DROP: live ClientSessions, namespacing/strip-on-call, display/description override maps, mcp_access_groups, DB backend, stored auth values.
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; ADAPT survives. Both common errors checked and neither applies. (a) Not a smuggled proxy: the judge explicitly DROPS the entire proxy mechanism (live ClientSessions, server_name/tool_name namespacing + strip-on-call routing, display/description override maps, mcp_access_groups, DB backend, stored auth values). What remains is purely local — Shepherd never connects to any MCP server; the spawned Claude Code session does, exactly as if the user hand-wrote a .mcp.json. Shepherd is only a launcher/registry, so no network server / no multi-user / no secrets storage is violated. (b) Not already-have-it: grep for "mcp" across all .lua/.js/.md returned ZERO hits — Shepherd has no MCP support today. Verified the mechanism is real and local: `claude --help` on this Mac (claude IS present at /Users/adam/.local/bin/claude, contradicting the stale 'no claude CLI' note) shows real `--mcp-config <configs...>` and `--strict-mcp-config` flags. The seams the judge maps onto all exist and fit: M.spawnFlags (cc-core.lua:3001) returns a flat argv list and is the single chokepoint for every spawn path (spawnSpec at 3061 threads it through kitty/vscode/terminal/ssh); FX.spawnSession->core.spawnSpec->spawnFlags is the live pipeline (claude-dashboard.lua:1523). The providers registry (cc-core.lua:2618-2740) is the exact precedent: a list of named non-secret records with authTokenEnv storing the env-var NAME only, expanded by the login shell via loginShellWrap (2754) — the secret discipline is already solved and reusable verbatim. Presets already carry {name,folder,editor,permMode,provider} (presetPush/presetList 3658-3694; PRESET_FILE claude-dashboard.lua:65) so adding an mcp field is a clean extension. resolveGateTools (2537) already has a reserved providerDefault layer that allowed_tools can feed. The reshape keeps only ~a quarter of the spec (the named-record registry + per-session/preset selection + flag/file emission), which is why it's ADAPT not ADD; and the underlying need is genuine, user-requested ('skills and MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough'), and reuses existing primitives, which is why it's not DONT_ADD. Only caveat (does not change the verdict): native Claude Code already reads project .mcp.json and `claude mcp add` keeps a registry, so the adapted feature should stay a thin fleet-level convenience (named reusable sets, per-session pick, gate wiring) and must NOT reimplement MCP — pure flag/file emission only, off by default like every other Shepherd registry.

#### Public MCP registry / discovery + BYOM server submission & approval workflow  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The user explicitly named MCPs as a high-interest gap ("MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough") alongside saved agents and a skills registry. The genuinely useful kernel inside this LiteLLM feature is the "registry" itself: a curated, reusable catalog of MCP server definitions you can browse and attach to a session — exactly the saved-agent/skills-registry direction the user wants. Shepherd already proves the local-registry pattern works: cc-presets.json is a named, capped, validated, save/delete/use catalog of spawn configs (folder/editor/permMode/provider), and the spawn modal already renders presets as one-click chips. An MCP catalog (cc-mcp.json) attached to the spawn flow — pick servers to enable for a new session, which Claude Code consumes via project .mcp.json / --mcp-config — slots straight into that existing modal and would meaningfully improve the local console. Even the approval/gating notion has a faint local echo: Shepherd already gates tools per session, so "which MCP servers is this session allowed" is conceptually adjacent to gate.tools.
- **Against:** The feature as specified is a multi-tenant, network-published governance pipeline, and almost every load-bearing element collides with a hard scope limit. The submission → admin review → approve/reject → make-public lifecycle (submitted_by/submitted_at/reviewed_at/review_notes, approval_status pending/active) only has meaning when one set of users submits and a different admin role curates — Shepherd is single-user, so the submitter IS the approver and the whole workflow degenerates to "I added a thing I want." The /discover and /registry.json endpoints are HTTP server surfaces; Shepherd has NO network server. available_on_public_internet and source_url are explicitly about exposing servers on the public internet — the direct antithesis of "NO network, NO multi-user." Copying this as-is would import a proxy's team-governance machinery into a local GUI that has no team, no network listener, and no second user to govern.
- **Ruling:** Split the feature into its two layers. The multi-tenant layer — submission rows, admin approve/reject, make-public, /discover, /registry.json, available_on_public_internet — is pure proxy/team plumbing that violates Shepherd's NO-network / NO-multi-user / single-user limits, so it is dropped wholesale (no admin to approve, no network to publish to). The remaining kernel — a curated local catalog of reusable MCP server definitions you can browse and attach when spawning — is real, fits the local Claude-Code-session model, and directly serves the user's stated MCP/skills/saved-agent interest. Because that kernel is an extension of an existing primitive (the spawn-preset registry + the spawn modal) rather than a clean new fit, the verdict is ADAPT, not ADD, and certainly not DONT_ADD. The reshape: a local cc-mcp.json catalog (name + command/args/env-var-refs, never secrets — same $ENV-resolution rule as providers), surfaced as selectable chips in the New Session modal, materialized into the session's MCP config at spawn time; the per-session "approval_status" collapses into the existing enable/disable + gate.tools allow-list rather than a cross-user review queue.
- **How (Shepherd):** Reuse the spawn-preset pattern: a new cc-mcp.json catalog with pure cc-core CRUD mirroring presetList/presetPush/presetRemove (name + command/args + env-var REFERENCES only — apply the providerEnv $ENV rule, never store secrets). Surface it in the New Session modal in claude-dashboard.lua next to the existing Presets chip row; at spawn, materialize selected servers into the session's MCP config (project .mcp.json or --mcp-config) via the FX file-write/spawn path. Fold the per-session "which servers are active" into the existing gate.tools / enable-list rather than an approval queue. Drop entirely: register/get-submissions/approve/reject/make-public endpoints, /discover, /registry.json, available_on_public_internet/source_url, MCPApprovalStatus — all multi-tenant/network surface.
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute ADAPT on both stock failure modes; neither downgrade survives, so ADAPT holds. (a) Proxy-dressed-as-local? The feature AS WRITTEN is overwhelmingly multi-tenant proxy plumbing: registry.json published at /discover, submission rows, an ADMIN who reviews/approves/rejects, approval_status active/pending, reviewed_at/review_notes, "made public," available_on_public_internet. Every one of these violates Shepherd's HARD limits (NO network server, NO multi-user). The judge already dropped this entire layer wholesale — correct: no admin, no second user, no network to publish to. So the verdict can't rest on the proxy layer, and it doesn't. (b) Already present? No. Verified zero MCP references in the entire repo — grep -i "mcp" across cc-core.lua, claude-dashboard.lua (324K lines of glue), all .sh hooks, all .json, README, context.md, todos.md returns NOTHING. Shepherd genuinely lacks an MCP catalog. This rules out DONT_ADD "already have it." Why ADAPT not ADD: the surviving kernel is an EXTENSION of an existing primitive, not a clean new fit. The spawn-preset registry already exists (presetList/presetPush/presetRemove, cc-core.lua:3658-3704; separate cc-presets.json operator data), and the never-store-secrets $ENV-reference rule already exists (providerEnv at :2714 emits {name,value,secret} with secrets as "$VAR" shell refs; envPrefix at :2738). A local cc-mcp.json catalog (name + command/args + env-var REFERENCES only) mirrors presetPush/presetRemove exactly and reuses the $ENV rule verbatim. The spawn path already builds an argv flags list (spawnFlags :3001, spawnSpec :3061), so materializing selected servers via --mcp-config / a written .mcp.json fits the existing FX file-write/spawn route; the per-session "active servers" folds into the existing gate.tools allow-list rather than an approval queue. Why DONT_ADD is wrong: the reshaped kernel genuinely improves the LOCAL single-user console — it directly serves the user's stated high-interest goal of better-leveraging saved/reusable MCPs in the spawn-a-session model, and it's absent today. The fit is real once the network/multi-user surface (register/get-submissions/approve/reject/make-public, /discover, /registry.json, available_on_public_internet, MCPApprovalStatus) is dropped entirely. Verdict survives scrutiny: ADAPT (high).

#### Provider-native Agents CRUD API (Gemini-style saved agent objects)  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The literal mechanism is the wrong layer, but the concept it encodes — agents as first-class, named, saved objects you can create/list/delete (with version history) — is exactly the user's stated top future interest: "pre-configured agents we can hand work off to, saved into a directory; same with skills and MCPs." Shepherd already proves this fits a local single-user model: cc-presets.json is a named, validated, capped CRUD store (presetList / presetPush / presetRemove) of {folder, editor, permMode, provider} spawn bundles, and providers is a parallel CRUD-ish list (providerById). A "saved agent" is just a richer preset — bundle a name + folder + provider/model + permission mode + a seed prompt/system task + a gate.tools allow-list + (future) attached skills/MCP servers, then spawn-from-agent. The "agent versions as first-class objects" idea maps cleanly onto something Shepherd already tracks adjacently: session lineage and the relabel/respawnSpec history — versioning a saved agent profile is a natural, local, file-backed extension (cc-agents.json with a version array), no network required. This directly advances the registry the user wants.
- **Against:** As specified, this is pure API-proxy plumbing for the multi-tenant proxy world. The evidence — BaseAgentsAPIConfig, AgentsHTTPHandler, get_complete_url, per-provider transforms against endpoints like Gemini v1beta/agents — is an HTTP transform/dispatch layer whose entire job is to translate a unified request into a provider's REST call and proxy it back. Shepherd makes NO API calls, runs NO server, stores NO secrets, and its "agent" is a running local Claude Code GUI session keyed by session_id on the filesystem, not a remote saved object behind a provider key. Adopting the LiteLLM mechanism verbatim would mean building a network client to talk to Gemini/provider agent endpoints — a hard scope violation (network + secrets to auth those calls). The "versions" machinery (AgentVersionsResponse) is meaningful only for a server-hosted object lifecycle; Shepherd has nothing remote to version. So the feature as-given is DONT_ADD on scope; only its idea survives.
- **Ruling:** Split the candidate into mechanism vs. need. The mechanism (provider HTTP CRUD transform, get_complete_url, per-provider agent endpoints, remote version objects) is squarely DONT_ADD: it is proxy plumbing that requires network calls and provider auth, both hard-banned, and Shepherd's "agent" is a local session, not a remote object. But the NEED — agents as saved, named, reusable definitions you can create/list/delete and version — is real, is the user's explicitly highest-weighted future interest, and already has a home: the existing cc-presets.json CRUD store plus the providers list. The right move is to reshape it into a LOCAL cc-agents.json registry: a saved "agent profile" = preset++ (name, folder, provider/model, permMode, seed prompt, gate.tools allow-list, later attached skills/MCP), with the same presetPush/presetList/presetRemove shape, an optional local version array, and a "spawn from agent" action that feeds spawnSpec. That is ADAPT by definition: the underlying need is real but must be rebuilt on Shepherd's own primitives (presets, providers, spawn, gate), reusing patterns already in cc-core. Verdict: ADAPT, high confidence — DONT_ADD the HTTP layer, ADAPT the saved-agent concept onto the local preset/provider store.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend the existing saved-config primitive rather than build a network client. New cc-agents.json registry modeled on cc-presets.json (cc-core.lua presetList/presetPush/presetRemove around line 3640) and the providers list (providerById, spawnProviderKey ~line 2632). An "agent profile" = preset superset: {name, folder, provider, model, permMode, seedPrompt/task, gate.tools allow-list, later: skills[], mcpServers[]}. Add agentList/agentPush/agentRemove (same validate+cap+replace-in-place shape) in pure cc-core (unit-tested, zero hs.*). Wire a "Spawn from agent" action through the existing New Session modal -> spawnSpec (cc-core ~line 3061), seeding the queue (queue per session) with seedPrompt and applying gate.tools. Optional local-only versioning: a versions[] array per agent (no remote object), reusing the session-lineage/respawnSpec mindset. NO get_complete_url, NO AgentsHTTPHandler, NO provider HTTP calls — secrets stay as authTokenEnv resolved by the spawned shell. This is also the natural anchor point for the user's skills/MCP-registry interest (attach to the same agent object).
- **Skeptic check:** I tried to refute the ADAPT on both common grounds and it survives. (a) Not a proxy-in-disguise: the judge already excised the entire mechanism (get_complete_url, AgentsHTTPHandler, per-provider HTTP CRUD, remote version objects) — all of which violate Shepherd's hard bans on network/secrets/multi-tenant. What remains is purely a LOCAL JSON registry feeding a LOCAL spawn; no network call, no key storage (provider auth stays as authTokenEnv resolved by the spawned login shell). (b) The "already have it" trap is the strongest angle and it nearly lands: Shepherd ALREADY has cc-presets.json with full named CRUD (cc-core.lua presetList/presetPush/presetRemove ~L3658-3705, capped, replace-in-place), already wired through the dashboard (claude-dashboard.lua ~L1704-1725) with one-click presetSpawn (~L4026) that feeds spawnSpec. A preset already saves {name, folder, editor, permMode, provider}. So a saved-spawn-config object is genuinely present today. BUT the proposed agent profile is a real superset, not a re-skin: it adds (1) a baked-in seedPrompt/task (presets carry none — task is read live from the n-task field), (2) a per-profile gate.tools allow-list (today gate.tools is per-session/fleet only — resolveGateTools ~L2537, DEFAULT_GATE_TOOLS ~L2525 — never bound to a saved object), and (3) the attach-point for skills[]/mcpServers[], which is the user's EXPLICITLY highest-weighted future interest ("pre-configured agents we can hand work off to, saved into a directory; same with skills and MCPs"). Those three deltas are absent from presets, so DONT_ADD ("just use presets") would deny the user's stated direction and is wrong. Verdict stays ADAPT (high): DONT_ADD the HTTP/proxy layer, ADAPT the saved-agent concept onto the existing preset/provider/spawn/gate primitives — ideally as a thin extension of the preset store (preset++), not a parallel subsystem. The judge's Shepherd mapping is accurate and load-bearing: agentList/agentPush/agentRemove in pure cc-core mirroring presetPush, spawn-from-agent through spawnSpec, gate.tools via resolveGateTools, optional local versions[]. No ADD to downgrade from; no basis to collapse to DONT_ADD.

#### Named MCP toolsets — curated tool bundles grantable as a unit  `[M/M]`
- **For:** The underlying need — "save a reusable bundle of tool config and apply it to a session as a unit instead of wiring it by hand each time" — squarely matches the user's stated high-interest area ("pre-configured agents we can hand work off to, saved into a directory; same with skills and MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough"). Shepherd already has two adjacent primitives this would extend cleanly: (1) the per-session gate.tools allow-list (resolveGateTools / cc-gate-tools/<key>), which already pattern-matches tool names including MCP-style mcp__server__tool names through the cc-approve.sh case block, and (2) spawn presets (cc-presets.json: named {folder, editor, permMode, provider} bundles). A "named toolset" is a natural new field on a preset — pick a saved agent profile in the New Session modal and it pre-populates the gate allow-list (and, when Claude Code's --mcp-config flag is wired, the MCP servers) for that session. That gives the user the "saved agent + skills + MCP registry" they asked for in a way that fits the local session model, with full CRUD living in the same operator-data JSON pattern Shepherd already uses for templates/presets/labels.
- **Against:** The LiteLLM feature as specified is an authorization/multi-tenancy construct: a toolset is GRANTED to keys/teams/agents and resolved at REQUEST time into the concrete allowed-tool list for the caller. Shepherd has no keys, no teams, no per-caller identity, and makes no requests — it is a single human watching one local fleet, so the entire "grant to N principals, resolve per request" machinery is dead weight. The {server_id, tool_name} schema presumes Shepherd manages a registry of MCP servers and brokers tool authorization, which it explicitly does not (no proxy, no secrets, no network). Shepherd also already has per-session tool gating that solves the only local need (least-privilege per session) without a bundle abstraction; a full CRUD toolset table risks scope creep into "build an MCP server manager," which is a bigger product than a single-user GUI console wants. The honest minimal need (reusable session setup) is arguably already covered by spawn presets — adding a toolset table on top may be a solution without a proven local problem.
- **Ruling:** The multi-tenant grant/resolve model (toolsets granted to keys/teams, resolved per request) is pure proxy authorization and is DONT_ADD on its own — Shepherd has no callers to authorize. But the kernel of the feature — a named, reusable bundle of tool/MCP config applied to a session as a unit — lands directly on the user's explicitly stated future interest (saved agents / skills / MCP registry) AND extends two primitives Shepherd already owns (gate.tools allow-list + spawn presets). That combination is the textbook ADAPT case: real local need, must be reshaped onto Shepherd's primitives (drop keys/teams/request-resolution; make it a named profile chosen at spawn that seeds the per-session gate allow-list and, eventually, --mcp-config). It is not ADD because the as-is schema is built around multi-tenant granting Shepherd can't use, and not DONT_ADD because the user specifically called this area out and the gate already pattern-matches mcp__ tool names, so the wiring is plausible. Confidence is medium: the gate-tools half is concrete and low-risk, but the MCP-server-management half depends on Shepherd gaining any MCP awareness it currently lacks, so the value could collapse to "just a richer spawn preset" if MCP management never lands.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend spawn presets (cc-presets.json / M.presetPush/presetList in cc-core.lua:3640+) with an optional named "agent profile" that carries a gate-tools allow-list (and later an MCP-config path). On spawn, the chosen profile seeds the per-session override at cc-gate-tools/<key> consumed by M.resolveGateTools (cc-core.lua:2537) and the cc-approve.sh case " $GATE_TOOLS " block (which already matches mcp__server__tool names). CRUD reuses the existing operator-data JSON + modal pattern (templates/presets). Explicitly drop the LiteLLM keys/teams/agents granting layer and request-time resolution — there are no callers in a single-user local console.
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; the ADAPT survives, but only at its weakest end. Confirmed in-repo: (1) PROXY HALF — DONT_ADD, judge is right. The whole grant/resolve model (toolsets granted to keys/teams/agents, resolved per request) is multi-tenant proxy authorization. Shepherd has no callers, no keys, no teams; HARD SCOPE forbids it. Drop entirely. (2) "ALREADY HAS IT" — partially true, which is why this is ADAPT not ADD. Shepherd already ships a per-session gate-tools override (README ~446: detail-panel Gate dropdown Default/All/None/Custom, stored at ~/.claude/cc-gate-tools/<key>, read every request via M.resolveGateTools cc-core.lua:2537 + the cc-approve.sh case block lines 87-100). So "apply a tool list to a session" exists. What does NOT exist: a *named, reusable, saved* bundle — the Custom override is per-session and ephemeral, not a library you pick at spawn. Spawn presets (cc-core.lua:3658, presetList/presetPush) carry only {name, folder, editor, permMode, provider} — NO tools field. So the residual kernel (a named gate-tools profile, choosable at spawn) is a genuine, if thin, extension of two shipped primitives, and it lands squarely on the user's stated interest in 'saved/reusable agents'. That keeps it above DONT_ADD. (3) TWO CORRECTIONS to the judge's mapping the implementer must heed, or value collapses: - SEMANTIC INVERSION: gate.tools is a HOLD-FOR-APPROVAL / escalation list, not a capability allow-list. Tools NOT in it run freely (cc-approve.sh:96 `*) exit 0`). Putting mcp__server__tool into gate.tools makes Shepherd *prompt* on it — the OPPOSITE of 'granting' it. A LiteLLM 'toolset grant' has no direct analog; the only honest local meaning is 'a saved named list of tools to escalate/hold per session.' The judge's 'seeds the allow-list' phrasing is backwards and would mislead implementation. - MCP HALF IS VAPOR: grep for mcp across claude-dashboard.lua = 0 hits; no --mcp-config, no mcpServers, no registry anywhere. The gate's `mcp__server__tool` matching is just literal whitespace-token string matching (it'd match any token), NOT MCP awareness. So the '--mcp-config path in the profile' is purely speculative and must be deferred, not promised. NET: ADAPT holds but is weak/low-value — the deliverable reduces to 'let the existing per-session Custom gate list be saved as a named, reusable profile and selected at spawn,' a convenience layer over already-shipped gate override + spawn presets. Drop keys/teams/request-resolution AND the MCP-config promise. Borderline DONT_ADD; only the user's explicit 'saved reusable agents' interest plus the genuinely-missing named/reusable layer keep it from falling.

#### MCP access groups + per-key/team tool-level permissions  `[M/M]`
- **For:** Two genuinely useful needs hide inside this feature, and Shepherd already owns the primitives for both. (1) Tool-level gating: Shepherd's gate already restricts WHICH tools require approval (gate.tools allow-list, resolveGateTools per-session least-privilege, autoAllow/autoDeny globs, nonce-bound headless decisions). MCP tools surface to Claude Code as namespaced tool names (mcp__server__tool), so teaching the gated-tools list and the auto-policy globs to recognize MCP tool names is a small, natural extension of a shipped feature — fine-grained per-session MCP tool gating with zero new architecture. (2) The "access group = reusable bundle of MCP servers" idea maps cleanly onto the user's explicitly stated high-interest direction (saved agents / skills / MCP registry we haven't leveraged). A named MCP bundle (servers + an allowed-tools list) could be referenced by a spawn preset / saved-agent profile and injected onto the claude launch line via --mcp-config/--allowedTools — exactly where spawnFlags/spawnSpec/presets/provider-profiles already live. That turns "hand work off to a pre-configured agent" into a concrete spawn, which is the single most-favored future direction.
- **Against:** As specified, this is multi-tenant proxy authorization, and almost none of it survives the move to a single-user console. The load-bearing machinery — API keys, teams, a LiteLLM_ObjectPermissionTable, group-to-key resolution in an auth layer, and a gateway that intercepts list_tools/call_tool RPC to filter and reject — presumes (a) many callers with distinct identities, (b) a proxy sitting on the MCP transport, and (c) server-side enforcement of who-can-call-what across a network. Shepherd has no keys, no teams, no per-caller identity, and crucially is NOT a proxy: sessions invoke MCP servers directly via Claude Code, so there is no chokepoint where Shepherd could intercept call_tool to "reject disallowed tools" — the only enforcement Shepherd has is its approval gate, which gates a SUPERSET (it can require approval, not silently deny at the protocol). The per-key mcp_rpm_limit is pure proxy rate-limiting with no local meaning. Building the as-is feature would mean inventing a key/team/permission-table model that contradicts the hard scope limits (no multi-user, no network).
- **Ruling:** DONT_ADD is wrong because the underlying need is real and doubly favored: tool-level gating already exists in Shepherd (so the MCP-tool slice extends a shipped feature), and reusable MCP-server bundles wired into spawn is the user's stated MCP/saved-agent interest. ADD is wrong because the feature's actual substance — keys, teams, an object-permission table, group->caller auth resolution, gateway interception of call_tool, and per-key RPM — is multi-tenant proxy plumbing that collides head-on with Shepherd's no-multi-user/no-network/not-a-proxy limits, and Shepherd has no RPC chokepoint to enforce protocol-level deny anyway. ADAPT is the honest middle: keep the IDEA (group MCP servers into named, reusable bundles; gate individual MCP tools per session) and reshape it onto Shepherd's real primitives — the gated-tools list / auto-policy globs (for per-session MCP tool gating, enforced via the approval gate, not silent denial) and a saved MCP-bundle that a spawn preset / agent profile references and injects on the claude launch line. Drop keys/teams/object-permission-table/mcp_rpm_limit entirely. Confidence is medium because the bundle-into-spawn half depends on whether Claude Code reliably accepts per-session --mcp-config/--allowedTools across the editors Shepherd targets (the VS Code extension spawn is keystroke-only, so injecting launch flags there is best-effort, same caveat that already limits provider-env spawns).
- **How (Shepherd):** Two anchor points, both existing. (1) Per-session MCP tool gating: extend M.resolveGateTools / M.parseToolList / M.DEFAULT_GATE_TOOLS in cc-core.lua and the matching `case " $GATE_TOOLS "` block in cc-approve.sh so the gated/allowed list recognizes namespaced MCP tool names (mcp__server__tool); reuse the autoAllow/autoDeny glob path to allow/deny MCP tools per session via the existing nonce-bound gate. (2) MCP bundle as a saved spawn ingredient: add a named MCP-server bundle (servers + allowed_tools) stored as operator data alongside cc-presets.json / the provider-profile model, referenced by a spawn preset / future saved-agent profile; thread it into M.spawnFlags / M.spawnSpec (cc-core.lua:3001 / :3061) to emit --mcp-config / --allowedTools on the claude launch line (best-effort for the VS Code extension flavor, reliable for kitty/terminal). Explicitly NOT added: keys, teams, LiteLLM_ObjectPermissionTable, group->caller auth resolution, gateway call_tool interception, mcp_rpm_limit.
- **Skeptic check:** The ADAPT ruling survives scrutiny; I could not refute it, and neither of the two warned failure modes applies. (a) NOT a disguised proxy feature. The literal LiteLLM feature IS multi-tenant proxy plumbing — access-group tags, keys, teams, LiteLLM_ObjectPermissionTable, group->caller auth resolution, gateway call_tool interception, per-key mcp_rpm_limit. That's exactly why ADD is wrong (collides with Shepherd's no-network/no-multi-user/not-a-proxy limits; Shepherd has no RPC chokepoint to deny a tool at the protocol level). But the judge already stripped every one of those by name and kept only two locally-coherent ideas: per-session MCP-tool gating and a saved MCP-server bundle injected on the claude launch line. Both run entirely against one user's local sessions — no network, no keys, no interception. (b) NOT already-present. I grepped all .lua/.sh/README/context.md/todos.md: ZERO mentions of mcp/MCP/allowedTools/--mcp-config. So this is genuinely new for the MCP slice. The gating MECHANISM, however, is real and shipped, which is why DONT_ADD is also wrong: I verified M.resolveGateTools/M.parseToolList/M.DEFAULT_GATE_TOOLS at cc-core.lua:2516-2547 and the `case " $GATE_TOOLS " in / *" $TOOL "*` substring matcher at cc-approve.sh:97-100 — a plain whitespace-delimited match that would accept namespaced names like mcp__server__tool with no parser change, enforced via the existing nonce-bound approval gate (allow/deny, never silent protocol denial). The bundle anchor is equally real: providerEnv (cc-core.lua:2714) is already a 'named bundle of env+model' stored as operator data alongside cc-presets.json (README:200,257), and spawnFlags/spawnInner/spawnSpec (cc-core.lua:3001/2970/3061) already thread launch flags onto the line — so emitting --mcp-config/--allowedTools is a natural extension, not new machinery. This is also the user's explicitly stated HIGH-interest area (saved agents/skills/MCPs in the local spawn-from-a-saved-profile sense), which the ADAPT keeps and the proxy version does not. Confidence stays MEDIUM for the right reason: the bundle-into-spawn half is reliable only for kitty/terminal; the VS Code/Cursor extension spawn is keystroke-only and can't reliably inject launch flags (best-effort) — the same documented caveat that already limits provider-env spawns (cc-core.lua:3108-3119). Drop keys/teams/object-permission-table/group->caller resolution/call_tool interception/mcp_rpm_limit entirely.

#### Per-server MCP health checks (status + last-checked + error)  `[M/M]`
- **For:** The user has explicitly flagged MCPs as a high-interest gap ("MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough") and wants saved/reusable agent and MCP definitions that fit a local Claude-Code-session model. A "did my MCP servers actually connect" signal is the single most useful piece of MCP management for an operator: a session can launch with a stdio/SSE MCP server in its config that silently failed to start, and the human only finds out when a tool call errors mid-turn. Surfacing per-server status (unknown/healthy/unhealthy), last-checked time, and the last error — on a tile or on a saved-agent/spawn profile — turns an invisible failure into a glanceable indicator, exactly the role Shepherd already plays for session liveness. It also dovetails with Shepherd's existing health vocabulary: it already has a stale flag, a hung watchdog, and escalation states, so an MCP-reachability indicator is a natural sibling, not a foreign concept. And it plugs into spawn: a saved-agent profile that declares required MCP servers could be pre-flighted before launch.
- **Against:** The LiteLLM implementation is pure multi-tenant-proxy plumbing and violates every hard scope limit. health_check_server opens its own network session to a shared, DB-registered MCP endpoint, writes status/last_health_check/error onto a server row, and serves it over an HTTP GET /server/health with teams attached — that is a network server, a shared multi-user registry, and a proxy actively connecting to remote services, none of which Shepherd does or should do. Shepherd makes NO API/network calls and stores no server rows; it reads local status files off disk. There is also a real redundancy argument: Shepherd's health story is about whether a Claude Code SESSION is alive (stale/hung/error), and the agent here is a running session, NOT a server — adding a second, network-flavored health subsystem risks scope creep into "Shepherd monitors infrastructure," which is precisely the proxy framing to avoid. Shepherd has zero MCP plumbing today, so this is net-new surface (config discovery, parsing, a polling cadence, a new tile region) for a signal Claude Code arguably should expose itself.
- **Ruling:** DONT_ADD is wrong because the user's stated MCP interest is explicit and weighted favorably, and a local MCP-reachability signal is genuinely useful to an operator driving sessions that depend on MCP tools. ADD is wrong because the LiteLLM mechanics — Shepherd opening network sessions, writing health onto DB rows, exposing /server/health with teams — are the exact proxy/multi-tenant/network shape Shepherd forbids and cannot be copied close to as-is. That leaves ADAPT. The reshaped feature drops the network ping and the HTTP endpoint entirely: instead of Shepherd connecting to servers, it READS Claude Code's own local MCP state (the .mcp.json / ~/.claude.json config plus whatever connection/error state Claude Code records) for a given session or saved-agent profile, and renders a per-server status + last-checked + last-error indicator using the same local-file, off-by-default, degrade-gracefully pattern as the rest of Shepherd. This serves the real need (know when a session's MCP servers failed) while staying a local single-user console. Effort is M: it needs a small reader for Claude Code's MCP config/state, a derivation step in cc-core, and a render region — but it is net-new surface (no existing MCP plumbing to extend) and depends on what MCP connection state Claude Code actually exposes locally, which is the main uncertainty.
- **How (Shepherd):** Do NOT replicate health_check_server / GET /server/health (no network pings, no server rows, no endpoint). Instead: (1) a local reader (new fx + a cc-status.sh-style helper, or direct file read) that parses Claude Code's MCP config for a session — .mcp.json in the project, plus ~/.claude.json — and any locally-recorded connection/error state; (2) a pure derivation in cc-core.lua alongside parseStatusList/the stale+hung logic that maps each configured MCP server to unknown/healthy/unhealthy + last-checked + last-error, mirroring how it.stale and it.hung are derived; (3) surface it in claude-dashboard.lua as a small per-tile or detail-panel indicator (sibling to the existing hung/stale/escalation badges), off by default; (4) optionally tie it into spawn/saved-agent profiles so a profile that declares required MCP servers shows their reachability. Reuse the existing local-file + graceful-degradation + opt-in conventions; no new network or storage primitives.
- **Skeptic check:** The ADAPT verdict survives refutation, but barely, and its scope must be tightened. I tried both standard downgrades and neither lands against the ADAPTED version. (a) Proxy-in-disguise: the ORIGINAL LiteLLM feature (Shepherd opening network sessions, pinging servers, writing health to DB rows, exposing GET /server/health) IS the forbidden network/multi-tenant shape — but the judge already stripped the ping and the endpoint, reshaping it into a LOCAL read, so this refutation only kills the un-adapted form. (b) Already-have-it: verified zero MCP plumbing anywhere in Shepherd — `grep -ri mcp` over cc-core.lua/claude-dashboard.lua/shell hooks/README/todos returns nothing; no saved-agent-profile or skills-registry feature exists either. Not present. The user's stated future interest in MCP surfacing is genuine and weighs favorably. So it is neither proxy nor duplicate. WHAT THE EVIDENCE FORCES (the judge's flagged 'main uncertainty', now resolved): the health signal this feature is NAMED for is NOT reliably available locally. `~/.claude.json` stores only MCP CONFIG (command/args/env/type, per-project enabled/disabled lists via mcpServers/enabledMcpjsonServers/disabledMcpjsonServers) and NO runtime status/last_health_check/last_error. The cc-status session JSON Shepherd reads has no MCP field. The only place with real per-server status+last-error+timing is ~/.claude/debug/*.txt (lines like 'MCP server \"postmark\" Connection failed: MCP error -32000' and 'Successfully connected ... in 1784ms'), but debug logging is OFF by default (needs --debug/MCP_DEBUG): verified only 2 debug logs exist, both ~3 weeks stale (May 22), and NONE of the 8 most-recent live sessions has one. mcp-needs-auth-cache.json only flags OAuth-needed for two claude.ai connectors. Consequence: a true health indicator would render 'unknown' for essentially every live session — not a feature. So the ADAPT must be narrowed from 'health check' to an MCP-server INVENTORY/ENABLEMENT indicator: a pure cc-core derivation reading ~/.claude.json + project .mcp.json that maps each session/saved-agent-profile to its configured servers and enabled/disabled state (the reliably-local part, tying into the user's high-interest 'saved agent profiles + MCP' theme), with best-effort last-error parsed from debug logs ONLY when they exist, degrading to 'unknown' — surfaced as an off-by-default per-tile/detail badge sibling to hung/stale, no network ping, no endpoint, no new storage. The verdict holds as ADAPT (not DONT_ADD) because the local-inventory core is real, useful, net-new, and on-theme; but the literal 'health/ping' core is the proxy-shaped, locally-unsourceable part that cannot be copied and must be dropped.

#### Access groups (named bundles of models/MCP/agents assigned to teams/keys)  `[M/L]`
- **For:** The underlying need — a single named, reusable bundle of capabilities (which model, which MCP servers, which skills/agent profiles, which tool gate policy) that you apply with one click — maps directly onto the user's explicitly stated highest-interest area: "pre-configured 'agents' we can hand work off to, saved into a directory; same with skills and MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough." Shepherd already has the seed of this: spawn presets (cc-core.lua:3640+) are named {folder, editor, permMode, provider} bundles persisted in cc-presets.json. An "access group" generalizes that preset into a capability profile: add mcpServers, skills/agentRefs, and a gate.tools allow-list to the bundle, and spawning from it would (a) launch claude with the right provider/model, (b) pass --mcp-config / --append-system-prompt or write the right .mcp.json, and (c) seed that session's gate.tools policy. That is a coherent, local, single-user version of "name a capability set once, apply it everywhere" — and it directly advances the saved-agents/skills/MCP registry the user wants.
- **Against:** The feature AS SPECIFIED is multi-tenant proxy plumbing. Its defining mechanic is the membership/propagation layer: assigned_team_ids/assigned_key_ids arrays, _sync_add_access_group_to_teams/keys endpoints, idempotent syncing of a group id into every team/key's access_group_ids with cache patching (schema.prisma:1226-1243, 417, 143). Shepherd has NO teams, NO keys, NO users, NO network server — every one of those primitives is a hard scope violation. The value proposition "edit the group once and it propagates to all members" is meaningless when there is exactly one user and access is enforced by claude itself at launch, not by a central authority handing out grants. Strip out the team/key propagation and you are left with "presets, but with more fields" — which is an incremental extension of an existing feature, not this feature. There is also real risk that "assign models/MCP/agents to groups" drifts toward implying Shepherd governs/enforces access (a permission system), when in a local model claude already controls what it can call; Shepherd can only seed launch config, not enforce.
- **Ruling:** DONT_ADD is wrong because the user's stated future interest (saved agents / skills registry / MCP management) is the exact subject matter, and Shepherd already owns a primitive (spawn presets) that this generalizes — so the NEED is real and local. ADD is wrong because the literal feature is built on teams/keys/membership-propagation, all of which are hard-scope-violating multi-tenant constructs with no Shepherd analog. That leaves ADAPT: keep the "named bundle of capabilities applied at spawn" idea, drop the entire assignment/propagation half. Concretely this is "presets v2 / agent profiles": extend the preset record (and cc-presets.json) with mcpServers, skills/agentRefs, and a per-profile gate.tools list, and teach the spawn path to materialize them (provider env it already does; MCP via --mcp-config or a written .mcp.json; system/skill injection via append-system-prompt; gate policy seeded into the new session). No central enforcement, no membership, no network. The membership idea has a thin local echo only — applying a profile to a whole session GROUP at once — but that is a nice-to-have, not the core.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend the existing spawn-preset primitive into an "agent/capability profile". cc-core.lua:3640-3722 (presetList/presetPush/presetForProject) gains new bundle fields: mcpServers (server ids/paths), skills/agentRefs (directory of saved agent definitions), and gate.tools (per-profile allow-list). Storage stays in cc-presets.json (PRESET_FILE, claude-dashboard.lua:65) — operator data, no secrets, consistent with current design. The spawn glue that already injects provider/model env (cc-config providers block, spawn.provider at cc-config.example.json:80,87-106) extends to materialize MCP (write/point at .mcp.json or pass --mcp-config) and skill/system injection at launch, and to seed the new session's gate.tools (cc-config.example.json:68) from the profile. Explicitly DROP: assigned_team_ids/assigned_key_ids, the _sync endpoints, and any central access enforcement — Shepherd seeds launch config, it does not govern access.
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; ADAPT survives. Verified against the codebase. (1) NOT a disguised proxy: the literal feature is pure multi-tenant — teams, keys, membership-propagation, central enforcement — none of which exist in Shepherd (grep for team/access_group/membership/assigned_/propagat is clean) and all of which the judge already DROPPED. What's left is launch-time-only config, no network/governance. (2) NOT already-present, but genuinely an EXTENSION of an existing primitive — which is exactly what ADAPT means, so this does not bump it to DONT_ADD nor down from ADD. Shepherd already owns the 'named bundle of capabilities applied at spawn' idea in two places: provider profiles are literally documented at cc-core.lua:2618-2620 as 'a named bundle of NON-SECRET env vars + a model id ... injected into the claude spawn', and spawn presets at cc-core.lua:3640-3722 are named {folder,editor,permMode,provider} bundles. The gate even RESERVES the next layer: resolveGateTools (cc-core.lua:2528-2547) documents precedence 'per-session override > provider/mode default > fleet gate.tools' with providerDefault 'reserved for a future per-provider layer (pass nil for v1)'. So the bundling machinery exists; what does NOT exist yet (grep clean) is MCP config, a skills/agent-definition registry, or --mcp-config/--append-system-prompt wiring. (3) The kernel the judge keeps — 'presets v2 / agent profiles': add mcpServers + skills/agentRefs + per-profile gate.tools to the preset record and materialize them at launch (env it already does at spawnSpec cc-core.lua:3061; MCP via --mcp-config/.mcp.json; system/skill via --append-system-prompt added to spawnFlags cc-core.lua:3001; gate.tools seeded through the already-reserved providerDefault slot) — maps cleanly onto real primitives and lands squarely on the user's stated high-interest area (saved agents/skills/MCP). It's local, single-user, off-the-network, no enforcement. ADD is wrong (membership/propagation are scope-violating); DONT_ADD is wrong (the need is real and the contents are genuinely absent). ADAPT is correct. Note: the 'apply a profile to a whole session group at once' echo of membership is a defensible nice-to-have (Shepherd has session groups) but is NOT the core — it must not be read as license to import any assignment/enforcement model.

#### Claude Code Plugin Marketplace (register/list/enable/disable/delete by git source + marketplace.json discovery)  `[M/L]`
- **For:** This is the single closest LiteLLM feature to the user's explicitly stated high-interest area: "Pre-configured 'agents' we can hand work off to, saved into a directory; same with skills and MCPs — we haven't leveraged those well enough." A Claude Code plugin is exactly the bundle that packages agents + skills + MCP servers, and this feature is a curated, enable/disable-able registry of them keyed to git sources. Shepherd already has the local analog of a profile registry (cc-presets.json: name/folder/editor/permMode/provider) and a spawnSpec that builds the launch command, so the "registry of reusable session add-ons, pick which are active, apply them on spawn" pattern slots into existing primitives. Crucially, the plugin FILES live on GitHub and Claude Code itself does the clone/install via its own `claude plugin` CLI and `~/.claude` config — that consumption path is local and single-user, exactly Shepherd's world. A local plugin/agent profile registry would let an operator say "spawn this folder as the 'reviewer' agent with these MCP servers + skills enabled," which is real leverage Shepherd lacks today.
- **Against:** The feature AS BUILT is a multi-tenant proxy primitive: a DB table (LiteLLM_ClaudeCodePluginTable), a repository layer, and an HTTP endpoint that SERVES /claude-code/marketplace.json so many networked clients can `claude plugin marketplace add` against the proxy and install `<name>@litellm`. That is a network server distributing a shared catalog to a fleet of remote users — a direct violation of Shepherd's hard scope (NO network server, NO multi-user, reads status off the LOCAL filesystem, does not proxy). None of the serving half makes sense for one person on one Mac who can just run `claude plugin marketplace add github:owner/repo` directly. The git-source registry/DB/manifest_json machinery is largely redundant with Claude Code's own native plugin and marketplace mechanism; Shepherd adding a curated catalog server would reinvent a feature the CLI already owns, for an audience of one. There is also genuine scope-creep risk: a "marketplace" invites a publish/version/discovery surface that pulls Shepherd toward being a distribution platform rather than a session console.
- **Ruling:** Split the feature in two. The SERVING half (DB-backed registry + marketplace.json HTTP endpoint + multi-client install-by-name) is pure proxy/network plumbing and DONT_ADD on its own — it breaks the no-network/single-user invariant and duplicates Claude Code's native plugin mechanism. But the NEED underneath — a curated, enable/disable set of reusable Claude Code extensions (which is precisely agents + skills + MCP servers) applied when you launch work — is exactly what the user flagged as under-leveraged, and it has a clean local shape. The win for Shepherd is not "serve a marketplace" but "make plugins/agents/MCP first-class in the spawn profile": extend the existing local preset registry with an enabled plugin/MCP/agent list and a per-entry enabled flag, and have spawn run `claude plugin install`/write the per-project config (or pass the relevant flags) so a session boots with the chosen bundle. That reuses cc-presets, spawnSpec, and the off-by-default/degrade-gracefully discipline, stays single-user and local, and directly advances the saved-agents/skills/MCP goal. Hence ADAPT — not ADD (don't copy the server), not DONT_ADD (the need is real and aligns with stated interest). Confidence is medium because the exact local mechanism depends on how the installed `claude plugin` CLI behaves on this Mac (memory notes there's currently no claude CLI here), so the apply-on-spawn path needs live verification.
- **How (Shepherd):** Do NOT build the DB table or the marketplace.json HTTP endpoint. Instead extend the LOCAL spawn-profile registry: cc-core.lua presetPush/presetList/spawnSpec (~lines 3061, 3640-3720) and cc-presets.json. Add an optional `plugins`/`mcp`/`agents` list (plus a per-entry `enabled` flag) to a preset, validated like the existing name/folder fields. Have spawnSpec/FX.spawnSession apply the enabled set on launch — either by invoking the local `claude plugin install <name>@<localmarketplace>` / writing the per-project plugin+MCP config, or by passing the appropriate launch flags — gated behind the existing `spawn.live` (default dry-run) safety. Surface it in the existing "New session" modal / preset chips (claude-dashboard.lua spawn UI), and ledger the applied bundle. Keep it off-by-default and degrade gracefully when the `claude plugin` CLI is absent.
- **Skeptic check:** The ADAPT ruling survives my attempt to refute it. I checked both error patterns and the live environment. (a) Multi-tenant PROXY dressed as local: The SERVING half of the feature — DB-backed registry + a /claude-code/marketplace.json HTTP endpoint + install-by-name across many clients — IS exactly that, and the judge already ruled it DONT_ADD on its own. It breaks Shepherd's hard NO-network/single-user invariant (context.md line 13) and duplicates native Claude Code mechanics. The judge did NOT recommend copying the server, so this error does not apply to the ruling. (b) Already has it: Shepherd has NO plugin/MCP/skill/marketplace handling anywhere — grep for "plugin"/"mcp"/"skill"/"marketplace" in cc-core.lua, claude-dashboard.lua, and the JSON configs returns nothing. So the ADAPTED half is not re-adding an existing Shepherd feature. BUT I did find that the underlying registry already exists NATIVELY in the local `claude` CLI: I ran `claude plugin --help` (exit 0 at /Users/adam/.local/bin/claude) and it ships install/enable/disable/list/uninstall plus `claude plugin marketplace add <URL|path|GitHub repo>`. This means the curated enable/disable set the judge calls "the need underneath" is already maintained by Claude Code itself — so the correct Shepherd move is NOT to build a registry but to invoke the existing CLI on spawn. That is precisely why the verdict is ADAPT (extend cc-presets/spawnSpec to carry an enabled plugin/MCP/agent list and run `claude plugin install`/write per-project config on launch), NOT ADD. What survives is a thin, genuinely-local extension of an existing primitive (M.spawnSpec ~cc-core.lua:3061, presetPush/presetList ~3673/3658, cc-presets.json), gated behind the existing spawn.live dry-run safety, ledger-able, and degrading gracefully if the CLI is absent — and it directly advances the user's stated high-interest goal of saved/reusable agents+skills+MCPs handed off at launch. It stays single-user, local, no-network. One factual correction strengthens (not weakens) the ruling: the judge's medium confidence cited memory that "there's currently no claude CLI here." That memory is now stale — the `claude` CLI IS present and fully plugin-capable on this Mac, so the apply-on-spawn path is feasible and the medium-confidence caveat is largely resolved. I could not push this to DONT_ADD (the local need and shape are real and aligned with stated interest), and ADD->ADAPT is moot because the ruling is already ADAPT. Verdict holds at ADAPT.

### Guardrails & Policy

#### Tool-call permission guardrail (allow/deny rules over agent tool calls, deny/allow-by-default)  `[H/S]`
- **For:** The underlying need — inspect a model's tool call and allow/deny it by rule, with a configurable default — is exactly Shepherd's PreToolUse gate, so this is squarely in scope (it's a local hook, not a network proxy). And there is ONE genuine capability gap worth the favorable weighting: LiteLLM's default_action: 'deny' gives a true deny-by-default / allowlist-only posture (anything not explicitly allowed is auto-denied with no human in the loop), which maps cleanly onto the user's stated interest in pre-configured, hand-off-able 'agent' profiles. A saved agent profile that says "this agent may only call Read, Grep, and Bash(npm test*); auto-deny everything else" is a least-privilege sandbox you can trust to run unattended — Shepherd today can name which tools are GATED (gate.tools / per-session override) but a non-matching call falls through to the native flow or a human, not to an automatic deny. on_disallowed_action: 'rewrite' is also a behavior Shepherd lacks (it only allow/deny/falls-through). So the feature points at a real, in-scope policy mode Shepherd doesn't fully have.
- **Against:** Shepherd already has the overwhelming majority of this feature and it's not a thin overlap — it's the same machine. cc-approve.sh is a PreToolUse gate that: pattern-matches tool names AND Tool(glob) command shapes (pattern_match), runs autoDeny then autoAllow lists with a documented precedence where deny beats allow ("safety first"), decides HEADLESSLY without a panel, has per-session gated-tool overrides with a "-"/NONE sentinel, and ledgers every decision with provenance. The LiteLLM rules/default_action/on_disallowed_action model is essentially Shepherd's patterns.autoDeny/autoAllow + gate.tools, already shipped and unit-tested (gate.test.sh). on_disallowed_action: 'rewrite' (mutating the model's response to drop a tool call) is a server-side response-rewriting trick that fits an HTTP proxy, not a local hook — Claude Code's PreToolUse contract is allow/deny/ask, not "edit the assistant turn," so rewrite is largely a scope mismatch. Building the LiteLLM feature as-described would re-implement what exists; the only net-new piece is a deny-by-default switch, which is a small flag on an existing mechanism, not a new feature.
- **Ruling:** The need is real and in-scope, but ~90% of it already ships in Shepherd's tool gate — so this is not ADD (don't rebuild) and not DONT_ADD (the one missing piece is genuinely useful and user-aligned). It's ADAPT: extend the existing policy engine with a deny-by-default mode rather than build a parallel guardrail. Concretely, the gap is a single new policy posture: today a tool call that matches no autoAllow/autoDeny pattern and isn't covered by autopilot/approveRepeats falls through to a human (or the native prompt). Adding policies.patterns.defaultAction: 'deny' would make the gate auto-deny any gated-tool call not explicitly allowed — turning gate.tools + autoAllow into a true allowlist sandbox. That is the least-privilege primitive a saved/reusable "agent profile" needs to be handed work unattended, which is exactly the user's stated future interest. The on_disallowed_action: 'rewrite' branch is dropped: Claude Code's PreToolUse hook contract is allow/deny/ask, and response-rewriting is a proxy-side behavior with no local analogue. So: adapt by adding a deny-default flag to the existing pattern policy + (later) wiring it into a per-agent-profile spawn, not by importing a new guardrail integration.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extends the existing PreToolUse gate. (1) cc-approve.sh: after autoDeny→autopilot→autoAllow→approveRepeats, before routing to the panel, add a deny-default branch gated on a new config key policies.patterns.defaultAction ('ask' = today's behavior, default; 'deny' = emit_deny for any gated-tool call that reached this point). Keep autoDeny-beats-everything precedence. (2) cc-core.lua: resolve/validate the new key alongside resolveGateTools/parseToolList; ledger the auto-deny with by:'defaultDeny' so the gate-decision log + risk score pick it up. (3) cc-config.example.json policies.patterns block + README. (4) Future tie-in to the user's saved-agent interest: a per-session override file (mirroring CC_GATE_TOOLS_DIR/<key>) could carry both the allowlist and defaultAction so a spawned 'agent profile' runs least-privilege. Drop on_disallowed_action:'rewrite' — no local hook analogue (PreToolUse is allow/deny/ask).
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute and could not — the ADAPT (high) ruling survives both common-error checks against the actual code. PROXY-DRESS CHECK (error a): The LiteLLM feature has two halves. The deny/allow-by-default + tool-name pattern-match half has a real LOCAL analogue: Shepherd's PreToolUse gate (cc-approve.sh) already pattern-matches tool names and runs no network/proxy. The on_disallowed_action:'rewrite' half is genuinely proxy-only — Claude Code's PreToolUse contract is strictly allow/deny/ask (no response rewriting), so there's no local hook for it. The judge already DROPS the rewrite branch, so the verdict does not smuggle in the multi-tenant-proxy behavior. Pass. ALREADY-HAVE CHECK (error b): Confirmed by reading the source. The gate ALREADY has: tool-name pattern matching (cc-approve.sh:48-61 pattern_match; 145-186 match_patterns over autoAllow/autoDeny globs), a tool allowlist (gate.tools + per-session override, lines 80-94 / cc-core.lua:2516-2546 parseToolList/resolveGateTools), and a configurable post-policy default — but ONLY 'route to human / native fallback'. There is NO deny-by-default: grep for defaultAction/default_action/defaultDeny/deny-by-default across cc-approve.sh, cc-core.lua, cc-config.example.json, README.md, context.md, todos.md returned ZERO hits. The policies.patterns block in cc-config.example.json (lines 159-170) is exactly {enabled, autoAllow, autoDeny} with no default-action key. So ~90% ships and the one missing piece (a deny-default posture that turns gate.tools+autoAllow into a true allowlist sandbox) is genuinely absent. Therefore not ADD (don't rebuild a parallel guardrail — extend the existing engine) and not DONT_ADD (the missing deny-default primitive doesn't exist and is in-scope, local, single-user). It is the least-privilege primitive the user's stated future interest (hand work to saved/reusable 'agent profiles' run unattended) needs, and the judge's mapping is faithful to the architecture: add policies.patterns.defaultAction ('ask' default = today; 'deny' = emit_deny for any gated-tool call that reached the end of the autoDeny→autopilot→autoAllow→approveRepeats chain), preserving autoDeny-beats-everything precedence, ledger by:'defaultDeny', config/README entry, with the per-session override file (CC_GATE_TOOLS_DIR/<key>) able to later carry both allowlist + defaultAction for a spawned agent profile. ADAPT (high) holds.

#### Key-scoped route/model/cache-control access lists + key_type  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The unifying idea behind this feature is least-privilege bound to a reusable identity: a key carries a capability bundle (which models, which routes, what mode) and requests outside it are refused. Shepherd already has the same instinct (Feature D per-session gated-tools least-privilege via resolveGateTools, plus autoAllow/autoDeny glob policies), and resolveGateTools explicitly reserves a `providerDefault` layer "for a future per-provider layer (pass nil for v1)" — i.e. a place to hang exactly this kind of profile-scoped policy. Two pieces map cleanly onto Shepherd's primitives: (a) `models` allow-list → a saved spawn preset / provider profile that pins ANTHROPIC_MODEL and refuses to spawn off-list, and (b) `key_type=read_only` → a coarse "read-only agent" preset that gates all mutating tools (Bash/Write/Edit/MultiEdit/NotebookEdit auto-deny) — a one-line preset that finally makes the gate's "gate everything" path useful. This rides directly on the user's stated interest in saved/reusable agent profiles: a profile that ships its own capability policy is a real, local, single-user win, and the gate already enforces it at the hook layer with no network.
- **Against:** The bulk of the feature is HTTP-proxy authorization that has no referent in Shepherd. `allowed_routes` / `allowed_passthrough_routes` / `key_type=llm_api|management` are route ACLs on a multi-tenant API surface — Shepherd has no server, no routes, no API keys (hard scope limit: NO network server, NO secrets storage). `allowed_cache_controls` polices `cache_control` directives on outbound Anthropic API requests, but Shepherd explicitly never proxies or makes API calls, so there is literally nothing to 403. route_checks.py and cache_control_check.py are pure proxy plumbing serving many keys/users/teams. Even the salvageable parts are largely redundant: per-session tool least-privilege (the closest analog) already ships and works, and "pin a model" is already what a provider profile does via env injection. So the genuinely new surface is thin — a read-only preset flavor — and could be argued as a minor extension of existing presets rather than worth flagging as its own feature.
- **Ruling:** Three of the four sub-features are flatly out of scope: route ACLs and key_type=llm_api/management are HTTP-proxy authorization with no Shepherd referent (no server, no routes, no keys), and allowed_cache_controls polices API requests Shepherd never makes. That kills a straight ADD. But DONT_ADD would be wrong too, because the spine of the feature — a reusable identity that carries a least-privilege capability bundle and refuses out-of-bundle work — is a real need Shepherd already half-implements (Feature D resolveGateTools + autoAllow/autoDeny) and explicitly left a seam for (the reserved providerDefault layer). The user's stated future interest in saved agent profiles is the multiplier: a saved profile that pins a model and ships a tool-gate policy (including a coarse read_only flavor that auto-denies all mutating tools) is a clean, local, single-user fit that strengthens both the gate and the presets system. That is the textbook ADAPT shape — real underlying need, reshaped onto Shepherd's gate/profile/preset primitives, with the proxy-only parts (routes, cache-control, key_type=management) dropped entirely.
- **How (Shepherd):** Hang a capability policy off the saved-agent/profile layer, enforced by the existing gate. Concretely: (1) extend the spawn preset / provider profile schema (cc-core.lua presetList/presetPush ~L3658, providerByModel ~L2631) with an optional `gateTools` policy and a coarse `keyType`/mode (e.g. "read_only" → gate all of DEFAULT_GATE_TOOLS, "standard" → fleet default). (2) Feed that into the already-reserved `providerDefault` argument of M.resolveGateTools (cc-core.lua:2537) so a profile's policy becomes the per-session default, with the per-session override still winning — KEEP IN SYNC with the override case block in cc-approve.sh:87-94. (3) Optional: a profile-level model allow-list checked at spawn time so spawning off-profile model is refused. Drop entirely the HTTP-only parts: allowed_routes, allowed_passthrough_routes, key_type=llm_api/management, and allowed_cache_controls (no routes, no API calls in Shepherd).
- **Skeptic check:** Refutation attempt fails; ADAPT holds. Verified against the code: Shepherd has NO HTTP server, NO routes, NO outbound API calls, and NO cache_control handling (grep clean in cc-core.lua/claude-dashboard.lua). That kills three of the four sub-features outright — allowed_routes/allowed_passthrough_routes (proxy authorization, no referent), key_type=llm_api/management (proxy auth tiers, no referent), and allowed_cache_controls (polices API requests Shepherd never makes). These are the error-mode-(a) "proxy feature in disguise" parts, and the judge already DROPS all three. So the ADAPT is not smuggling the proxy in. Error mode (b) "already has it" also does not apply to the surviving piece. The spine — a reusable identity carrying a least-privilege capability bundle that refuses out-of-bundle work — is genuinely only half-present. Confirmed: M.resolveGateTools (cc-core.lua:2537) takes a providerDefault arg that is reserved and the live call site passes nil (claude-dashboard.lua:5446: resolveGateTools(ovr, nil, gate.tools)). Presets (presetPush/presetList ~L3658-3694) carry only name/folder/editor/permMode/provider — there is NO gateTools policy and NO model allow-list on a saved profile today. So a saved profile that pins a model and ships a tool-gate policy (plus a coarse read_only flavor that auto-gates DEFAULT_GATE_TOOLS = 'Bash Write Edit MultiEdit NotebookEdit', cc-core.lua:2525) is a new, local, single-user capability that extends two existing primitives rather than re-adding one. It directly serves the user's stated interest in 'pre-configured agents saved into a directory.' Textbook ADAPT: real need, reshaped onto Shepherd's preset/gate/providerDefault seam, proxy-only parts dropped. One calibration note (not a downgrade): the judge's 'high' is slightly inflated — once 3 of 4 sub-features are cut, what remains is a modest extension (a gateTools field + optional model allow-list wired into the already-reserved argument), closer to medium value. The verdict is correct; only the confidence is generous.

#### Per-key guardrail/policy attachment, enforced params & disable-global-guardrails  `[H/M]`
- **For:** Shepherd already has every ingredient this feature is made of, just not bundled. It has a fleet-wide policy engine (policies.patterns.autoAllow/autoDeny, autopilot, approveRepeats enforced in cc-approve.sh) AND a per-session override layer (resolveGateTools / gate.tools, including a "-"/NONE sentinel that gates nothing for one session). That per-session override is structurally identical to LiteLLM attaching policy to a key and to disable_global_guardrails. The gap is real and worth closing: (1) policies today are anonymous fleet-level config, not NAMED reusable bundles a session can attach; (2) there is no clean per-session opt-IN/opt-OUT of the fleet policy as a unit (only the tool-list override exists, not "this session ignores the fleet autoDeny set"); (3) there is no enforced_params equivalent — a "saved agent" can't yet declare locked spawn settings (force permMode, force a gate.tools list it cannot loosen, force a provider). Crucially this lands squarely on the user's stated future interest: "pre-configured agents saved into a directory." A named policy/guardrail bundle that a spawn preset or provider profile attaches IS the local single-user version of per-key policy attachment — spawn-a-session-from-a-saved-agent-profile with its guardrails baked in. Presets already carry {folder, editor, permMode, provider}; adding an attached policy-set name and enforced/locked fields is a natural extension, not new surface area.
- **Against:** The literal feature is keyed on an abstraction Shepherd does not and must not have: an issued API key scoped to a user/team/app in a multi-tenant proxy. Shepherd has no keys, no users, no tenancy — its unit is one local running Claude Code session at one keyboard, so "per-key attachment" has no direct referent. The guardrails LiteLLM attaches are content-filter pipelines (PII redaction, prompt-injection/secret scanning) applied to request/response BODIES inside the proxy — Shepherd never sees a request body, makes no API calls, and is explicitly NOT a proxy, so that entire slice is impossible and out of scope. enforced_params is request-body field validation against an HTTP payload; there is no payload in Shepherd's keystroke-to-a-GUI-session model. And Shepherd ALREADY ships a per-session least-privilege gate plus a fleet policy engine with auto-allow/auto-deny globs — the marginal additions (naming the bundles, opt-out, locked spawn fields) risk being scope creep dressed up as a port, adding config-shape and a policy-precedence matrix to maintain for a single user who can already edit gate.tools per session.
- **Ruling:** DONT_ADD is too harsh and ADD is wrong (the key/request-body model doesn't exist here), so ADAPT wins.
