The against case correctly kills the literal feature — no keys, no request bodies, no content-filter pipeline — and that whole proxy slice is firmly DONT_ADD. But the residual NEED is real and already half-built: Shepherd has a per-session gate-tools override and a fleet policy engine, yet has no way to (a) name a reusable policy/guardrail bundle, (b) let a session opt in/out of the fleet policy as a unit (the local mirror of disable_global_guardrails), or (c) lock spawn settings a saved agent can't loosen (the defensible read of enforced_params). All three reshape cleanly onto existing primitives — gate.tools per-session override, the policies.patterns engine in cc-approve.sh, and spawn presets/provider profiles — and they directly advance the user's flagged interest in saved/reusable agent profiles. That is the textbook ADAPT bar: real need, reshaped from key→session/preset and from request-validation→spawn-time-locked-settings, extending features Shepherd already has. Drop the content-filter/PII guardrail interpretation entirely; keep the named-policy-bundle + per-session opt-out + locked-spawn-fields interpretation.
- **How (Shepherd):** Add NAMED policy bundles to cc-config.json policies (e.g. policies.bundles.{name} = {autoAllow, autoDeny, gateTools, lockedPermMode}). Extend the spawn preset/provider-profile schema (cc-core.lua presetPush/presetList ~3658-3694; provider profiles ~2618-2640) with an attached `policy` name and a `locked` flag set (the enforced_params analog: force permMode / force a gate.tools list the session can't loosen / force provider). At gate time, cc-approve.sh resolves the session's attached bundle: extend the per-session override block (~87-94) and match_patterns (~145-164) so a session can (i) point at a named bundle's autoAllow/autoDeny instead of the fleet set, and (ii) carry a disableGlobal flag that skips the fleet policies (the disable_global_guardrails mirror), with resolution kept in sync via a new core helper alongside resolveGateTools (~2537). Ledger the attached-bundle name in the existing decision events (ledger_decision, cc-approve.sh ~127). Explicitly OUT: any content-filter/PII/prompt-injection scanning of request bodies and any HTTP request-field validation — Shepherd sees no bodies.
- **Skeptic check:** I tried to refute the ADAPT and could not. I verified the cited primitives in the actual code: the fleet policy engine (cc-approve.sh ~138-196: policies.patterns.autoAllow/autoDeny, autopilot, approveRepeats), the per-session gate override (cc-approve.sh ~80-94 + cc-core.lua resolveGateTools ~2537, with a providerDefault param already "reserved for a future per-provider layer"), and the spawn preset / provider-profile schema (cc-core.lua presetPush/presetList ~3658-3694, providerById ~2632). All exist as the judge described. Failure mode (a) "proxy dressed as local" does NOT apply: the literal LiteLLM feature is pure proxy machinery (API keys, HTTP request bodies, content-filter/PII/injection scanning, enforced request fields), and the judge already excised every bit of it, putting content-filtering and request-field validation explicitly OUT. What survives — (1) named reusable policy bundles, (2) per-session opt-in/out of the fleet policy as a unit (the disable_global_guardrails mirror), (3) spawn fields a saved preset can't loosen (the defensible enforced_params read) — needs no server, no keys, no request bodies. It rides entirely on local cc-config + the gate's per-session override + preset/profile schema. Failure mode (b) "already has it" does NOT apply: grep across cc-core.lua, claude-dashboard.lua, cc-approve.sh and cc-config.example.json confirms there is NO named-bundle concept, NO unit-level per-session opt-out of fleet policies, and NO locked-field mechanism. Today the policies.* block is a single fleet-global object and the per-session override covers only WHICH tools wait (gate.tools), not the autoAllow/autoDeny set or an opt-out toggle. The residual need is genuinely unbuilt. It is also not merely a one-feature extension (which would push ADD->ADAPT) — it reshapes across three distinct primitives and shifts the model key->session and request-validation->spawn-time-locked-settings, while directly advancing the user's stated high-interest goal of saved/reusable agent profiles. That is the textbook ADAPT bar. Minor honest calibration within ADAPT: the named-bundle + per-session opt-out are the strong, clearly-useful half; the locked-spawn-fields piece is the weakest (a single-user console rarely needs to defend a preset against itself) and could be deferred — but that is scoping inside ADAPT, not grounds to downgrade. The ruling holds.

#### MCP security guardrail + end-user MCP permission filtering (block/alert on unregistered servers)  `[H/M]`
- **For:** Half of this lands directly on a primitive Shepherd already owns. In Claude Code, MCP tool calls hit the same PreToolUse hook Shepherd already intercepts (cc-approve.sh), arriving as tool names of the form `mcp__<server>__<tool>`. Shepherd's gate is purely tool-name + summary driven: `policies.patterns.autoDeny`/`autoAllow` are glob patterns matched against the tool, and `resolveGateTools` (cc-core.lua) + the per-session `cc-gate-tools/<key>` override already implement least-privilege "this session may only touch these tools." An "allow only registered MCP servers, block/alert on the rest" policy is structurally the same as autoDeny on `mcp__*` minus an allowed-server allow-list — and the `on_violation: block|alert` semantics map cleanly onto Shepherd's existing deny-vs-route-to-panel split, with violations landing in the JSONL ledger and feeding the per-session risk score. It also directly serves the user's stated MCP-management interest in a way that fits the local single-user model: an MCP server allow-list per session/project, surfaced as a guardrail, is exactly "leverage MCPs better" without becoming a proxy.
- **Against:** The candidate as specified is mostly proxy machinery. mcp_security diffs requested server URLs against a gateway's registered server set — Shepherd has no gateway, no registered-server registry, and no view of MCP server URLs (MCP transport config lives in Claude Code's own settings, not in Shepherd). And the entire mcp_end_user_permission half is irreducibly multi-tenant: it resolves a network caller's `end_user_id` to an `object_permission.mcp_servers`/`access_groups` set and strips the served tool list per user. Shepherd is single-user with no end_user_id, no served tool list, and no concept of "access groups" — that half is pure DONT_ADD scope (multi-user/proxy). There's also a real ceiling: Shepherd only sees a tool call at PreToolUse time; it can deny the call, but it cannot pre-filter the tool list a session sees at connect time the way a proxy can, so the "filter the list" framing can't be reproduced — only "deny on use."
- **Ruling:** Split the candidate and the verdict is obvious. The end-user/object-permission half is DONT_ADD (multi-tenant, no end_user_id, network-served tool list) and is dropped. But the core need — constrain which MCP servers a local session may actually invoke, and block or alert on the rest — is real, fits the user's high-interest MCP-management lane, and is an extension of an existing Shepherd primitive rather than a new subsystem. It reshapes onto the tool-name gate: treat `mcp__<server>__*` as gated, carry an allowed-MCP-server allow-list (fleet default + per-session override, mirroring resolveGateTools / cc-gate-tools), and autoDeny calls to unlisted servers (block) or route-to-panel/ledger-only (alert). That is the textbook ADAPT shape: real need, must be rebuilt on sessions/hooks/gate/ledger, extends gate.tools + policies.patterns. Confidence is high because the tool-name gate already does 90% of the mechanism; the only new pieces are server-name extraction from the `mcp__` prefix and an allow-list config key. Not ADD, because the LiteLLM implementation (URL diffing + object_permission) cannot be copied as-is; not DONT_ADD, because unlike a pure proxy guardrail this one has a clean local-session expression and the user explicitly wants MCP governance.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend the PreToolUse gate, not a new system. (1) cc-approve.sh: it already matches on `.tool_name`; add recognition of the `mcp__<server>__<tool>` prefix to extract the server name, and add a policy that allows servers in an allow-list and autoDenies (block) or routes-to-panel (alert) the rest — reuse the existing pattern_match/match_patterns + emit_deny path and ledger_decision provenance (new `by: mcpSecurity`). (2) cc-core.lua: add a pure `resolveMcpAllow(override, fleetDefault)` mirroring `resolveGateTools` (precedence: per-session cc-gate-tools-style override > fleet `mcp.allowedServers` config > built-in), unit-tested; keep it KEEP-IN-SYNC with the shell hot path like the gate.tools twin. (3) cc-config: new `mcp.allowedServers` + `policies.mcpSecurity.{enabled,onViolation:block|alert}` keys, panel-editable in Settings alongside gate.tools. (4) Ledger/risk: MCP-block events flow through the existing JSONL ledger and per-session risk score for free. Explicitly DROP the end_user_id/object_permission/access-group half (out of scope: single-user, no served tool list).
- **Skeptic check:** Refutation attempted and FAILED; ADAPT (high) survives. I verified the codebase against the judge's claims. (1) The end-user/object_permission half is correctly DROPPED — it needs end_user_id + a network-served per-user tool list = multi-tenant proxy, DONT_ADD; nothing in Shepherd. (2) The core "constrain which MCP servers a local session may invoke; block/alert on the rest" half is a genuine, bounded extension of an existing primitive — not "already have it" and not multi-tenant. Evidence: grep for "mcp" across cc-core.lua, cc-approve.sh, all hooks, README, CHANGELOG, todos, context.md returns ZERO hits — Shepherd has no MCP awareness today, so this is not a re-add. The gate IS real: cc-approve.sh matches on .tool_name against a flat allow-list (lines 97-100) and pattern_match does exact tool-name equality (lines 54/58); resolveGateTools (cc-core.lua:2537) has exactly the override>provider>fleet>default precedence the judge cited, with a panel-editable gate.tools and per-session cc-gate-tools overrides. MCP tools already reach PreToolUse as mcp__<server>__<tool>, so routing/alerting on them and autoDenying specific MCP tools works TODAY with no new code — confirming the judge's ~90%-reuse claim for the alert half. ONE correction to the judge (does not change the verdict): the "block unregistered server" semantic is default-deny-the-namespace, which Shepherd genuinely lacks — its gate is default-route-to-human and autoDeny is enumerate-the-bad, never deny-the-unknown (confirmed: no default-deny/allow-list-the-rest/"registered" primitive anywhere). So it's slightly more than the judge's "just an allow-list config key" — it needs a new evaluation MODE in the policy loop plus a resolveMcpAllow twin + mcp.allowedServers/policies.mcpSecurity keys. But that is still a bounded extension of the gate/ledger/config, not a new subsystem, which is the textbook ADAPT shape and exactly why it is neither a copy-as-is ADD nor a DONT_ADD. Fits the single-user local model once the end_user half is dropped, and lands squarely in the user's explicitly high-interest MCP-management lane. Verdict holds at ADAPT.

#### Guardrail lifecycle (CRUD) with event-hook timing + default-on/opt-in + applied-guardrails transparency  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The underlying need — operator-defined, timed, transparent allow/deny rules around tool calls — is real and Shepherd already half-ships it, so completing the structure is a natural extension the user explicitly wants ("we haven't leveraged policies/profiles well enough"). LiteLLM's three ideas map cleanly onto Shepherd primitives: (1) event-hook TIMING is exactly Shepherd's hook chain — PreToolUse→cc-approve.sh is pre_call, PostToolUse is post_call, and the append-only ledger IS the logging_only/observe-never-block tier; (2) BLOCK/FLAG already exist (gate deny = BLOCK, risk score + ledger marks = FLAG); (3) applied-guardrails transparency already exists as the ledger `by:` provenance plus gateDecisionSummary ("deny Bash ×4 (autoDeny: Bash(rm*))") and the 🔔 notification history — that is the "which guardrail fired" echo, just rendered in the panel instead of an HTTP header. The one genuinely net-new, worthwhile idea is NAMING the currently-anonymous policy globs into individually-toggleable guardrail objects and letting the per-session cc-gate-tools override reference a named policy — a small UX/config refinement that makes the existing gate far more legible and reusable (a saved "agent profile" could carry a named guardrail set, tying into the stated agents-registry interest).
- **Against:** Most of this feature is pure multi-tenant-proxy plumbing that Shepherd either already has or must reject by scope. CRUD-over-an-HTTP-endpoint (POST/PUT/DELETE /guardrails) and a Prisma LiteLLM_GuardrailsTable are server concerns; Shepherd's equivalent is a hand/panel-edited cc-config.json with no DB and no API. MASK is meaningless here — Shepherd never sees or transforms request/response bodies; it gates on tool name + a command summary, so two of the three guardrail actions don't even apply. default_on (always-runs guardrails) is actively anti-scope: Shepherd's hard rule is everything-off-by-default, so a "default-on guardrail" inverts the product's core safety stance. during_call has no analog in a hook model. The response-header echo is a proxy affordance. So the redundant-or-out-of-scope surface dwarfs the one small useful idea — arguably this is "we already have it" (the gate + policies.patterns) and not worth the churn.
- **Ruling:** ADAPT wins over both DONT_ADD and ADD. It's not DONT_ADD because the core need is legitimate AND already partially built, and the user explicitly wants better-leveraged policy/agent structure — naming the policies is a real improvement, not redundant churn. It's not ADD because the feature as-specified (HTTP CRUD, DB table, MASK action, default_on, response header, during_call) is mostly proxy plumbing or directly anti-scope (default-on violates Shepherd's off-by-default contract; MASK/during_call have no analog since Shepherd never touches bodies). What survives the scope filter is a narrow, valuable reshaping: turn the flat anonymous policies.patterns.autoAllow/autoDeny lists into NAMED guardrail entries, each optionally pinned to a tier (pre = gate deny/allow at cc-approve.sh; observe = ledger-only FLAG via the existing risk/notification path), keep them all opt-in, and let a session's gate-tools override or a saved agent profile reference a named set. Transparency is already solved by the ledger provenance + gateDecisionSummary, so no new echo mechanism is needed. The net-new build is the naming/structure + a panel toggle list, riding entirely on existing primitives — a textbook ADAPT.
- **How (Shepherd):** Reshape policies.patterns in cc-config.json from two anonymous glob arrays into a named list, e.g. policies.guardrails: [ { name, action: deny|allow|flag, tier: pre|observe, tools/globs, enabled } ]. The `pre` tier evaluates in cc-approve.sh exactly where match_patterns() already runs (autoDeny→deny first, autoAllow→allow), passing the guardrail `name` through to ledger_decision as the `pattern`/`by` provenance — which gateDecisionSummary (cc-core.lua) already renders as "which fired". The `flag/observe` tier needs no blocking path: it rides the existing ledger + risk score (RISK_WEIGHTS) as an indicator only. Per-session selection reuses the existing per-session override mechanism (cc-gate-tools/<key>, resolveGateTools in cc-core.lua) extended to name a guardrail set; a saved agent/spawn profile (the stated agents-registry interest, providers/spawn block) could carry a default named set. Keep ALL guardrails opt-in (do NOT port default_on — it contradicts the off-by-default scope limit). Drop MASK, during_call, the HTTP CRUD endpoints, the DB table, and the response header entirely — non-applicable to a bodiless, single-user, file-config console. Panel edit lives in the existing ⚙ Settings policies UI.
- **Skeptic check:** The ADAPT ruling survives both skepticism filters. (a) Not a proxy-in-disguise: the judge explicitly drops the proxy machinery — HTTP CRUD, DB table, MASK, during_call, response-header echo, and default_on. MASK/during_call have no analog because Shepherd is a PreToolUse permission gate, not a body-touching proxy (cc-approve.sh emit_allow/emit_deny only return permissionDecision). default_on is correctly refused as it violates the verified off-by-default contract (context.md; Settings UI dashboard:3219 'keeps all three OFF'). (b) Not already present: patterns today are flat ANONYMOUS arrays policies.patterns.autoAllow/autoDeny (cc-config.example.json:167-168; two plain textareas at dashboard:3225/3228). No 'named guardrail' concept exists anywhere in code/README/CHANGELOG/todos. Naming each rule + pinning WHEN it runs (pre=block / observe=FLAG) is genuinely net-new structure. And it does NOT collapse to DONT_ADD because it rides verified existing primitives: match_patterns() already passes a 'by'+'pattern' label into ledger_decision (cc-approve.sh:145-164); gateDecisionSummary groups on by+pattern (cc-core.lua:752) and the panel already renders r.by + ': ' + r.pattern (dashboard:4215) — so the 'which fired' transparency is solved WITHOUT the spec's response header, confirming the judge's no-new-echo claim. Per-session selection reuses resolveGateTools + cc-gate-tools/<key> (cc-core.lua:2537, dashboard:1939). The net build (naming/structure + a panel toggle list in the existing policies UI) is a true reshaping of existing primitives — more than DONT_ADD, less than ADD. Could not refute; ADAPT holds.

#### Named safety policies (versioned, scoped guardrail bundles with ordered pipeline + compliance templates)  `[H/M]`
- **For:** The underlying need is real and Shepherd already half-has it. cc-config.json has a `policies` namespace (`policies.patterns.autoAllow[]`/`autoDeny[]`, `policies.autopilot`, `policies.approveRepeats`) and cc-approve.sh already runs an ORDERED pipeline with terminal outcomes (autoDeny=deny → autopilot=allow → autoAllow=allow → approveRepeats=allow → else human panel), where pattern_match() supports `Tool` and `Tool(glob)` matching on the command/summary. That is structurally the candidate's "ordered guardrail pipeline where each step has on_pass/on_fail/next actions." What it lacks is the obvious next step: those patterns are a single flat fleet-wide list (plus a per-session gate.tools override). The user's stated interest is exactly "pre-configured agents/profiles saved into a directory" — and a NAMED, REUSABLE guardrail bundle that you attach to a project/group/provider-profile is the policy analogue of a saved agent profile. A "spawn-this-agent-with-this-safety-bundle" story fits the local session model cleanly: e.g. attach a tight bundle (Bash(rm*) autoDeny, MCP-unregistered-block) to a project, a looser one to your scratch group. Versioning-lite (a couple of named bundles you can switch between and revert to) and a few starter templates (a "read-only" bundle, a "no-network" bundle) genuinely reduce the per-session config friction Shepherd has today.
- **Against:** Most of the candidate's machinery is multi-tenant-proxy scope that has no referent in Shepherd. The attachment axes — teams[], keys[], models[], tags[] with wildcards — are proxy primitives: Shepherd has no teams, owns no API keys (it never stores secrets; it only names env vars the spawned shell expands), and serves no model deployments. The versioning lifecycle (draft→published→production, parent_version_id, is_latest, update_version_status legal-transition enforcement, rollback) is a database/governance workflow for many editors sharing one control plane — overkill for a single local user editing one JSON file under version control already. modify_response with data-forwarding and LLM enrichment requires being an inline proxy that sees and rewrites response bodies; Shepherd is explicitly NOT a proxy and never makes API calls, so on_pass/modify_response is unbuildable here. The compliance template catalog (GDPR, EU AI Act Art.5, regional PII, NSFW, aviation ops) is content for a hosted product subject to regulation; a local single-user Claude Code console has no legal-attestation surface and no PII pipeline to filter — shipping "dozens of compliance templates" would be cargo-cult scope creep. Building it close to as-is means a whole PolicyRegistry/Matcher/Resolver/version-state-machine subsystem to wrap what is currently ~40 lines of pattern matching.
- **Ruling:** ADAPT, not ADD and not DONT_ADD. Not DONT_ADD because the core idea — guardrails as a named, reusable, scopable bundle rather than one flat global list — is a genuine improvement that extends a feature Shepherd ALREADY has (policies.patterns + the ordered approve pipeline) and lands squarely on the user's saved-profile interest. Not ADD because the LiteLLM shape (teams/keys/models/tags attachment, draft/published/production version DB with legal-transition enforcement, modify_response, compliance packs) is built for a multi-tenant HTTP proxy and most of it has no local single-user referent. The right move is to reshape to Shepherd's real primitives: turn `policies.patterns` into a small set of NAMED bundles (each = its own autoAllow/autoDeny/gate.tools set), and let a bundle be ATTACHED by Shepherd's actual axes — project path, session group, provider profile, or session — instead of teams/keys/models/tags. Keep the existing deterministic ordered pipeline (it's already pure-tested logic), add resolution that merges the attached bundle into the effective per-session gate set (a natural extension of resolveGateTools' precedence chain), and ship maybe two or three starter bundles (read-only, no-Bash, MCP-only). Drop the version state machine (git already versions the config; at most offer "active bundle" switching), drop modify_response, drop the compliance catalog. That keeps everything off-by-default and within the no-proxy/no-secrets/no-multi-user limits.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend the existing `policies` namespace in cc-config.json: add `policies.bundles` = { name -> { autoAllow[], autoDeny[], gateTools, autopilot } } and `policies.attachments` = list of { match: {project|group|providerId|sessionKey (wildcards ok)}, bundle: name }. Resolution: a new pure resolver in cc-core.lua (mirror resolveGateTools' precedence — session override > attached-bundle > fleet default) computes the effective autoAllow/autoDeny/gate.tools for a session; the matcher reuses the project/group/provider context the dashboard already tracks per tile. The ordered evaluation stays exactly where it is (cc-approve.sh's autoDeny→autopilot→autoAllow→approveRepeats pipeline) — it just reads the resolved bundle instead of the flat lists; KEEP IN SYNC contract between cc-core.lua and cc-approve.sh as the existing gate-tools override already does. Surface bundle name + a "pattern source" line in the per-session gate-decision summary (gateDecisionSummary) and let Settings pick the active bundle per project/group. No version table, no modify_response, no teams/keys/models/tags, no compliance templates.
- **Skeptic check:** I tried to refute the ADAPT and could not. Checked both standard error modes against the code. (a) Proxy-dressed-as-local? The LiteLLM SHAPE is pure multi-tenant proxy and the judge already stripped all of it: attach-by-teams/keys/models/tags (Shepherd has no tenants), a draft->published->production version DB with legal-transition enforcement (git already versions cc-config.json), modify_response/data-forwarding (Shepherd's cc-approve.sh only sees PreToolUse decisions — it never sees or rewrites model responses, so modify_response has zero local referent), and the GDPR/EU-AI-Act/NSFW/regional-PII compliance catalog (serving-many-users concern). The reshaped feature carries NONE of that, so error (a) does not apply to what would actually be added. (b) Already has it? This is the real test, and the residual survives it. Shepherd today has only a SINGLE FLAT GLOBAL list: policies.patterns = one {enabled, autoAllow[], autoDeny[]} (cc-config.example.json:159-170) and gate.tools = one fleet string with a per-SESSION override file (resolveGateTools, cc-core.lua:2537). There is NO concept of a named, reusable gate/pattern bundle, and NO way to attach a different guardrail set per project / session-group / provider — the override layer is per-individual-session only, and groups (cc-core.lua:341+) currently drive bulk actions/filtering, not gate resolution. So 'named bundle attachable by Shepherd's real axes' is genuinely absent, not a re-add. The kept core is a true LOCAL improvement and lands on the user's stated saved-profile / 'pre-configured agents we hand work off to' interest: turn the flat policies.patterns into a few NAMED bundles (each its own autoAllow/autoDeny/gateTools set), attach them by project/group/provider (axes the dashboard already tracks per tile), and add ONE pure resolver mirroring resolveGateTools' precedence (session > attached-bundle > fleet default) that feeds the EXISTING ordered pipeline (cc-approve.sh autoDeny->autopilot->autoAllow->approveRepeats, lines 166-196 — already pure-tested) unchanged, plus surface the bundle name in gateDecisionSummary (cc-core.lua:752, which already carries by/pattern provenance). Ship 2-3 starter bundles (read-only, no-Bash, MCP-only). Stays off-by-default and inside the no-proxy/no-secrets/no-multi-user limits, preserving the cc-core.lua<->cc-approve.sh KEEP-IN-SYNC contract that the gate-tools override already follows. It is correctly ADAPT, not ADD (the literal LiteLLM feature is mostly proxy machinery with no local meaning) and not DONT_ADD (the named/scopable-bundle idea is a real extension of an existing feature, not something Shepherd already has). The judge's downgrade of the version state machine, modify_response, tenant-attachment, and compliance packs is exactly right. Verdict holds at ADAPT.

#### Agentic loop cap (max iterations / max budget per session)  `[H/M]`
- **For:** A runaway agent that loops forever is exactly the failure Shepherd's "everything-automating-is-bounded-and-off-by-default" philosophy exists to prevent. The conceptual need — "cap how many times an automated thing fires per session before it stops" — is real and load-bearing here, and it maps onto the user's stated interest in handing work off to saved/reusable agents: if you spawn-and-walk-away from an agent profile, you want a per-session ceiling (iterations and/or a wall-clock/turn budget) so it can't churn unattended. There's a concrete extension point: Shepherd already tracks per-session turn counts and time-blocked in the ledger/shift report and already enforces per-folder caps on its own auto-loops (maxRetries/maxAttempts), so a "session work cap" that watches the ledger's turn count and raises an escalation/stop when a session blows past a configured ceiling would slot in naturally as a new bounded guardrail.
- **Against:** The feature as built is pure proxy plumbing: it relies on a PRE-CALL hook that fires on every single LLM HTTP request, extracts a session_id from request metadata, increments a cache counter, and returns a 429 to abort the in-flight call. Shepherd makes zero API calls and has no interception point in a Claude Code session's agentic loop — it only reads coarse status transitions (idle/working/approval/done/error) off ~/.claude/cc-status/<id>.json, written by hooks at PreToolUse/PostToolUse boundaries, with no per-LLM-call granularity and no ability to inject a 429 mid-turn. It literally cannot count "iterations" the way LiteLLM defines them, and returning 429 to a session is not a primitive it owns. Worse, Shepherd already HAS the relevant guardrails for its OWN automation (bounded auto-respawn/auto-continue with per-folder caps, the hung/escalation watchdog, stale detection), so the "stop a runaway" need inside Shepherd's own loops is already covered — adding a session-internal iteration cap risks being redundant scope creep dressed up in proxy clothing.
- **Ruling:** The mechanism is squarely DONT_ADD (pre-call hook + 429 + per-call counter is proxy-only; Shepherd has no LLM-call interception and can't emit a 429), but the underlying need is genuine and aligns with the user's saved-agents direction, so the honest verdict is ADAPT, not a clean reject. Reshaped to Shepherd's primitives, this becomes a per-session "work budget" guardrail: watch the ledger's existing per-session turn count (and time-since-spawn), and when a session crosses a configured ceiling, raise an escalation notification and/or fire the existing Stop action — never an HTTP 429. Note Shepherd cannot see a session's internal agentic sub-iterations (those happen inside one Claude Code turn, invisible to the filesystem status writer); the honest granularity is TURNS/elapsed/auto-action attempts, not LLM calls. It's also partly redundant: per-folder caps on Shepherd's own auto-respawn/auto-continue loops already exist, so the only NEW surface worth adding is a human-walk-away cap on a long-lived session's own turn count, tied to spawn-from-saved-agent. Off by default, like every other automation. Effort is real but bounded because the counting substrate (ledger turn counts, escalation, Stop, per-folder cap pattern) all already exists.
- **How (Shepherd):** New bounded guardrail in cc-core.lua modeled on shouldAutoRespawn/shouldAutoContinue (per-folder/per-session attempt caps, lines ~2104-2239): add a pure "shouldHaltSession" gate keyed on the session's ledger turn count (sessionLedgerSummary / filterLedger by session_id already aggregate turns) plus elapsed-since-spawn, returning an escalation + optional auto-Stop when over a configured ceiling. Surface the ceiling in cc-config.example.json (off by default, e.g. session.workCap.maxTurns / maxMinutes), wire the escalation into M.NOTIFY_TYPES and the watchdog tick in claude-dashboard.lua, and let it dispatch the existing Stop action through the serialized keystroke chokepoint. No proxy, no 429, no per-LLM-call counter — it reads the same filesystem ledger/status it already reads.
- **Skeptic check:** Verified against the code; the ADAPT verdict survives, but its "high" confidence does not — this is the borderline "really just an extension of an existing feature" case. MECHANISM IS DONT_ADD (confirmed): the LiteLLM feature is a proxy pre-call hook with a per-LLM-call counter that returns HTTP 429. Shepherd does not proxy or make API calls (context.md hard scope), intercepts no LLM calls, and cannot emit a 429. The status writer (cc-status.sh ~L188-216) records only {session_id,name,cwd,status,updated,since}+metadata — NO turn/iteration counter. And the judge's own caveat holds: a session's internal agentic sub-iterations happen inside one Claude Code turn, invisible to the filesystem writer. So a faithful port is impossible; only the reshaped "work budget" survives. LARGELY REDUNDANT (the strongest refutation): Shepherd already ships escalation.hung (cc-config L45-49) — a stuck-session watchdog that fires when a session stays "working" with no transcript growth for N minutes, i.e. the runaway/wedged-agent case. It also already has per-folder caps on auto-respawn and auto-continue (cc-core L2116-2239, the shouldAutoRespawn/shouldAutoContinue gates the judge cited as the model), plus a per-session risk score and fleet/time-blocked stats. The "runaway agent" need is mostly covered. THIN BUT REAL RESIDUAL: the one genuinely-uncovered case is a HEALTHY, productively-working long-lived session that keeps going turn-after-turn while the human is away — escalation.hung only fires on a STALL, never on sustained healthy work. A turn-count/elapsed-since-spawn ceiling that raises an escalation (and optionally fires the existing Stop) is a genuinely local, single-user console improvement and fits the saved-agents direction (a walk-away cap on spawn-from-saved-agent). It is an EXTENSION of the existing escalation/per-folder-cap pattern, not a new capability. KEY CAVEAT THE JUDGE UNDERSTATED: the only turn signal (the ledger `prompt` event, cc-status.sh L265-267) lives in the OPT-IN ledger, which is OFF by default (ledger.enabled=false). The status file has no turn count. So this cap is inert unless the ledger is enabled — exactly like the risk score ("Requires the ledger to be enabled to have data"). Not disqualifying (precedent exists), but it makes the new surface even narrower. NET: mechanism maps to nothing; need is real but mostly redundant; residual is a thin, ledger-gated extension of existing primitives. ADAPT is the correct bucket (not DONT_ADD: there IS a genuine local gap; not ADD: nothing ports faithfully and it heavily reuses existing logic). The verdict holds, but at low-to-medium confidence, not high.

#### Custom-code guardrail with sandboxed execution + dry-run/test-a-rule endpoints  `[H/M]`
- **For:** Two halves, and the second is a genuine fit. The "test-a-rule / dry-run" endpoints solve a need Shepherd actually has today: operators arm `policies.patterns.autoAllow`/`autoDeny` globs and `gate.tools` lists completely blind, and cc-approve.sh:182 literally warns in a comment that an autoAllow `Bash(ls*)` glob ALSO auto-allows `ls; rm -rf /` or `ls && curl ... | sh`. There is no way to preview what a pattern will match before it goes live on the (off-by-default, but consequential) auto-deny/auto-allow path. The LiteLLM apply_guardrail/test endpoints are exactly the right ergonomic: feed sample input, see whether the rule would block/allow/transform, BEFORE arming it. The matching logic already exists as the pure-ish bash `pattern_match`; lifting it into a tested `core.patternMatch(tool, summary, pattern)` helper and exposing a Settings "test this rule against a sample command" box (and a blocked-words/list validator) is a small, in-scope extension of a primitive Shepherd already owns. The user also explicitly wants safer reusable config, and "validate before arming" reduces the foot-guns the existing security comment flags.
- **Against:** The headline feature — operator-supplied Python compiled with compile_restricted and run in a RestrictedPython sandbox on the request path — is a textbook scope mismatch on multiple axes at once. (1) There IS no request path: Shepherd does not proxy, does not make API calls, and never sees prompt/response content; its only "guardrail" surface is a PreToolUse tool-name+command gate reading local JSON. A content-mutating guardrail (mask/block/flag text) has nothing to act on. (2) Wrong runtime: Shepherd is Lua + POSIX shell on macOS, not a Python execution host; standing up a sandboxed Python interpreter is a whole new dependency and attack surface in a single-user app that deliberately keeps jq as its only hard dep. (3) It is explicitly a DECLINED direction: the audit-ledger memory records "sandboxes/isolation" under "Explicitly declined (fight the 'console not container' design)." A sandbox for executing untrusted code is the multi-tenant-proxy concern (protecting a shared server from tenant-supplied logic) — Shepherd is one trusted user on their own machine, so RestrictedPython buys nothing here. Even the test endpoints, as specced, exist to validate that sandboxed-Python-and-blocked-words pipeline, which Shepherd does not run.
- **Ruling:** Split the bundle and the verdict is clear. The sandboxed custom-code guardrail is DONT_ADD on every Shepherd axis — no request path to mediate, wrong language runtime, and "sandboxes/isolation" is a memory-documented declined direction ("console not container"). That half dies. But the rubric routes to ADAPT when the underlying NEED is real OR the item extends a feature Shepherd already has, and the dry-run/test-a-rule half does BOTH: Shepherd already ships pattern-based auto-allow/auto-deny policies and a tool gate, and the codebase itself (cc-approve.sh:182) flags that operators arm those globs blind with real blast-radius risk. "Apply a rule to sample text and see what it would do, before arming it" is precisely the safety affordance that warning is begging for, reshaped onto Shepherd's actual primitive (the glob/tool policy, not Python). So: drop the sandbox entirely, keep and reshape only the test/validate-before-arm idea as an extension of the existing gate policy. That is the definition of ADAPT, not ADD (it must be reshaped to the policy primitive) and not DONT_ADD (the salvaged need is real and local).
- **How (Shepherd):** Drop the RestrictedPython sandbox and any content-mutation/masking guardrail (no request path, declined direction). Keep ONLY a "test a policy rule before arming it" affordance, built on the existing gate: (1) lift cc-approve.sh `pattern_match` into a pure, unit-tested `core.patternMatch(tool, summary, pattern)` in cc-core.lua (the existing bash twin keeps using it / stays in sync, like the other hand-mirrored twins); (2) add a Settings dry-run box near the policies/gate.tools editor in claude-dashboard.lua where you type a sample tool+command and see which of autoDeny/autopilot/autoAllow/approveRepeats would fire first (mirroring the documented policy order) and the resulting allow/deny — no session, no FX, pure preview; (3) optionally a small list-validator for the gate.tools / pattern lists (normalized via the existing core.parseToolList) flagging empty/over-broad entries, echoing the cc-approve.sh:182 footgun warning. Off-path, read-only, no new runtime deps.
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; the ADAPT survives. The feature is a bundle and the split is correct. HALF 1 (RestrictedPython sandbox + content masking/blocking guardrail) is DONT_ADD on every axis, verified: (a) no request path to mediate content — cc-approve.sh emits only allow/deny JSON, with zero content-mutation/masking primitive anywhere (the only 'redact' in cc-core.lua:610 is ledger purging); (b) wrong runtime — project is Lua+bash, `find` confirms zero .py files, so RestrictedPython is a foreign runtime + new dep against the no-new-deps posture; (c) it is a memory-documented DECLINED direction — shepherd-audit-ledger.md:33 reads verbatim 'Explicitly declined (fight the console not container design): sandboxes/isolation'. 'Operator-supplied guardrail logic armed into the request path' is precisely the multi-tenant-proxy content-filter shape (failure mode a) — caught and correctly dropped. HALF 2 (dry-run / test-a-rule-before-arming) passes both refutation tests: NOT failure mode (b) 'already have it' — I confirmed there is no policy preview anywhere; the Settings policies editor exists (claude-dashboard.lua:3772-3778: autoAllow/autoDeny/autopilot/approveRepeats/gate.tools) but offers no 'see what this rule would do' affordance, and the only existing 'dry-run' is for spawn/queue. The salvaged need is real and local: cc-approve.sh:182 carries a live SECURITY warning that an autoAllow glob like Bash(ls*) blanket-approves 'ls; rm -rf /'; operators arm these globs blind. The fix must be RESHAPED onto Shepherd's actual primitive — the glob/tool gate evaluated in order autoDeny->autopilot->autoAllow->approveRepeats (cc-approve.sh:167-196) — by lifting pattern_match (cc-approve.sh:48) into a pure core.patternMatch with a bash twin, exactly the project's established hand-mirrored-twin pattern (KEEP IN SYNC markers already in both files; resolveGateTools/parseToolList at cc-core.lua:2516-2547 are this same pattern). Reshaping = ADAPT, not ADD; the real local need = not DONT_ADD. Ruling holds at ADAPT.

#### Per-guardrail & per-policy daily usage metrics (pass/block/flag, latency, score) + AI-assisted policy authoring/test panel  `[H/M]`
- **For:** Shepherd already owns the analog of both halves, so the underlying need is real and in-scope. Its gate (gate.tools allow-list + autoAllow/autoDeny glob patterns) IS a policy/guardrail engine, and its ledger + fleetStats/shiftReport already roll up decision outcomes (allow/deny/fallback) with provenance buckets and bucketEvents time-series. The one thing genuinely missing is attribution: today a decision event records `by` (autoDeny/human/...) but NOT which specific glob/rule matched, so you can't see which rule is doing the work, which never fires (dead rule), or which autoDeny pattern is over-blocking. Adding the matched pattern to the decision event and a per-rule daily rollup is a high-value, low-cost extension of an existing feature — pure local aggregation, no network. Likewise "AI-suggested policies" and "test a policy / impact preview" map onto real operator pain: writing good autoAllow/autoDeny globs is fiddly and risky, and an LLM suggestion (via the existing Improve/audit-review paste path — no keys, no network) plus a dry-run that replays a proposed rule set against the ledger of past tool_requests would let you tune the gate safely before arming it. The user's stated interest in reusable, pre-configured policy/agent artifacts also pulls toward making the gate more authorable.
- **Against:** The literal feature is proxy-shaped and partly on Shepherd's explicitly-declined list. LiteLLM's daily metric tables (LiteLLM_DailyGuardrailMetrics/PolicyMetrics with avg_score, avg_latency_ms, a SpendLogGuardrailIndex) are a multi-tenant DB rollup of per-request inference outcomes across many users — Shepherd is single-user, has no per-request inference, and computes severity/risk at render time rather than baking metric rows (the ledger roadmap deliberately stores raw facts, not pre-aggregated tables). avg_score/avg_latency_ms are inference-pipeline concepts with no Shepherd referent. Worse, the authoring half's core capability — /policy/test running the policy through /v1/chat/completions — requires making API calls, which Shepherd categorically does NOT do (no proxy, no network, no API calls), and an "impact preview" over live completions is exactly the "in-workspace change-impact/drift analysis" the governance roadmap already DECLINED. The streaming LLM enricher and the React pipeline_flow_builder are heavy proxy-console UI for composing multi-stage inference guardrails — far beyond a keyboard-driven local tile panel. Taken whole, it's scope creep toward a proxy admin console.
- **Ruling:** The need is real and lands squarely on primitives Shepherd already has (the gate + the ledger), so it's not DONT_ADD; but the literal implementation is multi-tenant/proxy/inference-shaped and partly on the declined list, so it's not ADD. Hence ADAPT, with a sharp split. KEEP, reshaped: (1) per-rule attribution + daily rollup — tag each `decision` event with the matched autoAllow/autoDeny glob (or gate.tools entry), then add a pure aggregation in cc-core.lua that buckets decisions by rule (fired count, allow/deny split, last-fired, dead-rule flag), surfaced in the existing insights/shift-report overlay; this is a clean extension of fleetStats and the provenance model. (2) AI-suggested gate rules via the EXISTING Improve/audit-review paste path (LLM through the running Claude session — no keys, no network), proposing autoAllow/autoDeny globs from observed ledger patterns. (3) "Test a policy / impact preview" reshaped to a deterministic DRY-RUN REPLAY: run a candidate gate config against the ledger of past tool_requests and show how many would flip allow/deny/changed — pure, no API call, the local analog of the impact popover. DROP: the daily metric DB tables, avg_score/avg_latency_ms, /policy/test against /v1/chat/completions, the streaming enricher, and the React pipeline-flow-builder (proxy/inference-only, and the live impact analysis is already declined). Effort is M, not S, because it touches the decision-event schema in cc-approve.sh + cc-status.sh, the pure aggregation in cc-core.lua (with unit tests), and a new overlay/dry-run view in claude-dashboard.lua — but it's bounded and reuses existing paths.
- **How (Shepherd):** Gate engine = the "policy/guardrail" (cc-approve.sh autoAllow/autoDeny globs + gate.tools / M.resolveGateTools + M.DEFAULT_GATE_TOOLS in cc-core.lua). (1) Per-rule metrics: add matched-rule field to the `decision` event written by cc-approve.sh (the gate branch already knows which pattern fired — it IS the provenance); new pure aggregator in cc-core.lua alongside M.fleetStats/M.bucketEvents that rolls up decisions by rule (fired/allow/deny/dead-rule), rendered in the existing fleet-insights / shift-report overlay in claude-dashboard.lua. (2) AI-suggested rules: reuse the existing auditReviewPrompt -> pasteIntoWindow path (no keys/network) to propose globs from ledger patterns. (3) Dry-run impact preview: pure replay of a candidate gate config over FX.readLedger() tool_request events in cc-core.lua, shown as a before/after diff in the Settings/gate overlay. Explicitly NOT mapped: daily metric DB tables, avg_score/avg_latency_ms, /policy/test via /v1/chat/completions, streaming enricher, React pipeline_flow_builder (proxy/inference-only; live impact analysis already on the declined list).
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; ADAPT survives. I verified the codebase against the ruling. The literal LiteLLM feature is proxy/inference-shaped and correctly DROPPED: avg_score/avg_latency_ms, daily metric DB tables, /policy/test against /v1/chat/completions, the streaming enricher, and the React pipeline-flow-builder all presuppose YOU run inference and serve many policies over HTTP — Shepherd makes zero API calls and stores no inference (HARD SCOPE: no network server, no proxy). So this is not a literal ADD. But it is NOT DONT_ADD either, because a real, local, absent kernel survives the reshape, and it maps onto primitives I confirmed exist: the gate (cc-approve.sh autoAllow/autoDeny globs) and the JSONL ledger. Key finding refining the ruling: the `decision` ledger event ALREADY carries the matched rule — ledger_decision() in cc-approve.sh writes `pattern:$pat` (lines 127-135), and core.gateDecisionSummary (cc-core.lua:752) ALREADY groups decisions per-session by {tool,outcome,by,pattern}. So the judge's claim (1) to 'add a matched-rule field' over-claims novelty — that attribution is already present. What is genuinely NEW and worth building: a FLEET-WIDE per-rule rollup with a dead-rule flag — fleetStats (cc-core.lua:954) only buckets decisions by ACTOR (provenance: autoAllow/autoDeny/human), never by the individual glob, and nothing flags a rule that has never fired. That is a clean pure extension of fleetStats/gateDecisionSummary surfaced in the existing fleet-insights/shift-report overlay. (2) AI-suggested gate rules reuses a path I verified real (improvePrompt -> FX.pasteIntoWindow / auditReviewPrompt, dashboard ~line 1004-1056): LLM via the running session, no keys, no network — and lands on the user's stated interest in reusable agent/policy definitions. (3) A dry-run replay of a candidate gate config over past tool_request ledger events is genuinely absent (every 'dry-run' hit in the tree is spawn/queue, not gate-config) and is the deterministic local analog of the proxy's impact preview. Net: most of the literal feature is proxy/inference (drop), one sub-claim is already-have-it (the pattern field — narrows the work), but the surviving kernel is real, local, single-user, and not present today. That is precisely ADAPT — keep a reshaped kernel, drop the proxy core. It neither collapses to DONT_ADD nor rises to literal ADD. Verdict holds. One caveat I could not confirm: the ruling's assertion that 'live impact analysis is already on the declined list' — I found no such entry in context.md/todos.md (only spawn/queue dry-run and unrelated declines), so that specific justification is unverified, but it does not affect the verdict since the dry-run replay is deterministic/local regardless.

#### Global tool registry with auto-discovery + trust/taint policy guardrail (trusted/untrusted/blocked)  `[M/M]`
- **For:** Two genuinely real needs are bundled here, and both map onto things Shepherd already does or wants. (1) The auto-discovered tool registry — a cumulative list of every tool the fleet actually calls, with call_count/first-seen/last_used — is exactly the kind of "I haven't leveraged tools/MCPs well enough" visibility the user explicitly asked for. Shepherd ALREADY captures the raw material: cc-status.sh and cc-approve.sh both read `.tool_name` (including MCP-namespaced names like mcp__playwright__browser_click), and the ledger already records per-tool `tool`/`outcome`/`summary` events and rolls up `toolRequests` in fleet insights. Aggregating those ledger events into a "tools seen across the fleet" panel (name, count, last_used, which projects) is a small, natural extension — the data is already on disk. It would also directly inform the gate.tools allow-lists the user maintains by hand today, surfacing the long tail of MCP tools nobody realized were being called. (2) The taint-chain guardrail attacks the lethal-trifecta / indirect-prompt-injection problem (untrusted tool output → privileged tool call), which is a real security concern for autonomous Claude Code sessions, and Shepherd already owns the exact enforcement seam: a PreToolUse gate (cc-approve.sh) that can deny a tool by name with a reason. A per-tool trust classification feeding an auto-deny rule is conceptually a richer gate policy.
- **Against:** The feature as specified is built for a multi-tenant HTTP proxy and most of its machinery is dead weight or impossible locally. The schema (LiteLLM_ToolTable, per-team/per-key policy overrides, first-seen team/key, object_permission_ids) is pure proxy RBAC — Shepherd is explicitly single-user with no teams, no keys, no DB; that entire dimension collapses to nothing. The PATCH-policy admin API and browsable per-tool usage-log endpoints are HTTP-server surface Shepherd has no business growing. Most damning: the taint-chain guardrail's load-bearing input is `output_policy=untrusted` tool OUTPUT already present in the conversation — but Shepherd's hooks never see tool output. cc-approve.sh is PreToolUse + input-only and matches by tool name; cc-status.sh treats posttooluse purely as a "back to working" state transition and reads no `tool_response`. Shepherd has no conversation-content model at all (it tracks status tiles, not transcripts-as-state), so it cannot evaluate "has any untrusted-output tool run earlier in THIS conversation." Re-creating that would mean parsing each session's transcript on every PreToolUse in a shell hot path — heavy, fragile, and against the deterministic-core design. And the trifecta defense is arguably Claude Code's / the model's job, not a status console's. The honest core that fits is just the read-only registry view; the enforcement half is a scope-and-architecture mismatch.
- **Ruling:** Split the feature and keep the half that fits. The TAINT-CHAIN GUARDRAIL is DONT_ADD-grade on its own: it depends on inspecting tool output and conversation history, which Shepherd's PreToolUse/status hooks structurally do not capture, and its team/key policy layer is multi-tenant proxy scope. Reconstructing transcript-as-state in a shell gate is the wrong altitude and contradicts the keep-it-deterministic core. But the TOOL REGISTRY half is a real, user-requested win that Shepherd is unusually well-positioned to deliver cheaply, because the ledger already records per-tool events and insights already aggregates toolRequests — so this is "extend a feature Shepherd already has," the textbook ADAPT case. Reshape to Shepherd primitives: derive the registry by aggregating existing ledger `decision`/`tool_request` events (and optionally widen cc-status.sh to emit a lightweight tool_seen ledger line on posttooluse so non-gated/MCP tools also appear) into a fleet "Tools seen" rollup (name, origin tier, count, last_used, projects) computed in cc-core.lua and shown in an overlay next to insights. The lightweight policy angle that DOES fit is letting an admin mark a discovered tool trusted/blocked and have BLOCKED feed straight into the existing gate.tools / autoDeny allow-list — a name-only, input-side decision the gate can already enforce — while explicitly dropping the output-taint chain as out of scope. That gives the user the saved-tools/MCP visibility they want and a one-click "block this tool fleet-wide" without pretending to be a proxy or a conversation-content analyzer. Confidence is medium because the registry-from-ledger piece is clearly sound, but the value of the trust-tier UI hinges on the user actually wanting to curate tool policy by hand, and the strongest part of the original (the trifecta guardrail) is the part being cut.
- **How (Shepherd):** Registry: aggregate existing ledger events (cc-core.lua filterLedger over `decision`+`tool_request` types; mirrors the gateDecisionSummary/insights toolRequests rollup) into a new pure builder e.g. M.toolRegistry(events) returning {name, count, lastTs, projects, firstSeen}; surface in a fleet "Tools" overlay in claude-dashboard.lua beside the insights/shift-report views. Optionally extend cc-status.sh posttooluse arm to append a minimal `tool_seen` ledger line so non-gated + MCP tools (mcp__*) are discovered, gated behind the existing opt-in ledger flag. Policy: a per-tool trusted/blocked tier persisted in cc-config (like provider profiles), where `blocked` is folded into M.resolveGateTools / the autoDeny glob set so cc-approve.sh refuses it by name — reusing the existing nonce-bound headless gate, NOT a new API. Explicitly OUT of scope: the output_policy/taint-chain guardrail (no tool-output or conversation-content visibility in the hooks) and all per-team/per-key overrides (single-user).
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute; the ADAPT ruling survives because the judge already excised the two parts that would have justified a downgrade, and what remains is a real local win Shepherd doesn't have. Verified against the code: (1) The taint-chain guardrail is correctly cut. cc-status.sh and cc-lib.sh capture NO tool output — the posttooluse arm reads only .tool_name (cc-status.sh:119-123), and grep for tool_response/tool_output/stdout/conversation across the hooks returns nothing. The status file and ledger carry tool name + input-summary only. So output_policy/taint detection (and the per-team/per-key layer = multi-tenant proxy scope) have no data source and are rightly out of scope; keeping them would be the (a) proxy-dressed-as-local error. (2) The registry half is NOT already present — failure mode (b) does not apply. grep for mcp/registry/saved-agent/skills across claude-dashboard.lua, context.md, todos.md, README found nothing. The closest existing primitives are aggregates of GATED decisions only: insights.toolRequests is a counter (cc-core.lua:981) and gateDecisionSummary groups decision events by e.tool (cc-core.lua:752-779). A discovery registry over ALL tools (incl. non-gated Read/Grep and mcp__* MCP tools) is a new view, so this is the textbook 'extend a feature it already has' ADAPT, and it directly serves the user's stated interest in MCP/skills/saved-agent visibility. (3) The local-fit pieces are confirmed feasible: posttooluse IS wired and sees .tool_name including mcp__* (so the proposed opt-in tool_seen ledger line works under the existing ledger flag), tool_request/decision events already carry the tool name for a registry rollup, and pattern_match matches on bare tool name (cc-approve.sh:58) with autoDeny running first and always winning (cc-approve.sh:167) — so a 'blocked' tier folding into autoDeny by name is a clean, name-only, input-side decision the existing nonce-bound gate already enforces, no new API. Net: drop the output-taint chain and team/key overrides (done), keep registry-from-ledger + one-click fleet-wide block. ADAPT holds.

#### Content filter guardrail: PII detection + masking/blocking + secret/hide-secrets detection  `[M/M]`
- **For:** Shepherd already owns a per-tool-request gate (cc-approve.sh) that matches tool calls against autoDeny/autoAllow patterns, and an audit ledger that captures last_prompt, Bash commands, file paths and tool_input. Two real local needs map cleanly onto the content-filter's NEED: (1) the existing gate only does shell-GLOB matching on the command summary (pattern_match), with a documented caveat that globs are loose (`Bash(ls*)` also matches `ls; rm -rf /`); a content/regex-shaped autoDeny that BLOCKs a gated request when its command/prompt contains a credential or PII shape (an AWS/GitHub/Slack token, an SSN/card) is a genuine safety upgrade — it catches `echo $AWS_SECRET`, `curl -H 'Authorization: Bearer …'`, or a prompt pasting a key, before the human eyeballs it. (2) The ledger is the one place Shepherd persists raw command/prompt text to disk; a write-time MASK pass (redact credential-shaped substrings to a [TOKEN_REDACTED] token before append, complementing the existing manual per-row redact) directly hardens Shepherd's own local footprint. Both extend shipped primitives rather than inventing a subsystem, and guardrails/policy is squarely in Shepherd's stated governance direction.
- **Against:** The feature as specified is request/response content filtering on the model traffic path — and Shepherd HAS no such path: it is not a proxy, makes no API calls, and the LiteLLM headline use ("redact/block secrets before they reach the model or logs") presupposes the proxy sits between user and model. The bulk of the feature (category YAMLs for profanity/self-harm/child-safety/bias, severity thresholds, blocked-words lists, conditional identifier-word categories, phrase_patterns) is content-moderation policy aimed at multi-tenant end-user-facing chat — irrelevant to a single trusted operator driving their own Claude Code sessions; Claude already refuses harmful content upstream, so Shepherd re-running a profanity/self-harm classifier on its operator's own prompts is pure scope creep. The secret-DETECTION angle also rubs against a HARD scope limit: Shepherd deliberately never stores or handles secret VALUES (env injection marks `secret` fields so the spawned shell expands `$VAR`, keeping the value out of Shepherd entirely); building a secret-scanner invites pulling secret material INTO Shepherd's process to scan it. And MASK-vs-BLOCK on the gate is awkward: Shepherd's gate emits a binary allow/deny PreToolUse decision — it cannot rewrite a tool_input to redact-and-proceed, so "MASK" has no faithful home on the hot path.
- **Ruling:** DONT_ADD for the feature as built (multi-tenant moderation + response filtering on a model path Shepherd doesn't have), but ADAPT wins because a thin, real slice of the NEED maps onto two existing primitives. Concretely: (a) extend the gate with regex-shaped autoDeny entries (credential/PII patterns) so a gated Bash/Write whose command or arg matches a credential shape is BLOCKED before a human even sees it — a small generalization of pattern_match from glob-only to "glob OR /regex/", keeping deny-first ordering and the binary allow/deny contract (no MASK on the hot path — masking a tool_input would silently alter what the agent runs, a foot-gun). (b) Add a write-time redaction pass in the ledger writers (cc-lib.sh cc_ledger_append + cc-core, mirroring the existing manual redact) that masks credential-shaped substrings in summary/last_prompt/command before the JSONL append, so Shepherd's own local store never accumulates raw tokens. Both are off-by-default, deterministic (testable in cc-core), and degrade gracefully. The category/severity/moderation machinery, the YAML phrase_patterns, and any "scan before the model" framing are DROPPED — they don't fit a local single-user console with no proxy and an explicit no-secrets-storage rule. So: reshape to gate-regex-deny + ledger-write-redaction, discard the rest.
- **How (Shepherd):** Two seams. (1) Gate regex deny: generalize pattern_match() in cc-approve.sh and the policy eval in cc-core.lua so policies.patterns.autoDeny accepts a /regex/ form matched against TOOL + SUMMARY (the Bash command / file_path / url already assembled at cc-approve.sh:104-116), firing the existing emit_deny + ledger_decision deny path; ship a small built-in credential/PII pattern set (AWS/GitHub/Slack token shapes, SSN, card, IBAN) togglable in Settings, default off, deny-only (no MASK). (2) Ledger write-time redaction: a pure core.redactSecrets(text) -> masked applied in cc-lib.sh's cc_ledger_append and the cc-core narrative/redact code (alongside M.ledgerDayIsPast / the per-row redact near cc-core.lua line 657), with a configurable token format, complementing the manual 📜 overlay redact. Pure logic unit-tested in tests/core.test.lua; no secret VALUES are ever stored — only matched-and-masked.
- **Skeptic check:** The refutation attempt fails on the evidence; ADAPT holds. Both downgrade triggers come back negative. (1) NOT a disguised proxy feature. The feature AS BUILT is multi-tenant proxy moderation: "scan before the model," category/severity thresholds (profanity/self-harm/child-safety/bias), response filtering, YAML phrase_patterns. Shepherd has no model path, no proxy, no HTTP route (context.md:13 "no network, no multi-user, no secrets"; cc-approve.sh emits only permissionDecision allow|deny). The judge correctly DROPS all of that. What survives is a thin local slice mapped onto two existing primitives — not the proxy feature smuggled in. (2) NOT already present. Slice (a): cc-approve.sh:48 pattern_match() is GLOB-ONLY (Tool(glob), shell-case match at line 55). Adding a /regex/ form to autoDeny (cc-approve.sh:167, deny-first) is a real, small generalization of an existing primitive, keeping the binary allow/deny contract. MASK on the hot path is correctly dropped — the gate (emit_allow/emit_deny, cc-approve.sh:37-43) cannot rewrite tool_input, so masking would either be impossible or silently alter what the agent runs. Slice (b): the existing redact (FX.redactLedger, dashboard.lua:586; M.ledgerDayIsPast cc-core.lua:614) is MANUAL, per-row, PAST-DAYS-ONLY tombstoning from the Audit overlay — NOT a write-time automatic pass. A new core.redactSecrets() applied in cc_ledger_append (cc-lib.sh:187) is genuinely absent and addresses a real leak: the ledger already captures secret-prone text — prompt (cc-status.sh:267), last_prompt (cc-status.sh:199), and summary = Bash command/file_path/url (cc-approve.sh:104-116) — so a pasted token or `curl -H Authorization:Bearer` lands in the local JSONL today, colliding with the explicit no-secrets scope. Both slices fit a LOCAL single-user console: pure-logic, unit-testable in cc-core (tests/core.test.lua already exercises pattern/redact-adjacent logic at lines 1529-2128), off by default, deterministic, degrade gracefully (jq-guarded). The category/severity/moderation/YAML machinery is correctly discarded. Reshape to gate-regex-deny + ledger-write-redaction. ADAPT stands.

#### LLM-as-a-judge guardrail (response quality scoring)  `[M/L]`
- **For:** The underlying need — "was this Claude Code session's output actually good, or did it go off the rails?" — is real and unserved. Shepherd already has the two halves this needs: (1) a Stop hook (cc-status.sh stop -> "done") that fires the moment a session finishes a turn, the natural post-call trigger point; and (2) an established "LLM-as-governance-reviewer" pattern — core.auditReviewPrompt and core.improvePrompt already wrap content in a read-only review instruction and paste it into a live session, and the per-session risk score (core.sessionRisk, weighted criteria summing to a 0-100 banded score with thresholds) is literally the same shape as the candidate's weighted-criteria/overall_threshold scoring. A "judge the last turn against my named criteria, flag the tile if it scores low" feature would slot onto the done-edge, reuse the risk-band UI (calm/med/high indicator), and ledger the verdict. It directly advances the human's job: knowing which of N finished sessions needs a second look before you trust/ship its work, without reading every transcript.
- **Against:** The candidate as built is a PROXY guardrail: it makes its own outbound API call to a judge_model to score every response inline and can BLOCK delivery below a threshold. Shepherd explicitly does NOT proxy or make scored API calls (it makes only the one undocumented OAuth usage GET) and has no concept of "blocking a response" — the response already streamed into the user's editor by the time the Stop hook fires, so there is nothing to block; "block/flag" collapses to flag-only. The blocking half also implies a per-call judge spend on a second model for every turn across the fleet, which is a meaningful cost the local single-user console has no budget primitive for. And Shepherd's whole philosophy is to delegate LLM judgment by pasting a prompt into an existing session (no new key, no new client, "$ENV resolved by the spawned shell, never stored"), not to stand up its own scoring LLM client — doing the latter would mean storing/holding a judge API key, crossing a hard scope line. Much of the value (denial patterns, risky tool use, off-the-rails behavior) is already captured by sessionRisk + the audit-review prompt, so a full inline judge risks being redundant scope creep.
- **Ruling:** The need is genuine and maps onto interest Shepherd already invests in (governance review, risk scoring), so DONT_ADD is too harsh — but the feature as specified (inline scored API call that blocks responses) violates two hard scope limits (no proxy/scored API calls, no secrets/key storage) and "block" is meaningless post-stream. That mismatch is exactly what ADAPT is for. The honest adaptation: fire on the Stop done-edge, build a "review the last turn against these weighted criteria" prompt with core.renderNarrative/auditReviewPrompt as the template, and either (a) paste it into a fresh review session and parse a 0-100 verdict back, or (b) more cheaply, extend sessionRisk's weighted-criteria scoring to fold in a transcript-quality signal. Surface the result as a flag/band on the tile and a ledger event — never a hard block. Keep it opt-in and per-session-budgeted like every other autonomy loop. That delivers the real value (triage which finished sessions need human eyes) while staying inside Shepherd's local, key-free, paste-not-proxy primitives.
- **How (Shepherd):** Trigger: the Stop hook done-edge (cc-status.sh stop). Reuse: core.auditReviewPrompt/renderNarrative as the judge-prompt builder (add a criteria[] + threshold variant), and core.sessionRisk's existing weighted-score/band/threshold machinery (RISK_WEIGHTS/RISK_THRESHOLDS) as the scoring shape and the tile indicator. Delivery: either paste the review prompt into a fresh/idle session via FX.pasteIntoWindow (the improvePrompt path) and parse the verdict, or extend sessionRisk to ingest a transcript-quality signal — NOT a new outbound scored API client. Output: a flag/band on the tile + a ledger event (append-only JSONL); strictly flag, never block. Off by default, per-session/per-folder budget like Auto-Continue/auto-respawn. New pure logic lives in cc-core.lua.
- **Skeptic check:** The ADAPT ruling survives both downgrade traps after verification against the codebase. (a) Proxy-dressed-as-local: The feature AS SPECIFIED is the proxy version — LiteLLM's judge guardrail intercepts the response in the proxy, makes a scored API call to a judge model, and BLOCKS. That genuinely violates Shepherd's hard limits (context.md:13 "No network, no multi-user, no secrets"; README:275 keys live in the shell, never stored; Shepherd "does NOT proxy or make API calls"), and "block" is meaningless because the response already streamed into the user's editor. But the judge did NOT recommend that version — it explicitly stripped the proxy/scored-API/block semantics and re-cast it onto local primitives. So the proxy objection is the BASIS for ADAPT, not a refutation of it. I tried to push it to DONT_ADD on this ground and it doesn't hold: the underlying need (triage which finished sessions deserve human eyes) is local-console-shaped and maps cleanly. (b) Already have it: I checked. The closest existing things are sessionRisk (cc-core.lua:1371 — weighted 0-100 score with bands/thresholds, but it scores ledger-observed BEHAVIOR: deny rate, autoDeny hits, volume — not response CONTENT quality against named criteria) and the on-demand 'Review activity' path (dashboard.lua:2184-2194 — pastes core.auditReviewPrompt(renderNarrative(events)) into a session, but it reviews the ACTIVITY LOG, asks for a prose flag, and does NOT parse a 0-100 verdict back or score content). No per-turn content-quality judge exists (grep found none in README/todos). So 'already have it' does not apply to the adapted feature — it is genuinely new, while being largely an EXTENSION of these, which is exactly what ADAPT denotes (ADD->ADAPT is correct, not DONT_ADD). Every primitive the judge's mapping relies on is real and verified: the Stop->done edge (cc-status.sh:77,167-168), FX.pasteIntoWindow already used for both improvePrompt and auditReviewPrompt (dashboard.lua:1055,2189 — the paste-not-proxy pattern), transcriptSnippet reading the latest assistant response locally with zero API cost (cc-core.lua:1544), and sessionRisk's weights/caps/bands/thresholds as the scoring shape and tile indicator. The honest adaptation (fire on Stop, build a weighted-criteria review prompt, either paste-and-parse a verdict or fold a transcript-quality signal into sessionRisk, surface as a tile band + JSONL ledger event, flag-never-block, opt-in + per-session budgeted) stays entirely inside Shepherd's local, key-free, paste-not-proxy model and aligns with the stated governance/risk interest. ADAPT (medium) holds.

### Observability & Alerting

#### Paginated spend/request log browser (filter UI + per-session view + per-request drill-down)  `[H/S]`
- **For:** The underlying need — "browse a filterable history of activity, slice it to one session, and drill into a single event's full detail" — is exactly an observability primitive Shepherd already values (it built the whole audit ledger for this). LiteLLM's three sub-capabilities map cleanly onto Shepherd concepts: (1) the filter UI = filterLedger's session/projectKey/date-window/type filters already wired to the audit overlay's chips; (2) the per-session view (all logs for one session_id) = core.sessionTimeline + the overlay's "timeline" view + the detail-panel "📜 Timeline" button — session_id is already the reliable cross-writer key; (3) per-request drill-down = the prompt/tool_request events plus the transcript snippet. The one genuinely new bit worth stealing is true pagination: today capLedgerSlice just truncates a newest-first slice at ~2000 and sets a `truncated` flag, with no "load older" path — a long-lived fleet could have history the operator literally cannot reach in the overlay. Adding offset/cursor paging (and a fuller per-event expand) is a small, on-strategy extension that makes the ledger genuinely browsable, not just recent-tail.
- **Against:** This is largely redundant with what Shepherd already has, and the parts that aren't redundant are deliberately out of scope. The filter/per-session/drill-down trio is already shipped via the audit overlay, sessionTimeline, and gateDecisionSummary — re-spec'ing it as a "new feature" is double-counting. The truly novel LiteLLM elements are the wrong shape for Shepherd: it's "spend logs" (per-key/per-team/per-user billing rows over LiteLLM_SpendLogs via raw SQL), which is multi-tenant proxy accounting — Shepherd has no users/teams/keys and tracks usage only as a local context-fullness indicator, not a billing ledger. And "expand a single request's full stored messages/response" requires persisting full prompts+responses, which Shepherd explicitly DECLINED (heavy evidence capture) — it stores trimmed 200-char prompts and tool summaries on purpose; the full transcript already lives in Claude Code's own file and is reachable via transcript snippet / fleet rg-search, so storing it twice is scope creep with a privacy cost. The "list rows exclude heavy columns for performance" optimization is a SQL/DB concern that simply doesn't exist for a few JSONL files.
- **Ruling:** The for/against collapse to: the need is real AND already mostly met, with one small legitimate gap. That is the definition of ADAPT over both ADD and DONT_ADD. ADD is wrong because building it "close to as-is" means SQL-over-spend-logs with key/team/user filters and stored-message persistence — multi-tenant proxy plumbing plus the explicitly-declined heavy-capture, both off-scope. DONT_ADD "already have it" is too dismissive because there IS a concrete improvement the existing overlay lacks: real pagination (load-older beyond the truncation cap) and a richer per-event expand. So the ruling is: extend the audit overlay rather than build a new browser. Reshape every LiteLLM dimension to Shepherd primitives — drop key/team/user (no such entities) and spend-as-billing (Shepherd's usage is an indicator), keep session_id/projectKey/date/type filtering (already there), and add offset/cursor pagination to capLedgerSlice + the overlay. For "request detail," reuse the transcript path rather than persisting full payloads, honoring the prior declined-capture decision.
- **How (Shepherd):** Extend the existing audit ledger, not a new subsystem. cc-core.lua: add a paged variant of capLedgerSlice (offset/cursor over the newest-first filterLedger output) returning {events, offset, hasMore} — pure, unit-tested. claude-dashboard.lua: the 📜 Audit overlay (its filter chips + "timeline" view already cover session/project/date/type) gains a "Load older" / page control driven by the new offset; the per-session view is already core.sessionTimeline behind the detail-panel "📜 Timeline" button. Per-request drill-down = expand a ledger row to its prompt/tool_request fields and link to the transcript via the existing transcriptSnippet / fleet rg-search path (do NOT persist full messages/response — respects the declined heavy-evidence-capture decision). Skip entirely: key/team/user filters and spend-as-billing (no such entities; usage tracking stays an indicator).
- **Skeptic check:** Tried to refute ADAPT in both common ways; neither sticks. (b) "already have it" overreaches: Shepherd's audit overlay (claude-dashboard.lua:3302+) already ships the session + type filter dropdowns and since/until date inputs (lines 3312-3334), a per-session view (core.sessionTimeline at cc-core.lua:732, reached via the detail-panel "📜 Timeline" button line 3072 and the open-session-timeline handler line 2081), and a narrative row per event — but it does NOT have the two things this feature centrally adds. There is no pagination past the cap: capLedgerSlice (cc-core.lua:553) hard-truncates to the newest 2000 and returns only a `truncated` flag; the UI merely prints "newest N loaded (older not shown)" (line 4678) with no offset/cursor control to fetch older events. And there is no per-request drill-down: auditRow (line 4653) renders a single text line plus a redact button — no click-to-expand to a fuller view. So the feature is not wholly present, ruling out DONT_ADD-as-redundant. (a) proxy-in-disguise also fails to flip it to DONT_ADD because the LiteLLM-specific load-bearing dimensions — key/user/team filters, spend-as-billing, and persisting full stored messages/response — map to nothing in Shepherd (no such entities, no network proxy, and heavy payload capture was explicitly declined). The judge correctly drops all of those and keeps only the session/project/date/type filtering Shepherd already has plus the one real gap (offset/cursor pagination on capLedgerSlice + the overlay, and a row expand reusing the existing transcript/ledger path rather than storing payloads). That is precisely the ADAPT shape: extend the existing audit-ledger subsystem to Shepherd primitives, don't build a new browser and don't dismiss it as already-done. Verdict unchanged: ADAPT.

#### Error/exception log table (failed-call tracking)  `[H/S]`
- **For:** Shepherd already DETECTS the error condition but never RECORDS it. `core.transcriptError` (cc-core.lua:1588) derives a session's `status=="error"` with an `error_message` from the `{type:"system",subtype:"api_error"}` transcript line, and the tile renders it (claude-dashboard.lua:5427). But grepping every `ledgerFor`/`appendLedger` call site shows there is no `error`/`failure` event type emitted at the moment of detection — only the downstream RECOVERY actions get ledgered (`continue` 2437, `auto_continue` 5615, `auto_respawn` 5595). So the append-only ledger, fleetStats, and the shift report can see that someone hit Continue, but have zero direct record of which sessions errored, how often, or with what message. That is a genuine observability hole: a reliability/cost-leak triage ("which projects keep dying on ECONNRESET / overloaded_error?") is impossible today. The fix is tiny and uses primitives that already exist — emit a `ledgerFor(it, {type="error", error_type=..., error_message=...})` on the same detection edge that already overrides the status, then teach fleetStats/shiftReport to bucket it. It directly serves the user's reliability goals and the existing Auto-Continue budget logic (per-folder failure counting) would gain a real history to reason about.
- **Against:** The LiteLLM feature as specified is deeply proxy-shaped: status_code, api_base, model_group, model_id, request_kwargs, request_id keyed rows, and a LITELLM_SUPPRESS_SPEND_LOG_TRACEBACKS env gate exist because a proxy is the one component that actually issues the HTTP call and owns the request/response envelope. Shepherd makes NO API calls — it reads status off the local filesystem and never sees a status_code, an api_base, or request kwargs; those fields are simply not observable to it, so a faithful ErrorLogs table would be mostly empty columns. There is also a strong "already have it" pull: Shepherd has the append-only JSONL ledger, the error tile state, Auto-Continue's per-folder failure budget, the hung/escalation watchdog, and the shift report — arguably the error story is already covered by status=="error" plus the recovery events, and adding a dedicated error event is redundant ceremony. A purist could say the need is fully met by the existing tile + Continue path.
- **Ruling:** The underlying NEED — a durable, queryable record of failed turns for reliability triage — is real and aligns with the user's stated reliability interests, but the implementation must be reshaped to Shepherd's primitives, which is the definition of ADAPT. DONT_ADD is wrong because the "already have it" claim doesn't hold: Shepherd records the error's CONSEQUENCES (Continue/respawn) but not the error EVENT itself, so the ledger and shift report literally cannot answer "which sessions errored and with what message." ADD is wrong because the LiteLLM schema is proxy-native — status_code/api_base/model_group/request_kwargs/request_id and the traceback-suppression env are unobservable to a non-proxy console and would be dead columns. The right move is to take the one thing Shepherd CAN see (the api_error subtype + message it already parses) and promote it to a first-class ledger event on the existing detection edge, then surface it as a failed-turns count in fleetStats and the shift report. This stays inside hard scope (local, single-user, no network, opt-in ledger) and reuses the exact event already detected, so it is cheap and high-fit.
