The credential-override use case (LiteLLM's whole point — admins testing keys they don't store) has no analog in a single-user console.
- **Ruling:** Not ADD: it's literally an HTTP route that takes credentials and makes the call — three direct scope violations, un-buildable as-is. Not DONT_ADD purely on "scope mismatch," because unlike pure proxy plumbing the NEED maps onto an existing first-class primitive (gateway provider profiles) and a documented failure mode (silent bad-gateway spawns, "unknown gateway provider"), and it advances the user's stated provider/agent-registry interest. So ADAPT wins: reshape "test_connection" into a "Test provider profile" preflight that runs a tiny bounded probe UNDER THE SPAWNED LOGIN SHELL (so $AUTH_TOKEN is expanded by the shell exactly as at spawn, never read into or stored by Shepherd's process), via the file-redirected hs.task pattern (the >tmpfile rule, retained-timeout backstop), pure result-parsing in cc-core, ledgered. Confidence is LOW and this stays low-priority: the honest cheap alternative (spawn-and-watch) already exists, so this only earns its keep if the user adds many gateway profiles. If reshaping it would require Shepherd to issue the network call from its own process or hold a key, the answer flips to DONT_ADD — that line must not be crossed.
- **How (Shepherd):** Provider profiles in cc-core.lua (providerById / providerEnv / envPrefix, ~L2618-2746) gain a pure probeProviderCommand(profile) that emits the same envPrefix + a minimal bounded probe (e.g. a single curl to ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL/models or a 1-token claude call) wrapped in run-under-login-shell so $authTokenEnv expands in the shell, not Shepherd. Execution goes through a new fx network/probe effect using the file-redirected hs.task pattern (context.md L54-59: redirect to tmpfile + retained-timeout backstop, NOT direct capture). Result parsing (pass / fail+reason: dns / 401 / wrong-model) is a pure cc-core function, unit-tested with recorder doubles. Surface it as a "Test" button in the Settings provider editor and gate-fail respawnSpec's "unknown gateway provider" path; emit a ledger event. Hard invariant: Shepherd's own process never reads or stores the token — it only ever emits the $VAR ref for the login shell to expand.
- **Skeptic check:** The ADAPT (low) ruling survives refutation. I verified both common error modes do NOT apply. (a) Proxy-dressed-as-local? No, once reshaped. The raw feature IS a proxy HTTP route that takes credentials and makes a call — three direct scope violations, correctly un-buildable. But the judge's reshape keeps the network call ON THE SPAWNED LOGIN SHELL (a curl to $ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL/models under `zsh -lic`, with the shell expanding $authTokenEnv), executed via the file-redirected hs.task pattern. That is the SAME trust boundary spawn already lives on: Shepherd's own process never issues the call and only ever emits the "$VAR" ref — it never reads or stores the token. Confirmed the primitives are real: providerEnv/envPrefix/loginShellWrap (cc-core.lua L2714-2757) already emit `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN="$VAR"` for the login shell to expand, and the file-redirected `/bin/sh -c "<cmd> > <tmpfile>"` + retained-timer backstop pattern is live at claude-dashboard.lua L866-897 (folder scan). So the adapted form does not cross the no-network-server / no-secrets line. (b) Already have it? No. grep for test_connection|probe|preflight|testProvider|connectivity across cc-core.lua + claude-dashboard.lua returns nothing. There is no probe/test-connection feature today. The NEED is real and maps onto a first-class primitive: provider profiles (providerById at L2632, providerByModel, providerEnv) plus a documented failure mode — respawnSpec returns canRespawn=false, reason="unknown gateway provider" (L2699), surfaced only as an hs.alert (dashboard L2351). A preflight "Test" button on the provider editor + gating that respawn path is a coherent extension, and it advances the user's stated provider/agent-registry interest. Why it STAYS low and does not upgrade: the honest cheap alternative genuinely exists — `transcriptError` (cc-core.lua L1588) derives a magenta Error tile client-side from the transcript tail, so a bad-gateway spawn already self-reports for free via spawn-and-watch, and arguably MORE faithfully (it runs the real `claude` binary/env, whereas a curl-to-/models probe can false-green when the model isn't on the gateway or the header format is wrong). That false-green risk is a quality caveat, not a scope violation, so it caps priority rather than flipping the verdict. The feature only earns its keep once the user maintains many gateway profiles. Net: not pure proxy plumbing (runs under the spawned shell, same boundary as spawn), not already present (no probe; spawn-and-watch is adjacent but distinct and already discounted by the judge), and it improves a local single-user console by giving a bounded preflight on an existing primitive's documented failure mode. ADAPT (low) holds. The hard invariant the judge drew — Shepherd never issues the call from its own process or holds a key — is the line; the reshape respects it.

## ⚖️ CONTESTED — judge labeled DON'T-ADD but the reasoning argues ADAPT (2 items)

These touch the user's stated priority (saved profiles / memory) — treat as borderline ADAPT, decide explicitly.

#### User/Team Memory store (namespaced key/value context CRUD)  `[M/M]`  (labeled DONT_ADD)
- **Ruling text:** DONT_ADD is tempting on "already have templates," but that undersells the user's explicit, weighted interest in saved agents / skills registry / MCP management — which is precisely a local namespaced library of reusable context profiles, and which templates only half-covers (templates are flat task-text with no metadata, no namespace, no per-bundle spawn opts). So the NEED is real and on-roadmap. But it must be reshaped to Shepherd's primitives, not copied: throw out user_id/team_id, _visibility_filter, _assert_write_access, Prisma, and the /v1/memory endpoints (all multi-tenant/proxy-only and out of scope). Keep only the local-file CRUD with a key NAMESPACE (prefix-scan over "agents/"/"skills/"/"mcp/") and an optional metadata blob — implemented as a new pure cc-core module mirroring templatePush/templateList/templateRemove/templateGet, persisted in its own JSON outside cc-config.json (same anti-clobber reasoning as templates/presets/labels), surfaced in the New Session modal as selectable "agent/skill profiles," and injected at spawn via spawnSpec / fed as a nudge — never auto-injected into a request body (Shepherd doesn't proxy). That is the textbook ADAPT: real need, extends an existing feature, reshaped onto sessions/spawn/config primitives. Confidence is medium because there's a legitimate DONT_ADD case if the user is happy treating templates as the one store; the namespace + saved-agent-profile angle is what tips it to ADAPT.

#### Model deployment registry / model CRUD (block/unblock) + capability introspection  `[H/S]`  (labeled DONT_ADD)
- **Ruling text:** DONT_ADD is wrong because the user explicitly flagged saved/reusable, directory-backed agent/model profiles as high interest and Shepherd already has the working local primitive for it — so this isn't pure proxy plumbing to reject, it's a feature to extend. But ADD is also wrong: the load-bearing parts of the candidate (push-into-llm_router, runtime block/unblock of served traffic, a DB-backed catalog with per-model dollar cost + tpm/rpm rate limits + an OpenAI-params capability map advertised to clients) all assume a multi-tenant HTTP proxy that Shepherd categorically is not (no network server, single user, flat subscription, JSON config not a DB, no request stream to throttle). ADAPT is the honest verdict: the real need (a registry of named, reusable model deployments with CRUD) is already met by provider profiles, and the only worthwhile new slices are small local reshapes — a per-profile "disabled/benched" flag (the safe analog of block/unblock) and optional capability metadata (max-input/output, mode, supported params) that the spawn modal can display/validate and `contextLimitFor` can lean on. Everything beyond that thin slice is proxy-serving and should be dropped.

## ❌ DO NOT ADD — 95 items (grouped; full reasoning each)

### Agents/Skills/MCP/Tools
- **MCP client connection config snippet (point an IDE/agent at the gateway)** — Match this exact candidate, not the broad topic. The candidate is LiteLLM's gateway-connection documentation: a mcpServers JSON block targeting http://localhost:4000/mcp/ with an api-key header, plus tools[].type='mcp' request shaping with require_approval. All of it lives on the wrong side of the wire for Shepherd — it is what a client drops in to reach a multi-tenant proxy, and it carries a network endpoint + a header-borne API key, both squarely inside Shepherd's hard NO-list (no network server, no proxy, no secrets storage, no API calls). Shepherd is the orchestrator that LAUNCHES local Claude Code sessions; it is neither the gateway being connected to nor the IDE doing the connecting. The genuinely valuable adjacent need — managing MCP servers for spawned sessions via Claude Code's own LOCAL mechanisms (.mcp.json / `claude mcp` / an --mcp-config flag on the launch line, carried by a saved-agent preset) — is a DIFFERENT feature that does not appear in this candidate's evidence and would not borrow anything from the LiteLLM snippet (no endpoint URL, no x-litellm-api-key header, no tools[] payload). So this specific item is DONT_ADD as a scope mismatch (it is proxy/network/secrets plumbing); the local MCP-management idea should be tracked as its own candidate rather than smuggled in under this one. The require_approval flag is the only conceptual echo of something Shepherd has (the gate), but Shepherd's gate operates on local tool invocations via the decision-file/nonce hook, not on a proxy's server-side approval of a remote MCP call.
- **Saved Agent definitions (CRUD registry: name + card + litellm_params + access/limits + make-public + per-agent analytics)** — The verdict turns on separating the NEED from the IMPLEMENTATION. The need — named, reusable "agents you hand work off to," with a saved backend/model, a starting task, and a scoped tool allow-list — is real, locally meaningful, and is the user's top stated future interest. The implementation — DB registry, multi-user access groups, public catalog, network rate limits, A2A cards, per-agent billing — is pure multi-tenant proxy scaffolding that has no home in a single-user local console. That split is the definition of ADAPT, not ADD (too much to copy as-is) and not DONT_ADD (the core need fits and is wanted). Concretely, Shepherd already has ~70% of the useful core: spawn presets are the agent record, provider profiles are litellm_params, gate.tools is object_permission, and the ledger/fleet-insights already give per-session activity rollups — so the candidate would EXTEND presets, not introduce a parallel system. The net-new, genuinely valuable piece is exactly the part the user flagged as under-leveraged: binding an MCP server set and/or a .claude/agents subagent + skills to the saved profile, which Claude Code consumes natively at spawn via --mcp-config and agent files. So adapt: extend the preset record to an "agent profile" {name, provider, permMode, initialTask/promptTemplate, gateTools allow-list, mcpConfig path/servers, claudeAgent/skill ref}, surface it in the New Session modal as "spawn from saved agent," and reuse the ledger for its activity view. Drop the DB, access groups, make-public, rate limits, A2A card, and per-agent billing entirely. Confidence is high: the local analog primitives are present and inspected, the scope violations are unambiguous, and the user's interest is explicit.
- **Agent invocation over A2A (JSON-RPC message/send) + agent card discovery + multi-backend completion bridge** — This is a textbook scope mismatch: a multi-tenant HTTP proxy capability (network server + access enforcement + API proxying to remote agent frameworks) dropped onto a local, single-user, no-network, no-proxy macOS GUI console. The candidate's three pillars — JSON-RPC /a2a endpoints, agent-card discovery over the network, and a completion bridge that calls remote backends — each independently violate Shepherd's stated hard limits. The user's real interest in "saved agents" is legitimate but is about spawning local Claude Code sessions from reusable profiles, which Shepherd already approximates (provider profiles + spawn presets) and can extend without any of A2A's protocol or proxy plumbing. So the favorably-weighted need does NOT rescue THIS feature; it points at a different, already-seeded local feature. Nothing here improves controlling local GUI sessions, and adapting it would require constructing the very proxy the scope rules out. Verdict: DONT_ADD (scope mismatch + pure API-proxy plumbing).
- **Skill injection into prompts + sandboxed code-execution loop (for non-Anthropic models)** — This is a multi-tenant proxy compatibility shim, the canonical DONT_ADD shape. The mechanism (system-prompt injection, skills-to-tools conversion, in-proxy agentic loop, container sandbox executor, inline-file collection) presupposes the proxy owns the request/response and the model runtime — neither is true for Shepherd, which only reads local session-status JSON and delivers keystrokes/decisions to GUI sessions running real Claude Code. Claude Code natively consumes SKILL.md and executes code itself, so injecting skills as tools and running a Docker loop is both impossible (no API in the path) and redundant (the session already does it). The non-Anthropic-emulation rationale is moot because Shepherd's providers are just env injection over real Claude Code. The genuine local need the user voiced — a saved/curated skills registry and spawn-from-a-saved-agent-profile — is real and fits Shepherd, but it is a DIFFERENT feature: discover/validate/toggle local skills+MCPs and pass them at spawn, NOT inject-prompt-and-run-a-sandbox. Copying this candidate would import a server runtime that breaches every hard scope limit. Ruling DONT_ADD; the registry idea should be tracked as its own spawn-primitive extension, not as an adaptation of this.
- **MCP unified tool catalog (list/call tools across all servers — gateway aggregation)** — The candidate's defining mechanism — a unified MCP endpoint that holds per-server sessions and fans list_tools/call_tool out to them for connecting clients — is exactly the proxy/gateway pattern that Shepherd's scope forbids (no network server, no proxy, never brokers tool/API calls). It also duplicates work Claude Code already does: every session is its own MCP client and aggregates its own servers. So the literal feature is DONT_ADD on both scope-mismatch and redundancy grounds. The genuinely valuable, user-aligned kernel inside it (a curated local registry/catalog of MCP servers to wire sessions to) is a DIFFERENT feature — config discovery + spawn-profile, not a runtime aggregation endpoint — and should be judged on its own as a separate candidate rather than smuggled in under this one. The aggregation endpoint that this card actually describes does not belong in Shepherd.
- **MCP static headers + env-var interpolation + per-user encrypted BYOK credentials/env-vars + upstream OAuth2** — The candidate's real subject is the LiteLLM gateway authenticating itself to upstream MCP servers on behalf of many users — header injection, encrypted per-user credential vaults, and an OAuth2 token-exchange/passthrough flow served over HTTP routes. All three pillars collide head-on with Shepherd's three hard scope limits (no network server, no multi-user, no secrets storage). Shepherd is not in the request path: Claude Code, not Shepherd, connects to MCP servers, so there is no connection on which to merge headers or cache a token. The user's MCP interest is real but points at a DIFFERENT, much smaller feature — managing which MCP servers a SPAWNED local session gets (e.g. curating .mcp.json / --mcp-config presets in the New-session modal), where secrets stay as $ENV names resolved by the login shell exactly like provider profiles do today. That adjacent feature should be evaluated on its own; this proxy-side credential/OAuth machinery is not it. Verdict DONT_ADD on grounds of scope mismatch (multi-tenant proxy + secrets storage + network OAuth) and redundancy with the existing non-secret env-injection model, with high confidence.
- **OpenAPI-to-MCP generation (turn any REST API into MCP tools)** — The against case is decisively stronger and the for case leans entirely on a future feature Shepherd doesn't have. OpenAPI-to-MCP generation only makes sense when something hosts and serves the generated MCP endpoint and makes the underlying REST/HTTP calls — that "something" is the LiteLLM proxy, and it is precisely the multi-tenant-proxy/network/server role Shepherd's hard scope limits rule out. Shepherd doesn't host MCP servers, doesn't make HTTP calls, and stores no credentials, so there is no primitive (sessions/hooks/gate/ledger/spawn/queue/providers) this plugs into without building the very server Shepherd refuses to be. The user's stated MCP interest is satisfied by MCP-server selection/management at spawn time (which existing MCP servers a saved-agent profile launches with) — a separate, addable idea — not by synthesizing brand-new servers from REST specs. This candidate is pure proxy plumbing for the local console. DONT_ADD.
- **Semantic MCP tool filtering (top-K relevant tools by embedding similarity) + settings** — Classic scope mismatch. The feature is an in-request, embedding-driven proxy optimization, and Shepherd is a local status-reader/controller that never intercepts the model call. The need it serves (don't overload a session with irrelevant MCP tools) overlaps the user's MCP-registry interest, but the only version that fits Shepherd would be a STATIC, deterministic per-saved-agent MCP/tool allow-list applied at spawn time (e.g. a profile that launches a session with a curated --mcp-config / tool set) — that is a different feature belonging to a future MCP/skills registry, not this semantic-router hook. Embeddings, similarity thresholds, top-K, and a pre-call interception layer are all out of scope and redundant given gate.tools already does deterministic name-based scoping. Verdict: DONT_ADD as specified; the adjacent local need is better captured by the separately-tracked saved-agent/MCP-registry work.
- **MCP sampling (servers borrow the gateway's LLM via sampling/createMessage)** — This is the proxy-core version of an MCP feature, and it loses on scope. The candidate's defining mechanic — the gateway becoming a keyless LLM endpoint that services sampling/createMessage with its own routing, credentials, cost metering, and rate limiting — is exactly the multi-tenant-proxy shape the schema flags as DONT_ADD (network server, secrets/credential routing, API proxying). Shepherd is not in the MCP transport path: it reads per-session status JSON written by Claude Code hooks and never makes API calls (confirmed in cc-core.lua: model handling is just a /model slash command and BASE_URL env injection, with an explicit comment that a base-URL change needs a relaunch, never a proxy). The host that actually satisfies MCP sampling for a local session IS Claude Code, not Shepherd. I weighed the user's genuine MCP interest favorably and looked hard for an ADAPT (observe/govern sampling via ledger + gate + context bar), but it fails the data-source test: there is no local artifact exposing per-server sampling activity for Shepherd to read, so any such feature would be speculative plumbing built on a hook Claude Code does not emit. If Claude Code ever started writing MCP-sampling events into ~/.claude status/transcript files, the observe-and-attribute slice could become a real ADAPT — but that's a different, future feature, not this one. As specified, it's pure API-proxy plumbing that breaks the charter.
- **MCP elicitation (relay an upstream server's interactive prompt to the client)** — DONT_ADD on scope mismatch — this is pure proxy-in-the-message-path plumbing. The relay only has somewhere to live because LiteLLM intercepts MCP JSON-RPC between client and server; Shepherd is categorically NOT in that path and has no hook or decision-file contract for elicitation the way it does for PreToolUse. Claude Code (the actual MCP client) already handles elicitation natively in its own UI, so even the human-relay value is already covered downstream. The superficially attractive adaptation ("render the form like an approval modal") collapses on inspection: there is no integration seam to feed Shepherd the elicitation request without building the entire MCP-proxy machinery the hard scope forbids. The user's real MCP interest is server MANAGEMENT (register/enable/attach MCPs to spawned sessions) — the opposite, high-level end from low-level transport interception. Against-argument wins decisively; this would require Shepherd to abandon its core "not a proxy" boundary for a capability the session layer already owns.
- **Built-in MCP gateway dispatch from Responses/tool calls** — DONT_ADD as specified — this is scope-mismatch proxy plumbing. The mechanism (intercept tool calls in a request, execute MCP servers, feed results back into the model loop) only makes sense when you are the thing sitting in the model's request path. Shepherd never is: confirmed in cc-core.lua, it spawns a local `claude` CLI and at most injects env to point it at a gateway (the "LiteLLM-style gateway" comment at cc-core.lua:2625), but it does not proxy, hold the agent loop, or make API calls. The MCP round-trip this feature performs is already performed by the Claude Code session itself, so there is no dispatch role for a local console to assume. Adopting it would force Shepherd to grow a network/request runtime and a model loop — direct hard-scope violations (no network server, no proxy). The user's favorable MCP interest is noted but points at a DIFFERENT, adaptable feature: managing which MCP servers a spawned session loads via a saved set on the preset/provider profile (config injection at spawn, no runtime). That adjacent idea is a legitimate ADAPT candidate on its own; THIS specific feature — the gateway dispatch handler — does not fit and is not the vehicle for that interest.

### Guardrails & Policy
- **Object permissions (MCP servers/tools/toolsets, vector stores, agents, models, blocked_tools) attachable to key/team/user/org/end-user** — Split the candidate into its multi-tenant machinery (the ObjectPermission row, the key/team/user/org/end-user FK fan-out, vector stores, search tools, access-groups) and its underlying need (reusable, saved profiles that grant/deny MCP servers and tools per session). The machinery is squarely DONT_ADD — multi-tenant proxy auth, banned by Shepherd's hard scope limits. But the need is real, deliverable against actual Claude Code launch flags (--mcp-config, --allowedTools), maps onto two primitives Shepherd already has (cc-presets reusable profiles + resolveGateTools/gate.tools per-session least-privilege), and is precisely what the user named as future interest (saved agents / skills / MCP management). That combination — real need + clean fit to existing primitives + explicit user pull — is the definition of ADAPT, not DONT_ADD. The adaptation is: add MCP-server set + tool allow/deny fields to a spawn preset (the local stand-in for a "reusable profile attachable by reference"), resolve them at spawn time into launch flags, and feed the per-session tool policy into resolveGateTools' reserved providerDefault layer. Drop the principal fan-out, vector stores, search tools, and access-groups entirely.
- **Prompt-injection detection guardrail** — Three independent disqualifiers, any one of which is sufficient: (1) Scope mismatch — this is a multi-tenant HTTP-proxy control against untrusted cross-network end-user prompts; Shepherd is local single-user with no prompt-inspection boundary and no API calls, so the pre-call hook and LLM-judge mechanics literally cannot exist here. (2) Inverted threat model — the human typing IS trusted, so detecting "jailbreak phrasing" in operator input is noise, not protection. (3) Redundancy — the genuine local risk (a destructive tool action) is already gated by the existing tool allow-list + autoAllow/autoDeny + human approval at the right boundary (the ACTION), which is strictly more reliable than fuzzy prompt-string heuristics that are trivially bypassed and false-positive-prone. The user's stated interest (agents/skills/MCP) does not pull this in: that's about reusable spawn profiles, not screening prompt text. The against side dominates decisively.
- **Block-code-execution guardrail (fenced-code detection by language + intent)** — Two independent reasons converge on DONT_ADD. (1) Scope mismatch: the feature is response-text content filtering for an HTTP proxy — detecting/masking fenced code in the model's reply stream. Shepherd is not a proxy, never sees model output, and renders no chat body to mask; there is literally no plug point for output-content rewriting. (2) The only part that maps to Shepherd's world — guarding what gets executed — is already covered, and covered better. cc-approve.sh + policies.patterns.autoDeny intercept the genuine PreToolUse Bash call with the literal command string and deterministically deny dangerous shapes via pattern_match (e.g. Bash(rm*)), logging provenance to the ledger. Bringing in fuzzy "intent phrase + confidence threshold" classification of code blocks would regress that to a probabilistic guess, contradicting the gate's deterministic, keystroke-is-a-contract design. There is no residual need worth building. If anything, the right roadmap move is to grow the existing autoDeny pattern library and per-session/per-agent gate.tools overrides — which already exist — not to import a proxy-side text scanner.
- **Team-submitted guardrail governance (submit -> pending_review -> approve/reject)** — This is a textbook multi-tenant governance workflow whose core value — gating one user class behind another user's approval — collapses to a no-op in a single-user app. Shepherd already has the guardrail substance (gate.tools allow-list + autoAllow/autoDeny patterns, configured directly by the one human who is by definition the admin), so the submit/approve/reject lifecycle, submitted_by, reviewed_at, and the pending_review/active/rejected state machine are exactly the parts that only make sense when many teams share a proxy over a network. That is the DONT_ADD scope-mismatch case (multi-user / network / proxy) per the rubric. The steelman-FOR — a local "stage a risky policy edit then confirm" guard — is real but is a DIFFERENT, much smaller feature (a confirm/diff overlay on config save) and should not be smuggled in under this candidate's governance framing; the candidate as written brings the multi-user machinery, which is dead weight here. The user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest is about reusable session-spawn profiles, not about a peer-review approval gate, so it does not pull this toward ADAPT either.

### Observability & Alerting
- **Email alerting (key created/rotated, user invited, budget) via SMTP/Resend/SendGrid** — This is a clean DONT_ADD on two independent grounds. First, the feature's actual payloads (keys created/rotated, user invitations, budget breaches) are 100% proxy/multi-tenant/secrets/billing concepts that have no referent in a local single-user console — Shepherd has no keys, no users to invite, and no dollar budgets (it tracks tokens by design). Second, the transport layer demands storing email-provider credentials, which directly violates the NO-secrets-storage scope limit. The only honest residual need (notify the operator when away) is already shipped: FX.push to ntfy.sh, wired into the exact escalation/hung events email would have used, with a Settings toggle, off by default, and zero stored secrets. Email would be a strictly heavier, secret-bearing duplicate of an existing channel that alerts on events Shepherd doesn't even have. Falls under the "redundant with what Shepherd already has and not worth extending" and "secrets/multi-user/network" DONT_ADD clauses simultaneously. Note: if Adam ever wants "more notification channels," the right move is a tiny additional sink behind the same FX.push fan-out (e.g. a webhook/Slack URL) that still stores no provider secret — but that is a different, much smaller feature than this LiteLLM email subsystem, and not what this candidate is.
- **Pluggable logging-callback fan-out (40+ destinations) + discovery/active-list view** — DONT_ADD wins decisively. The feature's defining characteristic — fanning one payload out to 40+ external observability/analytics services — is exactly the network-plus-secrets, multi-tenant-proxy shape Shepherd's hard limits exclude. Every sink in the list (Datadog, Langfuse, S3, OTel SaaS, PostHog...) needs credential storage and outbound HTTP, both forbidden, and the catalog/active-list endpoints are proxy admin surface for a product Shepherd is not. The legitimate kernel — "serialize fleet events once, then let them flow to multiple consumers" — is not a new need; Shepherd already implements it as the append-only JSONL audit ledger written by three disjoint writers through one `cc_ledger_append`, with export, on-demand LLM review, and notification history all reading off that single stream. So the strongest non-network reading of the candidate ("a pluggable local sink registry") is redundant with what Shepherd already has, and the only incremental piece — letting a user route ledger events to their own localhost collector or notify hook — is speculative (no demonstrated demand) and partly already possible via the anticipated user `cc-notify.sh` hook. It also does nothing for the favored future interests (saved agents / skills registry / MCP management). Both the as-is verdict (network/secrets violation) and the adapted verdict (redundant with the ledger) land on don't-build. If anything ever materializes here, it would be a tiny enhancement to the existing ledger's emit chokepoint, not a port of this feature — so it does not justify even an ADAPT.
- **Prometheus /metrics endpoint + service-health metrics** — Two independent disqualifiers, and the steelman-FOR collapses on inspection. (1) Scope mismatch on the mechanism: Prometheus export exists to let an external scraper pull telemetry over the network from a long-lived proxy serving many tenants — the exact multi-user/network/proxy shape Shepherd's hard limits exclude. (2) The substance splits cleanly: the proxy-specific families (budgets per key/user/team/org, deployment cooldown/success/failure, routing, cache, guardrail latency, TTFT/LLM-API latency, spend/token cost) have no referent in a local Claude-Code-session console that makes no API calls; the families that DO have a referent (decision counters, blocked-time, per-session/per-project rollups, time-series) are already implemented as pure cc-core aggregations over the ledger (fleetStats/fleetStandup/bucketEvents/sessionRisk) and surfaced in the insights + shift-report overlays and the fleet footer. So the feature is simultaneously redundant (on the fitting parts) and scope-mismatched (on the rest), with a forbidden delivery channel. The service-health idea is the only fresh seed, and it's already answered by `make doctor`/tooling_check + resolveBin degradation — converting that to a continuously-scraped gauge would require the very network endpoint that's banned. Verdict: DONT_ADD. (If a future itch arises, the right shape is not Prometheus but extending the existing in-panel insights overlay with a "dependency health" strip — but that's a separate, smaller card, not this feature.)
- **Durable Workflow Run tracking (run header + append-only event log + message inbox) with spend-log linkage** — The verdict turns on the gap between the NEED and the FEATURE. The need (durable, per-unit-of-work history that survives restarts) is real but already substantially met: cc-core.lua:496-575 is an append-only JSONL ledger with server-assigned sequence/id, session/projectKey/time/type filtering, retention, and redaction; lineageByProject/projectLineage (1040-1120) already rolls up the spawn/respawn/clear/continue churn for one projectKey ACROSS the session_ids it mints — that IS a 'run = projectKey lineage' header in all but name; and sessionTimeline (732) gives the chronological per-run event slice. Of the candidate's three pillars, the message inbox duplicates the transcript Shepherd already reads (and contradicts its no-store posture), the spend linkage is proxy-only and inapplicable (no API calls, no spend logs), and the event-log-with-auto-status machine is both redundant with the ledger AND architecturally wrong for Shepherd, which observes status live rather than replaying events. Adopting it as-specified means building a parallel persistence system that re-implements the ledger, copies transcript content it shouldn't, and carries proxy concepts that no-op locally. The only honestly-additive sliver — a first-class named run header — is a thin future extension of the existing lineage primitive, not a reason to import this 3-table store, and it is not the saved-agents/skills/MCP interest the user actually flagged.

### Cost & Budget
- **Per-model budget within a key (model_max_budget)** — All three load-bearing parts of model_max_budget — key scoping, request-path hard-block enforcement, and dollar denomination — collide with Shepherd's hard scope limits (no secrets/key store, no proxy/no API calls, tokens-not-dollars by explicit design). What remains after stripping those is just "per-model usage visibility," which Shepherd ALREADY HAS (sumUsage.byModel, rendered per-model + fleet plan-window bars). The only net-new idea worth anything is a per-model token soft-alert, but that is a small optional extension of the existing usage surface, not this feature, and it isn't compelling enough on its own to greenlight here (a single user already sees per-model rows and the 5h/weekly bars, and can switch models via the live /model picker). Verdict is DONT_ADD on grounds of scope mismatch (proxy/key/secrets/dollars) plus redundancy with existing per-model token tracking.
- **Model price + context-window + capability registry (model_prices_and_context_window.json)** — The candidate is a bundle of dimensions with very different fit. The window+override dimension is ALREADY HAD (contextLimitFor + per-provider contextLimit), so that part is not re-added. The capability/region/mode/multi-variant/tiered-pricing bulk is multi-tenant-proxy plumbing with no local consumer in a no-network, no-API-call console — DONT_ADD scope. But one narrow slice has a genuine local need: Shepherd already extracts the exact token counts (incl. both cache buckets, per model) that a cost map needs, and stops one multiply short of a dollar figure. For gateway/metered sessions that number is real and useful, and a per-provider cost override drops cleanly onto the existing provider profile and usage aggregator. That makes the honest verdict ADAPT, not ADD (don't import the JSON wholesale; build a minimal, config-gated cost-per-token map reshaped onto Shepherd's provider profiles + sumUsage) and not DONT_ADD (the cost need is real for the metered case the README itself carves out). Gate it OFF by default and scope dollar display to gateway/metered sessions so it never shows a misleading number to a flat-rate subscriber — consistent with Shepherd's everything-off-by-default discipline and its stated honest-limits stance.
- **Daily rollup spend tables (per user/team/org/tag/end-user/agent x model x key)** — DONT_ADD on the feature as specified — it is a multi-tenant proxy billing artifact (six entity tables keyed on api_key, spend upserted from the request path) and every load-bearing dimension (user/team/org/tag/end-user/api_key/provider, dollar spend) is out of scope for a single-user console that makes no API calls and stores no keys. The honest residual need — durable, cross-day historical token/cost trends per project per model — is real and partly unmet (Shepherd recomputes usage live and the shift report only covers one open window). But that need does NOT require importing this feature: the gap is "persist the rollup," not "build LiteLLM's spend tables." It would be satisfied by extending the EXISTING ledger/shift-report path (a small cc-rollup file written on the GC timer, fed by sumUsage + isoToEpoch + fleetStandup's projectKey grouping), which is a different, much smaller piece of work and properly lives under the audit-ledger roadmap, not as a port of LiteLLM's schema. Adopting the table-per-entity composite-index design as-is would be scope creep (a relational billing store) for ~one usable dimension Shepherd can already compute. The user's saved-agents/skills interest doesn't rescue it: "agent spend" here is a proxy principal, not a Claude Code session profile. So the verdict is DONT_ADD the LiteLLM feature; if the persisted-history itch is ever scratched, do it as a native ledger extension.
- **Cache-aware token cost accounting (prompt-caching read/write costs)** — Two independent reasons land this on DONT_ADD. First, redundancy: the cache-read/cache-creation accounting that is the literal name of this feature is ALREADY in Shepherd, sourced from the identical transcript fields, and Shepherd already treats cache reads as the cheap/near-free bucket (the `real` vs `total` split). The only novel content is converting tokens to dollars via a price registry. Second, scope mismatch on that novel part: the price registry (per-model, per-tier, multi-provider, frequently revised) is a proxy-billing artifact. Shepherd's primary user is on a flat Claude subscription where per-call dollars are meaningless, and its real metering signal — the official Anthropic plan-window % — is already shipped via the OAuth endpoint. Gateway pricing is unknown by design (README "Honest limits"), so a registry would either be empty for the cases that need it or confidently wrong when prices drift. The team already made this call deliberately and documented why. Adding a maintained price table to a local console to recompute a number that is flat-rate for the main user, and unknowable for the gateway user, is not worth the churn or the risk of lying. If a user ever truly wants gateway dollar estimates, the honest path is a tiny optional user-supplied price map applied to the token buckets Shepherd already aggregates — not importing LiteLLM's registry — and that's a future ADAPT, not this feature as specified.
- **Spend cold-storage offload (S3/GCS) for heavy request payloads** — DONT_ADD wins decisively as scope mismatch on multiple independent axes. The feature exists to relieve a network-served relational spend-log DB of multi-MB request blobs across many tenants by tiering them to cloud object storage and lazy-fetching by key. Shepherd has no DB (flat local JSONL is already the cheap tier), no network (S3/GCS would breach the NO-network-server limit), no secrets storage (S3/GCS credentials breach the NO-secrets limit), and no heavy payloads to begin with — the ledger and status files intentionally persist short summaries, not raw tool_input/payload blobs. Even the underlying NEED (control store growth) is already solved by local primitives: retention-day GC, scoped purge, export caps, and redaction. The steelman FOR only survives by hypothesizing a future where Shepherd captures full payloads — but that future was explicitly DECLINED in the ledger roadmap ("heavy evidence capture — screen recordings/HARs/Playwright traces" rejected to keep it a console, not a container). So there is no path where the cloud-tiering shape fits; if blob bloat ever materialized, a local sidecar-file spill would be the Shepherd-native answer, not S3/GCS. No ADAPT, because nothing about the object-storage mechanism survives reshaping — what survives (cap/spill heavy data) Shepherd already does without it.
- **Global spend dashboards (top keys, per-team/model/provider, daily activity)** — The endpoint SUITE is redundant-plus-out-of-scope. Top-N keys, per-model, and daily-activity time-series already exist as Fleet insights (core.fleetStats top-N + provenance), the Trends-24h hourly sparklines, sumUsage.byModel, and the shift report's per-project rollup — the README itself frames these as the local, zero-token read of the same data. Per-team and per-provider spend are multi-tenant proxy primitives with no local mapping (per-project is the existing analog), and /global/spend/refresh is server-side materialized-view plumbing — all DONT_ADD by scope. The lone fittable sliver is a token→USD estimate, which is a tiny optional extension to an existing byModel breakdown, not a reason to import the dashboard suite — and it's actively dubious under a flat-rate Claude plan where Shepherd already shows the authoritative plan-window %. Net: "already have it" for the analytics that fit, "scope mismatch" for the rest. Not worth extending.
- **Per-MCP-tool-call cost tracking & spend accounting** — The underlying NEED — chargeback/budget accounting per tool call across tenants — does not exist for a single-user local console with no one to bill and no proxy mediating calls. The part of this feature that IS real (attribute MCP activity to sessions/projects) is already shipped: tool_request events name the tool, MCP calls already flow through as named tools, and fleetStats/fleetStandup already roll those up per-session and per-project. The only net-new element is assigning arbitrary dollar costs to MCP calls — and because MCP tool calls carry no provider-billed cost, those numbers are operator fictions that add config surface and drift risk while delivering no decision the user actually makes. Shepherd already nailed the legitimate cost signal (zero-cost local token/context usage + plan-window %). The user's MCP interest is about management/registry and spawning from saved MCP configs, not spend accounting — so this does not even serve the stated future interest. This lands squarely in DONT_ADD: scope mismatch (multi-tenant chargeback) layered on top of something Shepherd already has (per-tool ledger aggregation), not worth extending with fabricated cost values.
- **Per-request spend log / structured per-call record (StandardLoggingPayload -> LiteLLM_SpendLogs)** — This is a textbook scope mismatch on the strongest axis: the feature is a proxy's per-request billing/audit fact table, and Shepherd is a local console that doesn't proxy. Its defining columns — response_cost, cost_breakdown, spend $, requester_ip, end_user, api_base, guardrail_information, cache_hit — are either multi-tenant-network constructs (DONT_ADD per the rubric: "multi-tenant proxy / network / multi-user / secrets") or the one explicitly-declined dimension (dollars, README.md:329). Crucially, Shepherd ALREADY HAS the legitimate local subset of this idea, and has it in a better-fit form: per-turn usage events parsed zero-cost from the transcript (parseUsageLine/sumUsage/computeUsage) plus the append-only JSONL ledger as the operator-action fact table that fleetStats/sessionRisk/shift-report all aggregate from. So the "one canonical row everything reads" need is satisfied by two existing primitives. The residual delta — a couple of enrichment fields (per-turn duration, request_tags, structured error_information) — is not the feature; it is a small optional add-on to the existing usage event, not a reason to import the LiteLLM schema, and the project has consciously kept the usage row minimal. Net: redundant where it fits, impossible/declined where it doesn't. DONT_ADD.
- **Atomic budget reservation (pre-charge before request)** — Three of the verdict's DONT_ADD triggers fire at once. (1) Scope mismatch: the mechanism is request-interception at a proxy with atomic per-counter cache keys to stop concurrent callers overshooting a shared spend budget — Shepherd has no proxy, no request to intercept, no shared dollar budget, and no concurrency-against-a-counter problem (one human, level-triggered dispatch). (2) It contradicts a stated design stance: Shepherd intentionally reports tokens not dollars because subscription cost is flat and gateway/local pricing is unknown, so the cost-estimation and reconciliation that the whole feature hinges on has no inputs. (3) Even the steelman ADAPT (a local "spend governor" gating spawn/feed on a token-budget ceiling) is a different feature — it would reuse none of LiteLLM's reservation machinery (no atomicity needed without concurrency, no rollback, no proxy reconcile loop), it would only borrow the vague notion "cap fleet burn," and it isn't what the user asked for (their interest is saved agents / skills / MCP, not budgets). Building a token-spend gate is a plausible FUTURE idea on its own merits, but importing THIS feature contributes nothing toward it; the answer to "would copying this feature in improve Shepherd" is no.
- **External cost-export integrations (CloudZero / Vantage FinOps)** — DONT_ADD on a triple scope violation — network egress, secrets storage, multi-tenant FinOps framing — each independently disqualifying per context.md's verbatim "no network, no multi-user, no secrets." The user's stated future interest (saved agents / skills / MCP management) is orthogonal to this; there is no way to bend CloudZero/Vantage SaaS export into a local Claude-Code-session primitive. The underlying NEED (visibility into Claude Code spend) is ALREADY met by Shepherd's existing local token/usage tracking and plan-window %, so even the legitimate kernel here is redundant. If a user genuinely wanted to feed their own FinOps pipeline, the right move is the already-existing local export pattern (a plain local cost-rollup file they own), NOT building first-party connectors to two commercial SaaS platforms with stored API keys. That hypothetical local-CSV-export is a different, much smaller feature — not this candidate — so it doesn't lift this one to ADAPT. This is classic multi-tenant-proxy scope creep.
- **Per-key rate limits (TPM/RPM/max_parallel_requests + allocation modes)** — The feature in its actual shape — per-API-key TPM/RPM/concurrency caps with allocation modes enforced in a proxy auth path — fails every fit test: no keys, no secrets storage, no API calls, no request interception, no multi-tenant capacity to allocate. It is the textbook DONT_ADD scope mismatch (multi-tenant proxy / network / secrets). The only salvageable kernel (a local concurrency cap or a plan-window-aware dispatcher pause) is a small, DIFFERENT feature that does not need TPM/RPM, key schemas, or allocation modes — it would reuse Shepherd's existing free-member dispatcher gate and usage tracking, and should be proposed on its own merits rather than imported as this. The strongest FOR case still reduces to "a couple of Shepherd's own primitives are vaguely analogous," which is exactly the scope-creep trap to resist. Ruling DONT_ADD, with the note that a quota-aware dispatcher guardrail is worth tracking separately.

### Governance & Access
- **Key blocking / expiry / soft-deletion with audit trail** — Strip away the proxy framing and ask what's actually new for a local single-user console, and nothing survives. Block = short-circuit auth: there is no auth, no credential, no API call to gate — Shepherd reads filesystem status and drives windows. Expire = invalidate a credential on a clock: Shepherd's time-based lifecycle (stale detection, hung watchdog, auto-respawn/drain bounds, ledger retention) already covers every local instance of "this thing aged out." Soft-delete-with-audit-trail = a tombstone whose value is the deleted_by / deleted_by_api_key / litellm_changed_by accountability columns: those columns are definitionally multi-actor/multi-tenant and collapse to nothing for one local user with no keys, and Shepherd's append-only ledger + scoped purge already gives accountable, non-silent removal. The against case is decisive on all three. The user's favorable interest is in saved AGENT/SKILL/MCP profiles, which this feature does not deliver — a key-credential admin lifecycle is not an agent registry, and bolting "blocked/expires/tombstone" semantics onto sessions would duplicate drain/stop/watchdog/purge under credential-flavored names. DONT_ADD on scope mismatch (multi-tenant proxy + secrets + multi-user accountability), redundant with existing lifecycle and ledger primitives.
- **Invitation links (admin invites a user to onboard)** — This is a textbook scope mismatch. Invitation links are an identity/access-provisioning primitive for a multi-tenant, networked, multi-user system — admin invites another human, emails them a claim link, tracks cross-person acceptance and attribution. Shepherd is explicitly single-user, no-network, no-secrets, no-email. Remove those four pillars and the feature is hollow: there is no one to invite, nowhere to host the link, nothing to email, and no distinct identity to record as accepter. The lone salvageable kernel ("time-bounded claimable spawn config") is already covered by spawn presets + the New-session modal, which onboard a session in one click without an expiry/claim handshake that buys nothing locally. The user's genuine interest in saved agent profiles / skills registry / MCP management is real, but it is served by extending presets into richer agent profiles — NOT by importing an inter-human invitation/onboarding flow. Verdict: DONT_ADD (multi-user/network/secrets scope mismatch; the usable remnant is redundant with existing spawn presets).
- **IP allowlisting / trusted-proxy XFF handling + allowlist management** — Triple scope mismatch against the stated hard limits: it requires a network server (Shepherd has none — "NO network server"), it is multi-tenant access control (Shepherd is single-user), and it is proxy request-path plumbing (Shepherd does not proxy or accept inbound connections). The against case is overwhelming and the for case collapses on inspection: the only Shepherd surface that touches "remote" at all is the SSH bridge, but that is outbound and authenticated by SSH itself, so there is no inbound client IP to derive, no XFF to parse, and no untrusted caller population to allow-list. The user's stated future interest (saved agents / skills registry / MCP management) is unrelated to this, so there's no favorable weight to apply. The trust boundary Shepherd actually relies on is the macOS user account, which IP allowlisting cannot improve. Nothing here reshapes into the gate, ledger, sessions, hooks, spawn, or queue with real value. DONT_ADD.
- **Read-only / view-only access tiers for keys and admins** — This is a multi-tenant auth/RBAC primitive and a textbook scope mismatch. The feature's whole reason to exist is letting DIFFERENT humans on a SHARED proxy observe without mutating, enforced at a network auth layer over key objects and spend — Shepherd is a single-user, no-network, no-secrets, no-key local console with one principal who is supposed to have full control. There is no second actor to down-tier and no key/route to gate. The only salvageable kernel — a single-user "observe mode" UI lock over the panel's high-blast-radius buttons — is a presentation-layer toggle, not an auth principal, has marginal value for a solo operator, and is not what this candidate is. The genuinely related thing (reduced-capability remote actors) Shepherd already does, correctly, via the SSH bridge's headless-only restriction, and via the per-session tool gate / off-by-default automation that already separate "what can act automatically" from "what needs the human." Adding principal tiers would be net complexity solving a non-problem. DONT_ADD on scope mismatch.
- **Virtual key issuance (master key + hashed token store)** — Pure scope mismatch on multiple axes at once: network server, multi-user/multi-tenant auth, secrets storage, and API-proxy plumbing — every one of which is an explicit hard-scope NO. The candidate's three mechanics (mint sk- token, hash-and-store, authenticate inbound requests) require Shepherd to BE the thing it explicitly is not: a credential-issuing, request-authenticating proxy. There is no local single-user Claude-Code-session reshaping that preserves the feature's identity: a local GUI controlling sessions never needs to issue API keys or authenticate Bearer headers, because it spawns the `claude` CLI which authenticates itself (claude.ai login or a login-shell-expanded env var). Shepherd already solved the only legitimately-overlapping need (credential injection without storage) in the exactly-opposite way the provider-profile comment documents — by naming an env var, never holding a key. The favorable weight for saved-agents/skills/MCP does not rescue this: an agent profile can reference a credential by env-var name without Shepherd ever minting, hashing, or storing a token. Even the governance-attribution angle is already served by the ledger's `by:` provenance and gate.tools scoping, without any token table. Building this would add credential-material handling to a process whose core promise is that it never sees one. DONT_ADD, high confidence.
- **Key rotation (auto-rotate + grace-period regenerate)** — The against case is decisive and the for case collapses under it. The entire feature is a lifecycle for secret values the system mints, stores (as hashes), keeps valid through a grace window, and revokes via a cron against a token database. Shepherd does none of these and is architecturally forbidden from all of them: no secrets storage, no network/server, no DB, no key issuance authority. It only references an env-var NAME that a downstream login shell resolves — it never sees, owns, or can rotate the key. The one survivable fragment (a "your credential is N days old" nudge) has no real data source because Shepherd can't read the credential's value or age, so it degrades to a hand-typed reminder field that adds noise without governance value — not worth the surface. This is the canonical multi-tenant-proxy / secrets-management feature the scope limits exist to exclude. The user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest doesn't rescue it: those are about reusable spawn profiles, not key custody. Clean DONT_ADD.
- **Service-account keys** — Pure scope mismatch on the strongest axis. Service-account keys are credential provisioning + server-side request validation for a multi-tenant proxy serving many teams — three things Shepherd categorically is not (no secrets, no multi-user/teams, no proxy/network). The single transferable idea — "mark automated vs human-driven actions for governance" — Shepherd already covers via its ledger's non-human gate-decision tagging and its off-by-default autonomy budgets, so even the salvageable kernel is redundant. The for-case requires fabricating teams, minted keys, and an inbound-request validator that have no referent in a local single-user GUI that spawns sessions under the user's own auth and stores zero keys. Nothing to ADD, and ADAPT would mean building the proxy substrate the feature assumes rather than reshaping it to an existing primitive.
- **Teams: membership with roles, team budget/limits, team-scoped models + delegated member permissions + default-team settings** — Four of the five named limits — roles/admins, delegated member permissions (RBAC over routes), team budget/spend/reset, and tpm/rpm rate limits — each independently trip a HARD scope limit: multi-user, network management API, secrets/spend metering, and proxy plumbing. Shepherd has one user, no server, no API calls, and no spend visibility, so there is nothing for membership, RBAC, or budgets to govern. The only piece with a single-user analog (team-scoped allowed models / model aliases / defaults) collapses onto capabilities Shepherd already ships: session groups for cohorting, providers[] for the model picker, gate.tools for the per-session allow-list, and spawn presets/provider for launch defaults. So it is simultaneously a scope mismatch on the bulk of the feature AND redundant on the salvageable remainder. The user's saved-agents interest is better served by a dedicated "saved agent profile -> spawn" feature, not by porting LiteLLM's tenancy model. Not worth building.
- **Organizations (top tier: org budget, org-scoped models, org members + org_admin role)** — Textbook scope-mismatch DONT_ADD. Organizations is the apex of LiteLLM's multi-tenant RBAC tree: its four pillars are (a) members + user_role, (b) an ORG_ADMIN that governs other users/teams, (c) a network-enforced dollar spend budget, and (d) HTTP route authorization. All four collide with Shepherd's hard limits — single-user, no multi-user, no network server, no secrets/billing. The only locally-meaningful residue ("namespace several projects together and roll them up") is already delivered by session groups, projectKey-keyed persistent state, project routing v1, and the per-project shift-report rollup; an "org" tier on top would be a vacuous wrapper around a one-person fleet. The user's interest in saved agents/skills/MCPs is real and weighted favorably, but it argues for a saved-agent-profile / skills-registry feature that spawns a session from a profile — NOT for an org tenancy layer with admin roles and spend caps, the wrong primitive to attach that to. Confidence is high: three of the four pillars are explicitly forbidden by context.md's scope, and the fourth is already shipped.
- **Internal users with roles, per-user budgets/limits + default-new-user params** — The feature is dominated by its multi-user, identity/RBAC, and metered-spend assumptions, every one of which is an explicit hard-scope NO for Shepherd (context.md line 13: "No network, no multi-user, no secrets"). Roles only matter when there is more than one human with differing privileges; Shepherd has exactly one operator who already has full control, so a LitellmUserRoles enum is dead weight. Per-user budgets/tpm/rpm only matter for a gateway enforcing quotas on outbound calls; Shepherd never proxies and has no per-user spend to meter. SSO / default-on-creation presupposes an auth/login system that does not and will not exist. The single genuinely portable kernel — "apply a saved default profile when you create a thing" — is not a user feature here, it is a SESSION-SPAWN feature, and Shepherd already implements exactly that without a user dimension: spawn presets, provider profiles, permission-mode pickers, and config-driven gate.tools defaults all flow into a new session at spawn-time. Bolting a fictional "user" entity on top to carry those defaults adds a multi-tenant abstraction with zero local payoff. The user's real interest (saved reusable agents/skills/MCPs) is better served by extending the existing spawn-preset / provider-profile primitives directly into a "saved agent profile" a spawn inherits — a separate ADAPT candidate, NOT this user/role/budget table. Verdict: DONT_ADD on scope mismatch (multi-tenant, identity, metered-proxy), noting its lone reusable idea is already covered by Shepherd's spawn presets.
- **End-users / customers (external-caller cost tracking, region pinning, blocking)** — Every load-bearing noun in the candidate — end-user, customer, per-customer budget, admin alias, block/unblock endpoints, router deployment region — only exists in a multi-tenant HTTP proxy serving many external callers over a network. That is precisely the scope Shepherd's context.md rules out: NO network server, NO multi-user, NO proxy, NO secrets. Shepherd has exactly one user (the operator), so there is no external identity to meter, budget, alias, block, or region-pin. The "FOR" adjacencies are mirages: Shepherd's usage tracking is token/context not dollars (it has no pricing source because it doesn't proxy); its gate blocks tools within the operator's own sessions, not other people; its provider pin chooses a backend at spawn, it doesn't filter a router's deployment pool by an external caller's allowed region. The one genuinely portable sliver — a per-project dollar-budget cap — isn't part of THIS feature's value (the value is external-caller governance) and would itself require a pricing model Shepherd intentionally lacks. This is the canonical DONT_ADD: scope mismatch (multi-tenant/network) plus API-proxy plumbing, and the user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest gives it no pull (a customer-blocking endpoint is nothing like a reusable local agent profile).
- **JWT / OIDC authentication with claim-to-role/team/org mapping & RBAC enforcement** — This is the cleanest possible scope mismatch: the feature is built from authentication of remote multi-tenant callers over a network using externally-issued secrets/tokens, and Shepherd is a local, single-user, no-network, no-secrets GUI console with no actor to authenticate. It cannot ADD as-is without breaking all four hard limits. It does not qualify as ADAPT either: ADAPT requires the underlying NEED to be real for Shepherd, but the need ("distinguish and gate many users/teams/orgs") simply does not exist for one human at one Mac — there is no second principal. The only salvageable fragment is "config-selected capability profiles per session," but that is a separate, already-mostly-covered idea (the gate.tools allow-list plus the user's saved-agent-profile interest), not this JWT/OIDC/RBAC machinery; pulling that thread does not preserve anything from this candidate. The gate Shepherd already has is the local answer to "should this action be allowed," and it works without any notion of identity. Verdict: DONT_ADD — multi-tenant proxy auth, redundant-at-best with the local gate, and four-for-four against the hard scope limits.
- **JWT-claim-to-virtual-key mapping (auto-register / reject behaviors)** — This is textbook multi-tenant HTTP-proxy auth plumbing: IdP JWT → claim → virtual key, with team fallback / 403 reject / auto-provision for unknown callers. It depends on three things Shepherd categorically does not and will not have — a network server accepting authenticated requests, multiple users/tenants to distinguish, and stored API credentials (virtual keys) to map identities onto. The only transferable idea, "resolve an identity to a saved profile with a default/refuse/create policy for the unknown case," is both extremely generic and already realized by Shepherd's provider-profile resolution (spawnProviderKey's nil/""/id tri-state) and its cwd/projectKey-keyed persistent state. The user's favorable interest in saved-agent/skills/MCP registries does not rescue it: that interest is about spawning a local session from a saved profile, which is a spawn-preset concern, not a JWT-claim authentication concern — wiring JWT claims into it would add zero local value while importing exactly the multi-user/secrets/network concepts the scope forbids. DONT_ADD on scope mismatch.
- **SSO login (Google/Microsoft/Okta/generic OIDC) + persisted SSO config (masked secrets) + CLI SSO** — DONT_ADD on a clean three-for-three collision with hard scope limits. SSO is the textbook example the prompt warns about: it ONLY makes sense for a multi-tenant HTTP service serving many users over a network, and it demands the exact trio Shepherd forbids — network server, multi-user identity, persisted secrets. Shepherd's authentication is already solved and arguably stronger: macOS gates physical/login access to the machine the webview runs on; the principal is the OS user. Bolting OIDC onto a single-user in-process Hammerspoon panel adds attack surface (stored client secrets, a redirect listener) to defend a boundary that doesn't exist for a local GUI. This is not even an ADAPT: there is no Shepherd primitive that the NEED reshapes onto, because the need (verify which of many remote humans this is) does not arise for a single local operator. The one genuinely portable idea — the write-once masked-on-read secret contract — is worth remembering IF Shepherd ever stores anything secret-ish, but today it explicitly stores nothing secret (keys live in the shell via authTokenEnv), so even that has nowhere to land. Verdict stands with high confidence.
- **SCIM v2 provisioning (Users & Groups sync from IdP)** — SCIM v2 collides with all three of Shepherd's hard scope limits simultaneously (network server, multi-user, secrets/IdP trust — context.md line 13 literally reads "No network, no multi-user, no secrets"), and its core noun — a provisioned human user identity — has zero referent in a single-user local console where the only user is the operator at the keyboard. The against case is overwhelming and the for case relies entirely on false cognates: SCIM Groups are sets of human principals synced from an IdP, whereas Shepherd's groups/teams are local tile-organization and queue-routing constructs with no identity backing; activate/deactivate is account lifecycle, which Shepherd has no concept of. The user's stated future interest (saved agents / skills / MCP registry) is about reusable Claude-Code-session PROFILES, not human-user provisioning, so it does not pull this feature in — a saved-agent-roster-from-a-file idea, if ever pursued, is a plain local config feature that needs none of SCIM's IdP/network/multi-user machinery. There is no adaptable kernel worth the scope cost.

### Routing & Reliability
- **Weighted load-balancing across deployments (weight/rpm/tpm pick)** — Scope mismatch plus redundancy-with-a-better-design. The need LiteLLM addresses — statistically distributing a high-volume fungible request stream across interchangeable same-model backends — does not exist for a local fleet of stateful, human-attended Claude Code sessions where each handoff is a discrete task pasted into one window. Shepherd already has the routing primitive (routePick/routeTask) and it was intentionally built deterministic; random weighted picking is an explicit anti-goal because predictable, contract-bound keystroke delivery is core. The rpm/tpm half is proxy plumbing Shepherd has no analog for (no API calls, no request metering). If a future need for biasing handoffs toward preferred session/agent profiles ever materializes, the right move is a deterministic priority/eligibility ordering inside the existing routePick (which already supports tiebreak ordering), NOT a random weighted shuffle — so even the steelman FOR resolves to "extend the deterministic picker," not "copy this feature." That makes it DONT_ADD.
- **Bounded retries with retry_after delay and max_fallbacks cap** — The core of this feature — retry N times, wait a minimum delay between attempts, cap the total — is already shipped in Shepherd's two recovery primitives. shouldAutoContinue/stepAutoContinue (cc-core.lua:2200-2239) is a one-to-one mapping: minSeconds = retry_after, maxAttempts = num_retries, with the fire-restarts-clock behavior giving the spacing LiteLLM gets from retry_after. shouldAutoRespawn/stepAutoRespawn (cc-core.lua:2116-2190) is the same pattern for session death, using the stale threshold as backoff and maxRetries as the cap. The only genuinely-novel sub-feature, max_fallbacks, presupposes a multi-deployment fallback walk that Shepherd structurally does not have (it does not proxy or switch model deployments mid-failure; it resumes the same session). Per the rubric this is "redundant with what Shepherd already has and not worth extending," with the residual piece being pure proxy plumbing. Verdict: DONT_ADD.
- **Deployment health-state cache (unhealthy-deployment exclusion)** — The need is real but already satisfied, and the specific mechanism is proxy-shaped. Shepherd's routing dispatcher already excludes non-healthy sessions from receiving work via sessionFree/routePick, and the candidate's defining feature — an unhealthy-ID set with staleness expiry — is literally Shepherd's `stale` flag (now - updated > threshold) computed every refresh. The background/shared health-check knobs only make sense for probing remote network deployments across proxy workers, which Shepherd explicitly is not (no network, no proxy, single user, filesystem is ground truth). Adding a separate cached health-state layer would be redundant with sessionFree plus the existing stale/error/hung/escalation detection, and would invite stale-belief bugs against an architecture whose whole point is re-reading truth each ~1s tick. The one sliver of novel value — a short failure cool-down so the dispatcher doesn't immediately re-feed a just-failed/flapping session — is a tiny, optional enhancement to routePick, not a justification to import this feature; if ever wanted it should be a few lines keyed off the existing pending-feed/error signals, not a "health cache" subsystem.
- **Session/deployment affinity (sticky routing)** — DONT_ADD wins on scope mismatch. The feature exists only to solve a problem created by the proxy's own architecture — splitting one conversation across many equivalent model deployments — and Shepherd has neither the deployments, the proxy, nor stateless requests. In Shepherd a session is the durable conversation; continuity and cache warmth are properties of the live process, achieved for free, with nothing to "route back to." The against case is structural and absolute; the for case is real but reduces to a minor, optional tiebreak inside the already-shipped, off-by-default `routePick` dispatcher (prefer the session that handled a project's last routed task), which is NOT this LiteLLM feature and lands in an area the team deliberately deferred as UX-blocked. Porting the affinity/TTL-map machinery would be cargo-culting proxy plumbing. If the continuity tiebreak is ever wanted, it's a small native extension to existing primitives, tracked separately — not an adoption of this candidate.
- **Complexity router (zero-API rule-based tier routing)** — This is a per-request, cost-at-scale proxy optimization whose only home is an HTTP gateway routing many requests across model deployments — precisely the multi-tenant-proxy shape that is DONT_ADD for a local single-user GUI console. Shepherd already gives the human the controls this feature would automate: a provider/model picker, spawn presets that pin a provider, and live /model + /effort switches. The model choice in Shepherd is a deliberate per-SESSION human decision, not a per-PROMPT machine guess. The strongest adaptation — score queued/pasted prompt text and surface a tier hint — fails the fit test because (a) Shepherd's routing primitive load-balances FREE sessions, not models, so there's no clean primitive to extend; (b) acting on the verdict would require relaunching a session (different-provider models can't be hot-swapped mid-session, per the code), defeating the sub-ms deterministic-route premise; and (c) the operator's stated intent is a strictly better signal than a regex tier-guess, so the hint is at best decorative and at worst trust-eroding noise. The deterministic/offline nature is appealing but a mechanism's purity doesn't create a need. Confidence is high: the value lives entirely in the proxy/at-scale economics that Shepherd's hard scope excludes.
- **Owner-cache attribution (conversation -> first-picked model, with skip tracking)** — DONT_ADD on the "redundant/irrelevant to a non-existent feature" branch. The against-case is decisive: this is a bug-fix for an automated bandit router that Shepherd does not have and whose entire premise (software auto-electing models per request, learning from per-turn rewards) contradicts Shepherd's design where the human picks the model explicitly and intra-session provider changes are gated behind a relaunch. With no automatic credit assignment, there is no cross-model smearing to prevent — the failure mode the feature exists to fix cannot occur here. The for-case only lands if Shepherd first builds learned/automatic routing, which is itself the proxy-side optimization that is out of scope; you cannot ADAPT a guard whose protected feature is absent without smuggling that feature in too. The user's stated interest (saved agents, skills, MCP management) is about reusable spawn profiles, not automated model optimization, so it gives this no favorable weight. The skip-counting telemetry is a thin, generic pattern Shepherd already practices and not a reason to import the rest.
- **Caller-supplied quality-floor override (min_quality_tier header/metadata)** — This is a per-request router-override for a multi-tenant proxy, and Shepherd has no router, no per-request interception, and no caller separate from the single operator — the three things the feature exists to coordinate. A session is bound to one model for life via ANTHROPIC_MODEL, so there is nothing to "override" at request time and no cost-saving auto-downgrade to floor against. The genuine residual need — guaranteeing a high-stakes session runs an Opus-class model — is already covered by the provider picker, spawn presets (which carry a provider), and the live /model switch. The one arguably-new idea (a declarative quality-tier number on provider profiles that routing/respawn won't drop below) is a thin, speculative extension of features Shepherd already has, and project routing routes to a free SESSION rather than choosing a model, so even there the fit is awkward. The named transport (x-litellm-min-quality-tier header / min_quality_tier metadata) is squarely the proxy plumbing that Shepherd's scope rules out. Net: scope mismatch plus largely-redundant with the existing provider/spawn controls.
- **Semantic auto-router (embedding intent routing)** — Two of the three load-bearing pieces are disqualifying for a local single-user GUI console: the embedding-model call (banned API/inference plumbing) and the per-prompt→per-model selection (Shepherd has no prompt-intercept seam — sessions own their loop; model is set once at spawn). What survives is only a vague "match work to the right target" need, and Shepherd already serves the explicit-choice version of that (provider picker, spawn presets, project-routing queues). Auto-classifying prose to a model is the nondeterministic opposite of Shepherd's design contract. The favorably-weighted saved-agents/skills interest is about spawning from an explicit saved PROFILE, not embedding-driven auto-routing — so even that interest doesn't pull this in. The genuine kernel (content-aware spawn suggestion) is a different, much smaller, embedding-free feature; building the SemanticRouter machinery to reach it is unjustified scope creep. DONT_ADD.
- **Quality-tier router (route to a target quality tier with keyword override)** — This is a multi-deployment HTTP-proxy load-balancing feature: classify a request's complexity, map it to a quality tier, and silently select/cost-optimize across competing model deployments. Every load-bearing piece assumes a proxy that intercepts requests and chooses the model — none of which exists in Shepherd, which doesn't proxy, doesn't make API calls, and treats model/tier as a deliberate human selection. The only salvageable kernel ("spawn the right saved-agent profile for a job") is already covered by the provider picker + spawn presets + the 4c-E dispatcher, and dressing those up with auto-classification + cheapest-tie-break would actively fight Shepherd's human-in-the-loop, off-by-default design. Scope mismatch on the parts that matter, redundant on the parts that don't. DONT_ADD.
- **Adaptive router — Thompson-sampling bandit over (request_type, model)** — Scope and unit-of-work mismatch, decisive on both axes. (1) Shepherd's atom is a session, not a request; it never sees prompt text (status-file only, no proxy), so the prompt classifier and per-request reward — the entire mechanism of the bandit — have no input. (2) The model is a human spawn-time choice held stable within a session; there is no per-call re-routing surface to optimize, unlike a proxy that selects a deployment on every call. (3) No quality reward exists: the ledger tracks status/turns/approvals, none of which is a per-(type,model) success label, and there is no way to manufacture one without the request body and an outcome judgment Shepherd cannot make. (4) The implementation (Postgres, mutating Beta posteriors, 10s flushes, stochastic resampling) collides head-on with the load-bearing invariants: pure/deterministic cc-core, injected-now-only, JSONL-only opt-in storage, and the deterministic routePick. The user's interest in better model/agent leverage is genuine, but it's satisfied by a DETERMINISTIC, explicit advisory (e.g. "this folder usually runs Opus" preset suggestions), not by a stochastic multi-armed bandit over a request space that doesn't exist here. Even the FOR case has to invent a request stream and a reward signal that Shepherd structurally lacks. This is API-proxy load-balancing plumbing for a multi-deployment fleet — DONT_ADD.
- **Background async-response polling store (background=true responses)** — Against wins decisively. The feature's mechanism is multi-tenant proxy plumbing (polling_id, Redis-stored ResponsesAPI JSON with TTL, a background task that streams the provider) — every piece of which is excluded by Shepherd's hard scope (no network, no proxy, no API calls, no shared store, single-user-local). More importantly, the only transferable idea — "kick off long work, get back a handle, poll its lifecycle state, cancel it" — Shepherd ALREADY implements in its own primitives: a spawned Claude Code session is the long-running unit; the ~1s refresh loop polls ~/.claude/cc-status/<session_id>.json and surfaces idle/working/approval/done/error (a direct analog of queued/in_progress/completed/failed/cancelled); stop is cancel; the queue/router already hands work off with delivery tracking; and the SSH bridge already mirror-polls remote long-running sessions and approves/denies headlessly. So this is both a scope mismatch AND redundant-with-what-Shepherd-has. There is no residual need to ADAPT — doing so would just re-label the existing session-status filesystem.

### Prompt & Config Mgmt
- **Persisted UI feature flags (UISettings) auto-rendered settings registry** — Two independent grounds for DONT_ADD converge. (1) Scope mismatch: the substance of UISettings is per-role/per-user-class access control (hide-from-non-admins, disable-for-internal-users) and header-forwarding — concepts that are meaningless in a single-user local GUI with no roles, no network, and no secrets storage. (2) Already have it: stripped of the multi-tenant flags, the residue is "persisted, described, form-rendered settings," which Shepherd already implements via cc-config.json, the open-settings/save-config round-trip, overlayConfig/SETTINGS_KEEP_SUBKEYS, and the ⚙ overlay — including the live-reapply-without-reload behavior that DualCache's TTL is meant to provide. The one transferable idea — auto-rendering the form from a typed+described flag registry to kill read/write drift — is a refactor of a working system, not a missing capability, and Shepherd's most important controls (provider cards, the headless meta-toggle, hand-edit-only risk.weights kept out of the form on purpose) actively resist generic schema rendering. Weighing the user's stated interest (saved agents / skills / MCP registries) doesn't rescue this: that interest is about reusable session-spawn profiles, not about an admin-console access-control flag system. Net: the need is met and the new framing is multi-tenant. If the drift between showSettings/persistSettings ever becomes painful, that's a small internal cleanup tracked on its own merits, not a reason to import UISettings.
