- **Store-config-in-DB / live config edit + persist (general settings editor, hot-reload)** — Redundant — Shepherd already has the whole feature, and better-fitted to its primitives than the LiteLLM version. The live-edit-merge-persist-hot-reload loop is implemented by the Settings overlay + core.overlayConfig + FX.writeFile + the loadConfig()-every-tick refresh. The merge even respects the same "patch named sub-trees, don't clobber" discipline LiteLLM's save_config() embodies, plus a deliberate keep-subkeys policy to protect hand-edited blocks. The remaining LiteLLM-specific element, the DB store (STORE_MODEL_IN_DB / LiteLLM_Config table), is a multi-replica proxy concern that directly contradicts Shepherd's NO-network/single-user/single-file scope, so there is nothing left to ADD or even ADAPT. If anything, LiteLLM is catching up to a pattern Shepherd already ships.

### Health & Ops
- **Hanging / stuck request watchdog** — This is the canonical "already have it" case, with high confidence. Shepherd's escalation.hung watchdog is not a rough cousin of the LiteLLM feature — it is the same algorithm reshaped to local primitives: poll loop, threshold, progress-resets-the-timer, one-alert-per-incident with re-arm, TTL/reset on incident end, fan-out to ledger + notifications + push. The scope brief explicitly lists "escalation/hung watchdog, stale detection" under WHAT SHEPHERD ALREADY HAS and instructs DONT_ADD for redundancy not worth extending. The one sliver the LiteLLM version adds — watching a request that was dispatched but never picked up — is HTTP-proxy request-lifecycle bookkeeping with no local equivalent (Shepherd issues no request ids; it reads hook-written status off disk), and the genuine local gap it gestures at (a session that wedges before writing any transcript bytes, leaving lastProgressTs nil) is already covered at a coarser grain by 90s stale display + the auto-respawn mid-turn-freeze death threshold. There is no need worth a new feature here; at most it is a tiny future tweak to the existing watchdog (seed lastProgressTs on the working transition so a zero-progress hang can arm), which belongs as an incremental hardening of Feature 8, not as importing a new LiteLLM capability.
- **Liveness probe (GET /health/liveliness)** — The against case wins decisively. The feature's reason to exist — an unauthenticated HTTP endpoint an external orchestrator polls to restart a dead worker — collides directly with two hard scope limits (NO network server) and the architectural fact that Shepherd is not itself a supervised daemon and has nothing orchestrating it. There is no orchestrator above Shepherd to consume a 200/503, and the "worker" abstraction (a fungible restartable replica) does not apply to interactive local GUI sessions. The legitimate kernel inside the FOR steelman — "Shepherd should detect when it has itself wedged and recover" — is real but is a different, non-HTTP feature (an internal self-watchdog / refresh-loop heartbeat), and even that is largely covered already by macOS process supervision (login item), `make doctor`, and the always-visible refresh loop; the session-level recovery need is met by the hung-watchdog + auto-respawn. Adding a listening port would also introduce an open unauthenticated network surface on a single-user machine that explicitly stores no secrets and exposes nothing — a net negative. This is API-proxy plumbing whose underlying need is already satisfied locally by existing primitives.
- **Readiness probe with DB status (GET /health/readiness, public + authed details)** — This is a textbook scope mismatch wrapped around a need Shepherd already satisfies. The feature's reason to exist — telling an external load balancer to drain a node, with anonymous-public vs authenticated tiers — requires the three things Shepherd explicitly forbids: a network server, multi-user/auth, and a remote dependency (a DB) to probe. None of those exist or should. Strip the HTTP/LB framing and the residual concept ("is my backing source reachable and fresh, distinct from 'am I running'") is real, but Shepherd already implements exactly that distinction in the right idioms: the `bridgeStale` flag is literally a readiness-vs-liveness split (lastOkTs + 3×interval), `make doctor` reports dependency health with hard-vs-degraded tiers, per-session `stale` plus the watchdog cover source freshness and hangs, and the `.panel-alive` heartbeat is the liveness marker. So the underlying need is not unmet — it's met by several existing primitives. The only thing the feature could legitimately add is cosmetic: rolling those scattered signals into one in-panel health readout. That is a polish item, not worth importing a proxy feature for, and would mostly re-surface data already wired. Verdict is DONT_ADD on both grounds the rubric names: scope mismatch (network/multi-user/HTTP-proxy) AND redundant-with-what-Shepherd-has. It does not advance the user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest. High confidence: the matching primitives are present and read directly in the code.
- **In-flight backlog / queue-depth gauge (GET /health/backlog)** — Two independent reasons converge on DONT_ADD. (1) Scope mismatch: the literal feature — counting concurrent HTTP requests in middleware to gauge per-pod worker load — is meaningful only for a multi-tenant network proxy, exactly the architecture Shepherd's hard scope limits exclude (no server, no requests, no pods). (2) Even after charitably reshaping the intent to "fleet load/backlog pressure," Shepherd already has it: the status-by-state tally in the fleet footer answers "how much is in flight (working/approval)," and core.queueDepth + the routing dispatcher answer "how much is queued/backed up." There is no residual need a /health/backlog gauge would fill that the existing footer + queue-depth primitives don't. The for-side intent is real but fully covered, so this is "redundant with what Shepherd already has and not worth extending."
- **Graceful drain endpoint + in-flight-aware shutdown manager** — This is pure API-proxy / Health & Ops plumbing for a networked multi-user server: an HTTP endpoint, a network token in secret storage, load-balancer rotation, and a SIGTERM request-counter drain tied to a K8s preStop hook. Every one of those is an explicit Shepherd non-goal (no network server, no secrets storage, no proxy, no multi-user). It is also redundant: the genuine "finish in-flight work before stopping" instinct already exists as Shepherd's drain (`shouldDrainClose`, off by default, in-memory per-tile intent, fires on the `prev≠done → done` transition) and is reinforced by auto-respawn deliberately not killing a mid-turn `working` session. The only transferable nugget — a bounded "don't wait forever" timeout fallback so an armed-but-hung session eventually closes — is a one-line hardening of the existing drain, not a reason to import the endpoint/token/manager machinery. Verdict is DONT_ADD on both grounds (scope mismatch AND already-have-it). The latent timeout improvement, if ever wanted, belongs as a small extension to the existing drain feature, not as this candidate.
- **Background health-check loop (periodic cached results) + per-deployment opt-out + history persistence + write-on-change + multi-pod coordination** — The feature's center of gravity — a periodic, cached, overlap-aware health loop with on-change history — is redundant with infrastructure Shepherd already ships (1 Hz refresh + pathwatcher, stale flagging, hung/escalation watchdog, and an event-driven JSONL ledger that is already write-on-change). The one differentiating capability, multi-pod Redis leader-election so only one replica probes while others read shared cache, is squarely out of scope: Shepherd is a single-user, single-host macOS console with NO network server and no replicas, so the coordination half is meaningless here (the SSH bridge mirrors remote tiles by pull, not leader election, and isn't a fit either). What's left after removing the redundant loop and the out-of-scope coordination is two minor optimizations — a per-session watchdog opt-out and an age-backstopped write throttle — neither of which rises to importing this feature; if Adam ever wants them they're sub-line tweaks to the existing watchdog/ledger, not this LiteLLM mechanism. This lands as DONT_ADD on the 'redundant with what Shepherd already has + partly out-of-scope' grounds, not as a need Shepherd is missing.
- **Integration/callback service health probe (GET /health/services)** — The against side wins decisively. The feature's defining payload — pinging cloud observability/alerting sinks via instantiated loggers behind an HTTP admin route — collides with all three of Shepherd's hardest scope limits at once (no network server, no API calls, no secrets). Those sinks are artifacts of a multi-tenant proxy's telemetry stack; a local single-user GUI has no analog to probe. Strip the feature down to the only part that COULD fit Shepherd — "report whether my dependencies are reachable" — and it is already shipped: `make doctor`/`install.sh --tools-only` plus `resolveBin` detect-use-degrade exactly the local CLIs Shepherd depends on, and the SSH bridge plus gate already surface their own reachability failures inline. The user's stated MCP/agents interest doesn't rescue it: a future "is this MCP server reachable" check would be a distinct, narrowly-scoped local-process probe, not a port of a telemetry-sink prober, and recommending this card would mis-anchor that work to a network/secrets shape Shepherd must avoid. Scope mismatch plus redundancy with an existing capability = DONT_ADD. If anything is worth a future nudge, it's folding MCP-server presence into the existing `make doctor` panel — a separate decision, not this feature.
- **Distributed cron-job leader election (LiteLLM_CronJob + PodLockManager)** — This is a textbook scope mismatch. Leader election only earns its keep when N>1 identical processes can run the same scheduled job; Shepherd is N=1 by design and forbidden from becoming a fleet (no network server, no multi-user). The genuine underlying concern — an autonomous timed action firing twice — is already handled, and handled better for this context, by Shepherd's existing single-process edge-detection (prev[key] swap each refresh) and bounded per-folder budgets, which are pure, deterministic, and unit-tested in cc-core. Adding a lease/lock primitive would import Redis/DB dependencies and distributed-systems failure modes (clock skew, split-brain, stale TTL) to solve a race that cannot occur. None of the user's stated future interests (saved agents, skills registry, MCP management) touch this either. There is no adaptation that survives contact with Shepherd's primitives — the mechanism IS the network-coordination it can't have.

### Caching
- **Per-request cache control (no-cache / no-store / ttl / s-maxage / namespace)** — This is pure API-proxy plumbing for a multi-tenant model-serving layer. Shepherd has no inference request path and no response cache to apply per-request directives to — it reads local status JSON written by hooks and never calls the API. Every directive in the feature (no-cache/no-store/ttl/s-maxage/namespace) presupposes a proxy serving completions, which is squarely outside Shepherd's hard scope. The only local hook (a manual "force refresh" on the ledger memo / folder index) is a different, trivial feature that doesn't need this feature's design and isn't worth dressing up in cache-control semantics. Scope mismatch on the dominant axis; DONT_ADD.
- **Pluggable cache backends (local/Redis/Redis-Cluster/S3/disk/GCS/Azure Blob) + namespacing + dual-cache + supported-call-type/default-on-off modes** — Pure scope mismatch. This is a proxy-side response cache, and Shepherd is categorically not a proxy: it observes and steers local Claude Code sessions off the filesystem and never issues an inference request, so there is no response to memoize. The against case is decisive — the feature's entire reason to exist (avoid a provider round-trip the cache owner would otherwise make) does not apply to Shepherd. The for case collapses on inspection: the latency/cost win belongs to whoever makes the call (Claude Code + the Anthropic API's own prompt cache), and Shepherd already does the only relevant thing in its lane — accounts for cache tokens in usage rollups. The network backends and multi-tenant namespacing directly violate the no-network/no-multi-user/no-secrets limits, and supported-call-types covers operations (embedding/rerank/transcription) Shepherd never runs. Even the strongest salvage — a local diskcache for repeated prompts — has no host: there is no Shepherd-owned inference path to wrap. The user's stated interest (saved agents / skills / MCP) is orthogonal; nothing here advances it. No primitive to ADAPT into, no real need to reshape.
- **Semantic (similarity-threshold) caching** — This is a textbook scope mismatch. Semantic caching is a proxy/request-path optimization: embed the inbound prompt, serve a prior completion when cosine similarity clears a threshold. Every one of those verbs requires being the thing that handles the LLM request — which Shepherd is architecturally forbidden from being (no network server, no API calls, no proxy; status is read off the local filesystem). Shepherd never possesses the prompt or the response to cache, so the core function is inapplicable, and the LiteLLM backing (Redis-vector / Qdrant + an embedding model) directly violates the no-network and no-secrets hard limits. The user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest is favorable for spawn-from-profile features, not for response caching — even the most generous reframe (flag semantically-similar saved templates) is a distinct, low-value local-dedup feature that wouldn't cache anything or save tokens, so it doesn't rescue this candidate. DONT_ADD with high confidence.
- **Cache administration: health-check/ping, key delete / flushall, redis info, DB-backed config + self-describing settings form + connection test** — Scope mismatch on the dominant capability, plus double violation of hard limits. The feature exists to administer a network Redis cache that stores proxied LLM responses for many tenants — Shepherd has no proxy, no network server, no cache backend, and explicitly stores no secrets, so ping/delete/flushall/redis-info/encrypted-DB-settings/connection-test have no referent here. The only genuinely portable ideas are (a) a typed self-describing settings schema and (b) validate-before-save, but (a) is a cosmetic refactor of an already-shipped, working Settings UI (not worth the churn against its load-bearing invariants), and (b) already exists in spirit as `make doctor`/`resolveBin` dependency probing. Neither rises to ADAPT: there's no real unmet local NEED here, just two thin patterns Shepherd has good-enough equivalents of, attached to a capability (server cache admin) that is pure DONT_ADD. It also doesn't advance the user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest. Verdict: DONT_ADD, high confidence — this is the textbook multi-tenant-proxy feature the scope rule names.

### Admin UI
- **Per-page UI access control (sidebar visibility)** — This is a textbook multi-tenant RBAC feature: its purpose is gating page visibility per user role (enabled_ui_pages_internal_users, falling back to role permissions). Shepherd is a single-operator local console with no users, no roles, and no admin/non-admin split — the premise is structurally absent and explicitly out of scope. There's nothing to "adapt" because the underlying NEED (limit what OTHER people can see) does not exist for one person who owns the whole config. The only salvageable fragment — a personal "which views show in my ☰ drawer" preference — is a different and minor feature that Shepherd already largely addresses via the drawer collapse, and hiding your own audit/gate/usage views would undercut the oversight the tool exists to provide. DONT_ADD on scope mismatch (multi-user) plus partial redundancy with the existing toolbar drawer.
- **UI theming: custom logo + favicon (with upload)** — This is the canonical multi-tenant-proxy / hosted-admin-console feature: its whole purpose is letting a vendor re-brand a product that other people log into over a network. Shepherd is a local single-user macOS webview with no server, no favicon, no tenants, and no audience to brand for — so the core need the feature serves does not exist here. The superficially similar thing Shepherd already has (the cards/bar/contrast/dots theme switcher) is a layout/density selector, not white-labeling, and stretching it into "custom logo + upload" would import server-side image storage/serving for zero single-user benefit. The against case dominates decisively on scope. Not worth adapting; there is no real local need hiding inside it.
- **In-product nudges / onboarding survey gating** — Scope mismatch wins decisively. The feature is a multi-tenant SaaS growth surface (survey/feedback funnel driven by cross-user tagged spend through a proxy), which is precisely the network/multi-user/proxy shape Shepherd's hard scope limits exclude. The only transferable kernel — Claude Code usage detection — is something Shepherd does better and more directly (status files vs. User-Agent-tag inference), so it adds nothing. The suppress flag is real-but-empty: Shepherd already ships quiet-by-default with notification history and no vendor nudges, so there is nothing to gate. Weighting the user's stated interests (saved agents / skills / MCP) does not rescue this — it's orthogonal to all of them. No adaptation produces a feature Shepherd lacks; it would be inventing a popup just to add a switch to hide it.

### Provider/Proxy Core
- **Custom LLM provider plugin (register your own backend)** — This is pure provider/proxy-core SDK plumbing with no analog in a tool that doesn't make API calls. The real underlying need — "run a Claude Code session against a non-Anthropic or local backend I control" — is ALREADY met by the existing gateway provider profile, whose own code comments cite LiteLLM as the intended target: Claude Code itself is the client, and you point it at any OpenAI-compatible/local endpoint via `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`. Shepherd never dispatches completions, so there is nothing for a registered `CustomLLM` to plug into. Implementing the LiteLLM feature would require Shepherd to become an in-process request runtime hosting operator backend code and (inevitably) auth — exactly the proxy/secrets scope the project forbids. The user's stated interest in saved agents/skills/MCPs is about spawn-from-a-saved-profile against Claude Code sessions, not about authoring a new LLM backend; that interest is served by the provider picker that already exists, not by this. Verdict: DONT_ADD as redundant-with-gateway-profile plus a hard scope mismatch.
- **OpenAI-compatible generic endpoint (openai_like / litellm_proxy / hosted_vllm / lm_studio / llamafile)** — Pure redundancy plus a scope-mismatch on the part that isn't redundant. The desirable half (generic base_url endpoint, no per-provider code, secrets-via-env-only) is already shipped as the gateway provider kind in cc-core.lua (providerEnv/providerById/providerByModel) and documented in the README as supporting precisely vLLM/LM Studio/Ollama/LiteLLM. There is nothing to extend: a new session against a local OpenAI-shaped server is literally a gateway profile pointed at a LiteLLM/compatible base_url today. The remaining half — LiteLLM's server-side OpenAI<->provider request/response transformation — is API-proxy plumbing that contradicts Shepherd's hard limits (no network server, no proxy, no API calls). Shepherd doesn't talk to the model at all; Claude Code does. So there is no place for transformation code to live. Verdict: DONT_ADD, already have it; the not-yet-had part is out of scope by design.
- **Video generation lifecycle (generate/status/content/remix/extend/edit + characters)** — Hard scope mismatch on every axis. The feature's whole existence is making outbound API calls to a video-generation provider and shuttling the resulting media — Shepherd explicitly never proxies or makes API calls and never stores artifacts; it reads local session status JSON. There is no Shepherd primitive (session/hook/gate/ledger/spawn/queue/provider) this reshapes onto: the "async job" similarity is too thin to be a real adaptation, because Shepherd's async unit is a local OS process surfaced via filesystem hooks, not a remote provider job ID polled over HTTP. The user's favored future direction (saved agents, skills registry, MCP management for handing off Claude-Code work) is unrelated to media generation. Nothing to build, nothing to extend.
- **Built-in mock/test responses across endpoints** — Two independent kill conditions both fire. (1) Scope mismatch: the feature is pure API-proxy plumbing — it fakes provider response objects for endpoints Shepherd has no analog of (Shepherd controls local GUI sessions; the "agent" is a running session, not an HTTP route). (2) Redundant: the legitimate need (test without real side effects) is already met by the fx dependency-injection seam — cc-core is pure and tests swap in a recorder/fake fx. The user's stated future interest (saved agents, skills registry, MCP mgmt) is about reusable spawn profiles, not test mocking, so the favorable-weighting clause doesn't rescue this candidate. No reshaping into a Shepherd primitive yields net value over what fx_recorder already provides.
- **Model-group aliasing / wildcard routing** — The candidate is, at its core, multi-deployment request routing for a multi-caller proxy — squarely inside Shepherd's hard scope exclusions (no proxy, no API calls, no router). The one transferable idea (a friendly handle decoupled from the raw backend) is already implemented as provider profiles, where label provides exactly that 1:1 abstraction over model+baseUrl, and /model switching plus respawn matching key off real ids by design. The distinctive parts of model_group_alias — 1:name->MANY, '*' wildcards, load-balance/fallback — have no consumer in a single-user GUI where each session is bound to one chosen backend. The favorable future interest (saved agents/skills/MCPs) is better served by an explicit agent-profile primitive that references an existing provider id directly; it does not need a separate alias-routing layer, and bolting one on would add indirection to the spawn/respawn paths (complicating providerByModel) for no behavioral win. Redundant-with-provider-profiles plus proxy-only routing semantics = DONT_ADD.
- **Reusable named credentials store (encrypted + masked display)** — Split the feature into its two halves. (1) The encrypted-credential-store core (CredentialsRepository persisting encrypted values, _get_masked_values redaction, by-name CRUD over a secrets table) is a flat scope violation and a multi-tenant-proxy concern: Shepherd stores NO secrets by explicit, documented design — it stores the NAME of an env var and lets the login shell expand it, so there is literally nothing to encrypt or mask. That half is DONT_ADD. (2) The underlying NEED — define a reusable named connection/auth bundle once, reference it by name from multiple deployments — is real and lands on a primitive Shepherd already has (provider profiles), and aligns with the user's stated "saved/reusable definitions" interest. So this is ADAPT, not DONT_ADD: keep the named-reference idea, reshape the values to Shepherd's NON-SECRET form (authTokenEnv env-var names, baseUrl, headers, model, contextLimit), and drop encryption/masking entirely. Verdict ADAPT, but a deliberately thin one — confidence high that the secrets machinery must be cut, medium that even the DRY layer clears the bar given how short the config is.
- **OCR / document understanding endpoint** — This is the cleanest DONT_ADD in the set. The feature is pure provider-API plumbing: ocr()/aocr() make an outbound HTTP call to a model deployment, require a stored/forwarded API key, and do request/response transformation — the exact three things context.md line 13 forbids ("No network, no multi-user, no secrets... does NOT proxy or make API calls"). The "FOR" steelman collapses on inspection: the controlled Claude Code sessions ALREADY read PDFs and images natively (reference a path or paste an image — Shepherd already supports image paste via parseDataUrl), so a separate Mistral OCR proxy is redundant with a capability the sessions have, not additive. It also maps to none of the user's favorably-weighted interests (saved agents, skills registry, MCP management) — none of those are an OCR proxy. Adapting it to a local "drop a PDF, extract text into the queue" utility would either require Shepherd to make the very API call it is defined not to make (scope breach), or reduce to "tell the session to read the file" — which is just the existing spawn/nudge/queue path, needing no new feature. Either way there is nothing worth building. Verdict: DONT_ADD on scope mismatch (network + secret + proxy) compounded by redundancy.
- **Web search endpoint (provider-native search) with its own cost calc** — Textbook DONT_ADD on two of the three disqualifiers. (1) Scope mismatch: the feature IS an outbound HTTP call to a paid provider plus normalization plus billing — the proxy plumbing Shepherd explicitly is not. It would require a network client, an API key (banned by the no-secrets rule), and per-call cost math for a request Shepherd never makes. (2) Redundant framing: web search already exists as Claude Code's own WebSearch tool, invoked by the session, not the console; Shepherd's legitimate job toward it is gating/observing, and Shepherd already gates arbitrary tools and ledgers their cost via hooks. The cost-attribution value the FOR side imagines is already covered by the existing token/usage tracking and ledger — if you wanted per-search cost in the rollup, that's a ledger/insights enrichment fed by hook payloads, not a search endpoint. There is no version of "Shepherd runs search()/asearch()" that respects the no-network/no-secrets/no-proxy invariants. The user's agents/skills/MCP interest does not rescue it: that interest is about spawning sessions from saved profiles and managing local MCP servers, not about the console itself calling a remote search API. Verdict stands at DONT_ADD with high confidence; this is the clearest kind of proxy-only feature.
- **Secrets-manager config override (HashiCorp Vault) with test-connection** — The against case is overwhelming and the for case is thin. The feature's entire purpose is to manage where a network proxy retrieves secrets — Shepherd has no proxy, makes no API calls, and by explicit invariant stores no secrets, instead deferring credential resolution to the spawned shell ($VAR). Persisting a Vault token/approle would make Shepherd a secrets holder; a live httpx test-connection would make it an outbound network client. Both are categorically out of scope and not extensions of anything Shepherd has. The only salvageable grain — "validate a provider profile before saving it" — does NOT require Vault, secret storage, or HTTP from Shepherd: if that UX is ever wanted, it would be a separate, much smaller idea (e.g. a local dry-run spawn or a shell-side `$VAR present?` check), not this feature. The candidate as specified is DONT_ADD on scope mismatch (secrets + network + proxy-config), and the user's stated interest (saved agents / skills / MCP) gives it no lift because none of those are about credential vaults.
- **Unified provider abstraction (model-prefix routing to 150+ providers) + per-endpoint param mapping/capability introspection** — The candidate is LiteLLM's reason-for-being: a request/response transformation + routing engine that turns one call shape into a native provider API call. Shepherd's hard scope (context.md, confirmed in code) is "does NOT proxy or make API calls" — the only HTTP it does is a metadata OAuth usage GET, an ntfy POST, and a leaderboard claim; there is no transform layer, no Messages-API client, nowhere for BaseConfig/get_llm_provider to plug in. The harness is permanently Claude Code, which owns all provider talking, so transform_request/map_openai_params have no caller here. Shepherd already has the only slice of this that fits its model — provider profiles (incl. an explicit LiteLLM gateway kind), live /model switching, and a per-provider contextLimit override — so the genuinely useful residue (knowing a gateway's context window / capabilities) is already addressable as config and is at most a small ADAPT (a capability/window lookup table), NOT this framework. Adopting the engine would also drag in key forwarding, violating the NO-secrets invariant. This is textbook Provider/Proxy Core plumbing for a multi-backend proxy: DONT_ADD. The user's provider/MCP/agents interest is real but is served by extending the existing profile/spawn primitives, not by importing a proxy's routing core.
- **Multi-modal image endpoints: generation / edit / variation** — Textbook DONT_ADD on scope mismatch. The feature is provider/proxy core: outbound, key-bearing, cross-provider HTTP normalization of an images API. Every one of those properties is on Shepherd's hard-exclude list (no network server, no secrets, no API calls, not a proxy). The superficial "Shepherd handles images" overlap is a false friend — Shepherd's image path is clipboard-IN to a chat input (a nudge attachment via FX.writeImageTemp/pasteIntoWindow), the opposite of prompt-to-image generation, and shares no code or concept with an images endpoint. It plugs into none of the local primitives, and the user's stated interest (saved agents / skills registry / MCP management spawned as local sessions) is unrelated to image-generation APIs. There is no honest ADAPT: to adapt it you would have to build the network/proxy/secrets machinery the project was deliberately built to avoid.
- **Rerank endpoint (Cohere-style document reranking)** — Pure API-proxy plumbing with a direct collision against three of Shepherd's hard scope limits (no network calls, no secrets, no proxy). rerank() is not a capability Shepherd lacks — it's an HTTP normalization shim for hosted rerank vendors, which is meaningless in an app that never makes API calls. The superficially adjacent need ("order a list by relevance") is already covered locally and deterministically by fuzzyFilter, the status RANK sort, and rg search ranking — none of which want a remote semantic model, and a single user controlling a small local fleet gains nothing from one. Even the favorably-weighted future interest (agents/skills/MCP registry) does not rescue it: picking a saved agent profile is a small-N local fuzzy/keyword match, not a RAG reranking problem, and wiring a paid Cohere/Jina call to choose among a handful of local profiles is over-engineering that breaks the offline, no-secrets, degrades-gracefully contract. Scope mismatch, decisively.
- **Vector store CRUD + search + config registry** — This is textbook proxy/multi-tenant scope creep. The feature's value lives entirely in making provider API calls, storing per-resource credentials, and routing tool calls to backends — three things Shepherd is constitutionally forbidden from doing (no API calls, no secrets storage, no proxy routing). The lone redeeming thread is the user's interest in a saved-agent/skills/MCP registry, but that interest is satisfied by a local catalog of session-spawn profiles (which folder, which provider env, which MCPs to enable on launch), NOT by Shepherd becoming a vector-store API client that holds credentials. If anything from the RAG world ever belongs in Shepherd, it is at most "a saved agent profile may list MCP/RAG servers to spawn with" — and that lives in the future MCP/agent-profile work, not in copying LiteLLM's managed-vector-store CRUD+search+credentials machinery. Copying this feature as-is would require building HTTP provider clients and a secrets vault, directly contradicting context.md. The need this serves (knowledge retrieval for agents) is real but is owned by Claude Code's own config, not by the fleet console. Clear DONT_ADD.
- **Batch API (async bulk job submit/retrieve/cancel/list)** — This is a textbook DONT_ADD: a multi-provider HTTP proxy primitive that requires the three things Shepherd is constitutionally defined against — making network/API calls, proxying to model deployments, and holding provider secrets. Shepherd's "agent" is a running Claude Code GUI session driven by keystrokes against a local window, not an HTTP route or a server-side inference job. The only real overlap (bulk task fan-out) is already covered by primitives Shepherd has and shipped: per-session queue, bulk-paste (queueSplitLines/queuePushAll/templates), and the 4c-E level-triggered project routing dispatcher that distributes queued work to free sessions. If the user ever wants "submit a backlog, walk away," that NEED is satisfied by extending the existing local queue/dispatcher — NOT by importing a provider Batch API. Adopting the LiteLLM feature as-is, or even adapting its mechanism, would mean building a network/secrets/proxy layer that contradicts the project's core invariant. The user's stated future interests (saved agents, skills, MCP) are orthogonal to this and weigh in favorably for *other* candidates, not this one.
- **Files API with unified file IDs across providers (multi-target managed files)** — DONT_ADD on scope mismatch. The candidate is provider/proxy core: a model-input file store that abstracts over multiple provider backends behind a single id and persists bytes to pluggable cloud storage. Shepherd's hard limits forbid all three pillars — no network server, no secrets, no API proxying — and it never uploads files to any model (the spawned Claude Code session does that itself through the shell). The unified-cross-provider-id mapping is meaningless without a request path to map onto, which Shepherd deliberately lacks. The only sympathetic reframe (a local reusable-attachments locker tied to the saved-agents idea) is a different, much smaller feature that reuses Shepherd's existing recent-dirs/label persistence and the existing clipboard image-paste effect; it would NOT adopt this feature's architecture, so this is not an ADAPT of the candidate — it's a reason to decline the candidate and, if ever wanted, build something unrelated. No part of the actual feature plugs into sessions, hooks, the gate, ledger, spawn, or queue.
- **Fine-tuning job management** — This fails the fit question on every axis and trips all three of Shepherd's hard scope limits at once. It requires a network client making outbound API calls (Shepherd makes none — it reads session JSON off the local filesystem), it requires storing/forwarding provider API keys (Shepherd never stores secrets, only resolves $ENV via the spawned shell), and it requires a normalized cross-provider HTTP API (Shepherd is not a proxy). Crucially it has no mapping to ANY Shepherd primitive: a fine-tuning job is not a session, has no hook-written status file, no window to drive, no permission to gate, and produces nothing for the ledger or shift report. The user's saved-agents/skills/MCP interest is about spawning local CC sessions from reusable profiles — that is orthogonal to provider model training. The superficial overlap with the existing Providers feature is a category error: Shepherd's provider picker only injects ANTHROPIC_MODEL/BASE_URL env into a spawned shell; it does not call provider APIs. There is no version of this that is "reshaped to local sessions" — the entire value is the multi-tenant proxy plumbing. DONT_ADD, scope mismatch.
- **Responses API (stateful conversations via previous_response_id) + compaction + emulated file_search** — This is the canonical DONT_ADD: a multi-tenant-proxy core concern (stateful wire-protocol conversation store + provider-capability emulation) with no place in a local GUI that doesn't proxy or call APIs. The only genuinely real underlying need — resume a prior conversation — is already owned by Claude Code's native --resume against its transcripts, and the only adjacent Shepherd feature (compact + context bar) already exists and does NOT want server-side encrypted continuity items; it just drives the editor's own /compact. Emulated file_search is flatly out of scope. None of it serves the saved-agents/skills/MCP direction, so there's nothing to ADAPT toward.
- **Realtime / WebSocket multi-modal sessions (voice) + ephemeral client secrets** — This is the textbook multi-tenant-proxy feature the scope rules carve out: it is simultaneously a network server, a secrets issuer, and an API proxy — three explicit "NO"s — with no underlying local single-user Claude-Code-session need to adapt toward. There's no salvageable kernel: even an ADAPT would have to invent a Claude Code realtime/voice surface that does not exist, and the secret-minting half is forbidden outright. The lone steelman (it's "provider-adjacent") collapses because Shepherd's provider feature is launch-time env injection into a spawned shell, the polar opposite of a live audio relay holding minted credentials. Clean DONT_ADD on scope mismatch.
- **Code-interpreter Containers lifecycle + container files** — DONT_ADD wins on scope mismatch, the clearest kind. The feature IS network/HTTP-proxy plumbing: an endpoint factory registering routes (endpoints.json) that forward to OpenAI's hosted Containers API. That collides with all of Shepherd's hard limits — no network server, no API calls, no proxying, no secrets, single-user. The genuine local need it gestures at (provision a worker, seed it with files, hand it a task, tear it down) is already met by Shepherd's existing spawn/preset/queue/drain/respawn primitives operating on local Claude Code sessions, none of which require or benefit from a remote-sandbox CRUD API or a route registry. Even the favorably-weighted user interest (saved agents/skills/MCP) is about reusable local session profiles — orthogonal to provisioning OpenAI-hosted compute. There is nothing to ADAPT here that isn't either already present (spawn-from-preset) or out of scope (remote provisioning + HTTP routes).
- **Assistants/Threads API (stateful assistant runs)** — This is the canonical scope mismatch. The Assistants/Threads API is provider-proxy plumbing whose whole job is to stand up an OpenAI-compatible server that creates remote assistants/threads and makes streaming completion calls — the multi-tenant HTTP-proxy posture the fit question says is an automatic DONT_ADD. Shepherd's "agent" is a running GUI Claude Code session driven by keystrokes and a decision-file gate, not an HTTP route or a remote run object; it deliberately makes no API calls and stores no keys. The user's real interest (saved agents handed work off + skills/MCP registries) is legitimate, but the correct vehicle is the EXISTING spawn-preset / New-session-modal path (a saved "agent" = a reusable spawn spec: cwd, model, effort, permission-mode, queue) plus the queue/routing dispatcher for handing work off — none of which needs the Assistants API. Borrowing the literal API would force network proxying and stateful remote storage with zero gain for controlling local sessions. So: DONT_ADD on scope-mismatch grounds; the underlying saved-agent need is captured by an already-shipped primitive, not this feature.
- **Native provider-format interfaces (Anthropic /v1/messages, Google GenAI generateContent)** — Pure scope mismatch and pure API-proxy plumbing — the two clearest DONT_ADD triggers. The candidate is format-native request/response translation behind an HTTP interface, which is exactly the proxy core Shepherd was explicitly architected NOT to be (no network server, does not proxy or make API calls). Shepherd's only contact with "providers" is non-secret env injection at spawn time so that Claude Code — the real API client — talks to whatever endpoint, including a "LiteLLM-style gateway" that lives entirely outside Shepherd and would be where these adapters belong. The faint steelman (provider-aware error mapping to sharpen the transcript-derived Error state / Auto-Continue) does not rescue it: that would be a tiny, separate enhancement to core.transcriptError pattern-matching, not the /v1/messages or generateContent interface itself, and Shepherd reads error state from the local transcript tail, never from a parsed HTTP response it issued. There is no caller in a local single-user macOS GUI for a provider-format server interface. DONT_ADD.
- **Provider passthrough proxy (raw provider-native API access with credential resolution)** — Three independent hard-scope violations stack here: it requires Shepherd to (1) make outbound API calls, (2) run a request-forwarding path (proxy core), and (3) handle/resolve actual credentials rather than just env-var names. Any one of those is disqualifying per context.md's charter; together they make this the purest possible scope mismatch. The lone overlap — provider profiles with api_base + an env-var auth name — is exactly the boundary Shepherd deliberately stops at: it hands those env vars to the spawned shell and lets Claude Code own the wire. Adapting "let me hit a raw provider endpoint" into a local single-user GUI for controlling Claude Code GUI sessions yields nothing a user of that console would want, and it doesn't advance the saved-agents/skills/MCP interests at all. Verdict: DONT_ADD, high confidence.
